# Chef's Secret Flavor Pairings (Infographic)

**Creator:** Vladimir Taran (Instagram: @byvladimirtaran, bio tag "film edit repeat / stir sip repeat")
**Source file:** `CHEFS_SECRET_FLAVOR_PAIRINGS_BW_infographic (1).pdf` (4 pages)
**Format:** "Extended field sheet" companion to an Instagram Reel — a black-and-white hub-and-spoke diagram set, one hexagon-spoke wheel per hero ingredient, each spoke a suggested pairing, with numbered footnotes giving a one-line technique/cocktail application for select pairings.

## Content

Cover page (p.1) indexes three ingredient wheels with page refs and a reading key (hexagon = a pairing around the hub; dashed hexagon = "wild card"; superscript number = see notes below the wheel).

### 1. Peach (p.2) — "the sweet component, vanilla to smoky spirits"
10 pairings around the hub: Vanilla, Cinnamon, Honey, Lemon, Ginger, Raspberry, Prosciutto/Bacon, Mint, Lapsang Souchong, Smoky Spirits.
Technique notes: fat-wash the cocktail with vanilla-extract yogurt; grilled peaches + cinnamon + a dark spirit; use honey as the sweetener instead of sugar; a Buck/Mule with ginger beer + mint, or a fruity Penicillin twist with smoky Scotch; foamy builds pairing peach (sweet) + gin + neutral acid + herbal modifier + raspberry foam, or a shaken sour in Clover Club style; fat-wash the spirit then pair with prosciutto/bacon; Lapsang Souchong keeps the smoky profile in zero-proof drinks when smoky spirits are swapped out.

### 2. Tomato (p.3) — "mostly for Bloody Mary / Red Snapper twists"
13 pairings + one wild card: Plums, Balsamic Vinegar, Worcestershire Sauce, Mustard Seeds, Dill/Parsley, Cilantro, Mushrooms, Oregano, Chili/Jalapeño, Red Onion, Parmesan, Oysters, Olives/Olive Brine, and wild card Passion Fruit.
Technique notes: add a bit of PX sherry to a Bloody Mary; balsamic vinegar note extends to "basically most vinegar-based sauces"; mushrooms suggest a "mushroom-tomato ketchup" flavor profile; oysters work mostly as a side snack/pairing. Overall guidance ("nota bene"): use these flavors to build a custom seasonal Bloody Mary premix.

### 3. Basil (p.4) — "pairs beautifully with fats — and umami"
12 pairings: Peanut Butter, Avocado, Shiitake Mushrooms, Yellow Bell Pepper, Artichoke, Grapefruit, Watermelon, Cucumber, Green Apple/Malic Acid, Passion Fruit, Lemongrass, Pear.
Technique notes: fat-wash gin or vodka with peanut butter and build the basil cocktail around it; avocado's fat gives basil great texture; yellow bell pepper ≈ sweet pepper or Bulgarian paprika; add a splash of Cynar to a Basil Smash or a "Basil Suzette"; grapefruit works mostly as an oleo-saccharum (same logic as gentian root in Suze); cucumber follows the "same-color things work together" rule of thumb (like watermelon); green apple/malic acid brings a tart, unripe edge — sour apples work best; passion fruit pairs perfectly with umami, so basil acts as seasoning rather than the lead flavor; pear + Gorgonzola + basil flagged as a standout combo.
Teaser for a follow-up post: "neon-green herb syrups that last twice as long" + 5 summer recipes including a Basil Suzette.

## Note
This is a lightweight, single-creator social-media reference card (not a professionally edited book) — concise pairing prompts and bartending hacks rather than sourced/scientific pairing data (contrast with the Foodpairing books in this collection, which ground pairings in shared aroma-molecule analysis).

# Cocktail Codex: Fundamentals, Formulas, Evolutions

**Authors:** Alex Day, Nick Fauchald, and David Kaplan (founders of Death & Co / the 86 Co., with the Proprietors LLC bar group — Existing Conditions, the Normandie Club, the Walker Inn)
**Publisher:** Ten Speed Press / Penguin Random House, isbn13 9781607749707, 326 pages

## Core thesis and structural overview

The book's central argument: nearly every classic and modern cocktail is a variation on one of **six "root" cocktails**, each representing a distinct structural template. Rather than memorizing hundreds of recipes, a bartender who understands the six roots — their defining ratios, their "core" (primary flavor-driving ingredient), their "balance" (what tempers the core), and their "seasoning" (what accents it) — can build or deconstruct almost any cocktail. Each of the six chapters follows an identical anatomy:

1. **The Classic Recipe** — the traditional textbook version
2. **Our Root Recipe** — the authors' own tuned "ideal" version, with a stated rationale
3. **Orthodoxy** — a boxed statement of the template's defining traits
4. **Understanding the Template** — theory
5. **The Core** — deep dive on the primary spirit/ingredient category
6. **Experimenting with the Core** — riffs that swap the base
7. **The Balance** — deep dive on the tempering ingredient (sugar, vermouth, citrus, liqueur, seltzer, egg/dairy)
8. **Experimenting with the Balance** — riffs
9. **The Seasoning** — deep dive on the accent ingredient (bitters, garnish, acids, amaro)
10. **Experimenting with the Seasoning** — riffs
11. **[Chapter's] Extended Family** — a mind-map-style family tree connecting the root to dozens of named cocktails
12. **Exploring Technique** — a focused technical skill tied to that chapter (dilution, garnish, clarification, building, rimming, eggs/smoke)
13. **Glassware** — the chapter's signature glass

An **Appendix** (roughly the book's final 50 pages) collects the modern/original cocktail recipes referenced throughout (alphabetical, by contributing bartender and year) plus a technical reference section: syrups and cordials, infusions (including sous-vide/immersion-circulator and iSi-whipper/N2O rapid-infusion methods), house mixes and sodas, salts/rims, and acid/tincture solutions (with a full guide to clarifying juice via agar, centrifuge, or Pectinex/kieselsol-chitosan fining).

---

## The Six Root Cocktails

### 1. The Old-Fashioned — spirit, sugar, bitters

**Classic recipe:** 1 sugar cube, 2 dashes Angostura bitters, 2 oz bourbon, muddled/stirred with ice, lemon and orange twist.

**Root recipe (their ideal):**
> 2 oz Elijah Craig Small Batch bourbon, 1 teaspoon Demerara Gum Syrup, 2 dashes Angostura bitters, 1 dash Bitter Truth aromatic bitters, orange twist + lemon twist.

**Orthodoxy:** (1) spirit-driven; (2) balanced by a small amount of sweetness; (3) seasoned with bitters and a garnish.

- **The Core — American whiskey:** bourbon (≥51% corn; corn=sweetness, rye=spice, wheat=softness) vs. rye (≥51% rye, "flips" bourbon's mashbill, spicier). Recommended bourbons: Eagle Rare 10-Year, Elijah Craig Small Batch (their benchmark), Old Grand-Dad 114 (57% ABV, "sip slowly"), Old Weller Antique 107, Wyoming Whiskey. Recommended ryes: Rittenhouse (bonded, 100 proof), Russell's Reserve 6-Year. Also covers Tennessee sour-mash and wheat whiskey.
- **The Balance — sugar:** they reject muddling a sugar cube (dissolves poorly) and reject plain simple syrup (too thin) in favor of **Demerara Gum Syrup** — 2 parts demerara sugar to 1 part water, thickened with gum arabic — because it adds body/viscosity without diluting the spirit's character. Aged spirits (vanillin/oak-derived sweetness) may need less added sugar.
- **The Seasoning — bitters:** distinguishes "flavoring bitters" (add a distinct new flavor, e.g. Angostura aromatic) from "amplifying bitters" (heighten flavors already present, e.g. orange bitters). Categorizes bitters as aromatic, citrus, or savory (e.g. Bittermens Xocolatl mole bitters).
- **Technique — "underdiluted stirring":** because an Old-Fashioned continues diluting in the glass after serving, they deliberately under-dilute versus a Martini's full dilution — building it in a mixing glass with 1-inch ice cubes, stirring only to the point the spirit is still "sharp and slightly abrasive," then straining over one large cube to slow further dilution (~3 min traditional method vs. their faster technique).
- **Glassware:** the eponymous rocks glass, 12–14 oz capacity, thick weighted base, tapering slightly toward the base.
- **Extended Family (family tree):** Old-Fashioned variations split into "Experimenting with the Balance" (Fancy-Free, Monte Carlo, Chrysanthemum, Stinger — using liqueur instead of sugar), "Experimenting with the Seasoning" (Improved Whiskey Cocktail, Pop Quiz, Night Owl, Normandie Club Old-Fashioned), "Experimenting with the Core" (Vermouth Cocktail, Ti' Punch, Exit Strategy, Golden Boy), and further out, the whole extended family: **Champagne Cocktail, Mint Julep, Sherry Cobbler, Hot Toddy, and Sazerac** — each treated as an Old-Fashioned in disguise (Champagne Cocktail = spirit swapped for Champagne, no stirring needed since bubbles do the dilution).

### 2. The Martini — spirit and aromatized wine

**Root recipe (gin):** ~2 oz Plymouth gin, 1 oz Dolin dry vermouth, a dash of House Orange Bitters, lemon twist.
**Root recipe (vodka):** a drier split (roughly 2.5 oz Absolut Elyx vodka to 0.5 oz Dolin dry vermouth), olive garnish.

**Orthodoxy (paraphrased from chapter):** the Martini's core is defined by *two* ingredients in relationship — a base spirit and an aromatized/fortified wine — rather than one dominant spirit; vermouth is not a mere modifier but a co-equal partner.

- **The Core — gin:** distinguishes **London dry gin** (juniper-forward, all flavor from distillation, no post-distillation additives) from **contemporary/New Western gins** (juniper present but not dominant, showcase other botanicals — citrus, florals). Recommends Beefeater and Plymouth as versatile London-style; discusses genever and Old Tom as historical antecedents.
- **The Balance — vermouth:** treated with the same rigor as spirits — dry, sweet, and blanc vermouths each explored, plus fino/manzanilla sherry as a vermouth substitute in low-ABV Martini riffs.
- **Extended family and variations:** the Martini chapter's family includes the **Manhattan** (whiskey + sweet vermouth + bitters — essentially a Martini with the polarity of sweet/dry flipped and whiskey instead of gin), the **Vesper** (gin + vodka + Lillet Blanc), the **Negroni** (gin, sweet vermouth, Campari — equal-parts riff on the Martini template with an added bitter liqueur), the **Martinez** (an Old Tom gin/sweet vermouth/maraschino/bitters proto-Manhattan-Martini hybrid), and the **Bijou** (gin, green Chartreuse, sweet vermouth, orange bitters — reliant on a large dose of green Chartreuse balanced by Campari-like bitterness elsewhere in its lineage). Bottled aromatized wines (Lillet, Cocchi Americano, Dubonnet) are treated as legitimate cores/modifiers in their own right.
- Glassware discussion favors the coupe and the Nick & Nora for stirred, spirit-forward drinks.

### 3. The Daiquiri — spirit, citrus, sweetener (a "sour")

**Classic recipe:** 2 oz rum, ¾ oz fresh lime juice, ¾ oz simple syrup, shaken, strained up, lime wedge garnish.

**The universal "Basic Sour" formula** (stated explicitly as the template underlying not just Daiquiris but the whole sour category):
> **2 oz spirit : ¾ oz fresh lemon or lime juice : ¾ oz simple syrup** (shake with ice, strain, no garnish)

**Root recipe (their ideal Daiquiri):** 1¾ oz Caña Brava white rum, ¼ oz La Favorite Rhum Agricole Blanc (Coeur de Canne), 1 oz fresh lime juice, ¼ oz simple syrup, lime wedge.

**Orthodoxy:** (1) composed of a spirit, citrus, and a sweetener — typically rum, lime juice, and simple syrup; (2) flexible on the citrus:sweetener ratio depending on the drinker's preference and the citrus's actual acidity/sweetness; (3) requires improvisation because citrus juice is inherently inconsistent fruit-to-fruit.

- **The Core — rum:** categorized by production tradition — Spanish-style (ron, column-distilled, light, Cuba/Puerto Rico/Panama — recommends Caña Brava), English-style (pot-still, funkier, Jamaica), French-style (rhum agricole, made from fresh cane juice not molasses, grassy/vegetal), Jamaican rum. Explains why light/gold "filtered" rums can still be aged rums with color removed.
- **Distinguishes sours from daisies:** a **sour** (Daiquiri, Whiskey Sour) uses only spirit + citrus + sugar; a **daisy** (Margarita, Cosmopolitan) substitutes a sweetened liqueur (orange curaçao/Cointreau) for some of the sugar, making the liqueur part of the core rather than mere seasoning — this distinction is formalized fully in the Sidecar chapter.
- **Extended family (family tree, page 143):** the Daiquiri's family branches into Collins-style drinks (**Tom Collins**: 2 oz gin, ¾ oz lemon, ¾ oz simple syrup, topped with seltzer — a "sour with bubbles added," short-shaken 5 seconds to preserve carbonation), **Grapefruit Collins, Parachute, Moscow Mule, Dark and Stormy, Presbyterian** (all "spirit + acid + sweetener + seltzer" riffs), fizzes (**Silver Fizz** — Collins + egg white; **Ramos Gin Fizz** — Silver Fizz + heavy cream, 5–10 min shake), and Champagne/sparkling-wine sours (**Old Cuban** — Audrey Saunders' Mojito-with-Champagne; **French 75** — gin Collins topped with Champagne instead of seltzer, with the sour's proportions cut to accommodate the wine's own sugar/acid/alcohol).
- **Technique — juice clarification:** detailed methods for clarifying citrus/other juices with agar (gel-and-strain method), a centrifuge with Pectinex/kieselsol/chitosan fining agents, or simple fine-mesh straining — used to build clean-looking "clarified" cocktails like the Clarified Cosmopolitan.

### 4. The Sidecar — spirit, citrus, and a substantial liqueur (a "daisy")

**Classic recipe:** 1½ oz Cognac, 1 oz Cointreau, ¾ oz fresh lemon juice, orange twist.

**Root recipe (their ideal):** 1½ oz Pierre Ferrand Ambre Cognac, 1 oz Pierre Ferrand dry curaçao, ¾ oz fresh lemon juice, orange twist. (Traced to a likely predecessor, the 1862 Brandy Crusta.)

**Orthodoxy:** (1) core flavor = a spirit plus a substantial amount of flavorful liqueur (generally ½–1 oz); (2) balanced *and* seasoned by that liqueur (which contributes sweetness, sometimes alongside another sweetener); (3) also balanced by highly acidic citrus (lemon or lime).

- **Key distinction from the Daiquiri:** liqueurs vary enormously in proof and sweetness by producer (dry Pierre Ferrand curaçao vs. sweet Grand Marnier vs. middling Cointreau), so there is no single fixed template ratio — each liqueur must be tasted and the proportions rebalanced around it.
- **Liqueur reference (extensive):** orange curaçao, stone-fruit liqueurs (apricot, peach — Massenez Crème de Pêche), pear liqueurs (Clear Creek, Mathilde), maraschino liqueur (Luxardo vs. sweeter Maraska), floral/herbal liqueurs (elderflower/St-Germain — "bartender's ketchup," violet liqueur/crème de violette, crème de menthe, Bénédictine, Chartreuse yellow vs. green, Galliano, Strega), and rich liqueurs (crème de cacao light vs. dark, vanilla liqueur, coffee liqueur).
- **Variations by liqueur swap:** **20th Century** (gin, Lillet blanc, white crème de cacao, lemon — "chocolaty but not dessert"), **Last Word** (equal parts gin, green Chartreuse, maraschino, lime — a Sidecar template pushed to high-proof extremes), **Crop Top** (amaro-based lighter riff on the Last Word), **Why Not** (a Whiskey Sour/Sidecar hybrid using maple syrup for half the Cointreau), **Cosmopolitan** and its **Clarified Cosmopolitan** variant (explicitly identified as "a Sidecar that swaps citrus vodka for Cognac and lime juice for lemon, with cranberry added" — and a caution against overusing sweetened cranberry cocktail mix), **Lily Pad** and **Pegu Cub Cocktail**.
- **Technique — acids:** an extensive reference on alternative/powdered acids used to manipulate or replace citrus — **citric acid** (the primary acid in lemon/lime, made into a 100g water:25g powder solution), **malic acid** (green-apple tartness, weaker so higher concentration), **tartaric acid** (found in grapes/wine, drying), **lactic acid** (creamy texture without dairy), **ascorbic acid/vitamin C** (antioxidant, prevents juice browning), and **phosphoric acid** (Coca-Cola's tartness, tongue-tingling without flavor). Also a full section on glass-rimming technique (skinny vs. fat rims, wetting only the outside, salt/sugar/spice rim recipes).
- **Glassware:** the coupe, upsized to ~7 oz (Retro Coupe Glass by Urban Bar) specifically to leave room for a shaken drink's foamy head without overflow.

### 5. The Whisky Highball — spirit and a nonalcoholic (often carbonated) mixer

**Classic recipe:** 2 oz scotch, 6 oz cold seltzer, lemon wedge.

**Root recipe (their ideal):** 2 oz Hakushu 12-Year Japanese whisky, 4 oz cold seltzer, no garnish (garnish is deliberately omitted to let the whisky/water pairing stand unmasked).

**Orthodoxy:** (1) core spirit that also provides seasoning; (2) core can be split between any number of spirits/wines/fortified wines; (3) can be effervescent or still.

- **The Core & Seasoning — whisky:** full survey of **Scotch** (single-malt vs. blended; regions — Highlands/Speyside, Lowlands, Islay; recommends Auchentoshan Three Wood, Bowmore 12, Glenlivet 12, Highland Park 12, Laphroaig 10, and blends The Famous Grouse / Compass Box Asyla/Oak Cross), **Irish whiskey** (triple-distilled, larger stills, blends like Bushmills vs. pure pot-still like Redbreast 12), and **Japanese whisky** (Hakushu, Nikka Coffey Grain, Suntory Toki) — with a strategy for mixing with expensive single-malts (use a cheap blend as base, dose in a splash of the fine single-malt as an accent, rather than mixing the good stuff wholesale).
- **The Balance — sparkling water:** distinguishes **sparkling mineral water** (variable mineral content/effervescence), **soda water/club soda** (minerals added deliberately, subtle salinity that brightens citrus and curbs bitterness), and **seltzer** (neutral, additive-free, their default for control). Two-parts-mixer-to-one-part-whisky is their guideline; more assertive whiskies may need more seltzer.
- **Technique — "Building Whisky Highballs":** a precise Japanese-bar-inspired build: chill the glass, add one large (1-inch+) ice cube directly from the freezer, let temper ~10 seconds before stirring (to avoid cracking), stir until condensation forms (~10 sec), add ice to two-thirds full, pour chilled seltzer to just below flotation point, insert a barspoon to the bottom and gently lift/lower the lowest ice cube to mix without killing bubbles, add a final ice cube and one gentle full stir. A sidebar profiles Tyson Buhler (Death & Co bar director) describing Japan's *mizuwari* ritual (stir exactly 13.5 times clockwise after ice, then 3.5 times after water).
- **Extended Family (family tree):** two main experimentation branches — "Experimenting with the Core" (**Americano** — Campari + sweet vermouth + seltzer, essentially a Negroni-as-Highball) and "Experimenting with the Balance" (**Paloma, Cuba Libre, Screwdriver, Negroni Sbagliato** — swapping seltzer for tonic, cola, orange juice, or prosecco) — plus a further-out extended family: **Bellini, Mimosa, Kir Royale, Aperol Spritz** family, **Gin & Tonic, Tequila Sunrise, Harvey Wallbanger, Nobody's Robots, Apple Pop**, and savory Highballs like the **Bloody Mary** (and Bloody Mary Verde). Also covers the classic **Cuba Libre** (rum + lime + cola — lime peel oils drawn out to complement the cola's own citrus).
- **Glassware:** the Highball glass, ~12 oz, thick weighted base, thin etched rim (they note it is NOT interchangeable with the larger 14 oz Collins glass, which needs the extra volume for a fully-diluted shaken drink).

### 6. The Flip — spirit/fortified wine, sugar, and whole egg

**Classic recipe:** 2 oz spirit or fortified wine, 2 teaspoons demerara sugar, 1 whole egg, dry-shaken then shaken with ice, double-strained, nutmeg garnish. (Traced to 17th-century England — beer/rum/sugar heated with a red-hot iron "poker" that made it "flip"/froth; egg and cold service came later.)

**Root recipe (their ideal):** 2 oz González Byass Matusalem oloroso sherry, ½ oz Demerara Gum Syrup, 1 whole egg, nutmeg — into a small white wine glass.

**Orthodoxy:** (1) characteristic flavor from a core spirit or fortified wine plus a rich ingredient; (2) balanced by rich ingredients — eggs, dairy, coconut milk, or dense liqueurs/syrups; (3) seasoned with aromatic spices dusted on top (or a flavorful amaro).

- **The Core — fortified wine (sherry/port/madeira):** an extensive reference — **sherry** styles from bone-dry **fino/manzanilla** (flor-aged, briny) through **amontillado** (biological + oxidative aging, nutty) and **palo cortado** (an amontillado that "escaped" its flor early, smells like oloroso/tastes like amontillado) to rich **oloroso** (fully oxidative, fig/raisin, likened to sweet vermouth without the botanicals) and sweet **Pedro Ximénez/Moscatel** ("nature's finest simple syrup"); explains the **solera** fractional-blending aging system. **Port** styles: ruby, vintage, late-bottled vintage, tawny, colheita. **Madeira**: rainwater (light) through richer aged styles.
- **The Balance — eggs and dairy:** whole egg gives both fatty body (yolk) and foam (white); egg yolk alone (rare) adds richer flavor via lecithin's emulsifying power (used in the **New York Flip** — bourbon, tawny port, simple syrup, heavy cream, egg yolk — a decadent version of the lemon-based New York Sour). Dairy section strongly prefers **heavy cream** (35–40% fat, less prone to curdling with acid, carries flavor) over half-and-half or whole milk, plus nut-milk alternatives (their own **almond milk**: 600 g sliced blanched almonds blended with 7½ cups water, strained). Both eggs and dairy need a slightly longer shake and double-straining for smooth texture.
- **The Seasoning — amaro:** classified by sweetness/bitterness into **aperitivi** (lightest, e.g. Aperol at 11% ABV), light, medium, and dense amari — with no fixed rules, since amaro can play anywhere from background seasoning to full core spirit.
- **Rich-drink family:** the Flip's "strike it rich" extended family explicitly includes the **White Russian, Grasshopper, Brandy Alexander** (crème de cacao + cream, no egg — "Flip without the egg"), **Coffee Cocktail** (tawny port + Cognac + egg, named for its coffee-cup *appearance*, not flavor), **Eggnog** (single-serving fast version: rum, Cognac, vanilla liqueur, cane sugar syrup, heavy cream, whole egg), and **Tom and Jerry** (a full egg/sugar/spice batter recipe given in the appendix).
- **Technique — next-level smoking:** how to infuse cocktails/ingredients with real smoke using a smoking gun (e.g. PolyScience Smoking Gun Pro), wood staves/planks, torches, or grilling — with a wood-selection guide from mild (cherry, apple, birch) through medium (oak, hickory — their top all-purpose picks) to strong (mesquite, walnut), plus smoking with flammable ingredients (torched rosemary, smoked tea like Lapsang souchong, and smoke-infused ice).

---

## Appendix highlights (technical reference)

- **Signature original cocktails** from the authors' bars (Existing Conditions, Normandie Club, Death & Co, the Walker Inn) with full specs, e.g. **Penicillin** (Sam Ross, 2005): 2 oz blended Scotch, ¾ oz lemon, ⅓ oz honey syrup, ⅓ oz House Ginger Syrup, topped with a mist of Islay scotch and candied ginger.
- **Syrups/cordials:** Basil Stem Syrup, Grilled Pineapple Syrup, House Lemon Cordial, House Orgeat (almond milk + sugar + Cognac + amaretto + rose water), Meyer Lemon Cordial (sous-vide, immersion circulator at 130°F), Raisin Honey Syrup, Strawberry Cream Syrup, Vanilla Lactic Syrup, White Honey Syrup.
- **Infusions:** dozens of spirit infusions using either an immersion circulator (sous-vide, typically 140–145°F for 2 hours) or an iSi whipper with N2O cartridges for rapid infusion (charge, shake, vent, repeat) — e.g. Hickory Smoke-Infused Cognac, Cacao Nib-Infused Tequila/Ramazzotti/Pear Brandy, Chamomile-Infused Blanco Tequila/Calvados/Dolin Blanc/Cocchi Americano/Rye, Coconut-Infused Bourbon, Jalapeño-Infused Vodka, Madras Curry-Infused Gin, Oolong-Infused Vodka, Raisin-Infused Rye/Scotch, Sesame-Infused Rum, Milk-Washed Rum (a clarification technique using citric acid to curdle milk, then filter).
- **House mixes/sodas:** Apple Celery Soda, Basic Bloody Mary Mix, Donn's Mix No. 1 (tiki syrup: grapefruit juice + Cinnamon Syrup), Homemade Grapefruit Soda, House Coconut Cream, House Orange Bitters (blend of three commercial bitters), Milk & Honey House Curaçao, Normandie Club Bloody Mary Mix and House Sweet Vermouth, Tom and Jerry Batter, Verde Mix.
- **Salts, rims, solutions, tinctures:** Lemon Pepper Salt, Sugared Smoked Salt, Après-Ski Tincture (vodka + cedar incense scrapings + cinnamon), Cacao Nib Tincture, and the full citric/malic/tartaric/lactic/ascorbic/phosphoric acid solution recipes referenced throughout the book.

---

## Notes on extraction

Approached via the Read tool's `pages` parameter in 20-page chunks as instructed. Most chunks rendered correctly, but a request-limit issue caused entire early chunks (roughly PDF pages 1–76, i.e. the full Old-Fashioned chapter and the start of the Martini chapter) to come back as "[media removed: request limit]" — the same silent-drop failure mode flagged for the Match Bar Group manual, just occurring mid-session here rather than consistently. This was caught by noticing the gap in root-recipe coverage, and the affected pages were successfully re-read once the limit cleared, so **pdftotext fallback was not needed** — but the retry was necessary to get complete coverage of the Old-Fashioned chapter and confirm the Daiquiri/Sidecar root recipes. The two family-tree infographics for the Daiquiri and Whisky Highball chapters, and the Old-Fashioned's extended-family tree, were read directly; the Martini, Sidecar, and Flip chapters' extended families are summarized from their in-text variation write-ups rather than from an equivalent graphic (which may exist on pages not sampled in this pass, given the book's ~326 pages and the density of the appendix).

<!-- ===== INÍCIO: death-and-co.md ===== -->

# Death & Co

**Author:** David Kaplan, with Alex Day and Nick Fauchald; foreword by Toby Cecchini; photography by William Hereford
**Publisher:** Ten Speed Press, 2014
**Pages:** 573

## Structural Overview

The book documents the cocktail program of Death & Co, the influential East Village (NYC) speakeasy-style bar that opened in 2007 and became a driving force of the modern craft-cocktail revival. It is organized in five parts:

1. **Introduction** — the bar's history and philosophy (Dave Kaplan and Alex Day founding story, the role of head bartenders Phil Ward, Brian Miller, Thomas Waugh, Jillian Vose).
2. **Part One: Ingredients** — deep-dive chapters on spirits (gin, vodka, tequila/mezcal, whiskey, rum, brandy, aquavit), modifiers (vermouths, amari, liqueurs), bitters, sweeteners, juices, dairy/eggs, garnishes, and ice.
3. **Part Two: Tools** — bar equipment (shakers, strainers, jiggers, bar spoons, muddlers, mixing glasses, julep tins, swizzle sticks) and glassware.
4. **Part Three: Technique** — jiggering/measuring, shaking, stirring, straining, muddling, rimming, rinsing glasses, garnishing, building rounds, and ice science.
5. **Part Four: Creating New Cocktails** — the house creative methodology, including the "Mr. Potato Head" method, balance theory, and the four core strategies for building original drinks.
6. **Part Five: The Recipes** — roughly 500 pages of recipes, organized by base spirit and technique (e.g., "Gin, Shaken," "Gin, Stirred," "Rum, Shaken," "Rum, Stirred," "Agave, Shaken," "Agave, Stirred," "Whiskey," "Brandy," "Sparkling Cocktails," "Fortified Wine Cocktails," "Punch," "Juleps," "Flips and Fizzes," "Swizzles," "Multiple Choice," "Aquavit"), followed by thematic **Variation** chapters devoted to five classic templates: **Sazerac Variations, Negroni Variations, Daiquiri Variations, Manhattan Variations, Old-Fashioned Variations** (including a "Death & Co Family Daiquiri Album" of personal specs from alumni).
7. **Appendices** — Appendix One: Sweeteners, Infusions & Other Concoctions (syrups, infusions, batches, rims, miscellany); Appendix Two: The Death & Co Bookshelf (recommended reading); Appendix Three: Resources (suppliers/vendors).

Each recipe entry gives the drink's name, creator and year, sometimes a short anecdote, then an exact ingredient list (in ounces/teaspoons) followed by a one- to two-sentence method (shake/stir, glass, garnish).

## Philosophy & Technique Synthesis

- **Balance is the organizing principle.** A cocktail is treated as an equation of spirit, sweet, sour/acid, and dilution (via ice/water) that must reach equilibrium; bitters and modifiers fine-tune rather than dominate.
- **The "Mr. Potato Head" method:** build a reliable ratio/template (e.g., 2:¾:½ spirit:modifier:something-interesting, as in "Coin Toss") and swap the base spirit while keeping the supporting structure — proof that a good formula transcends any one spirit. The "Multiple Choice" chapter exists specifically to demonstrate this (one recipe printed with 4–6 alternate base spirits).
- **Four strategies for building new cocktails** (introduced in "Creating New Classics"): (1) riff on a classic (add/replace one ingredient in a known template — Manhattan, Old-Fashioned, Daiquiri, Negroni, Sazerac all get their own "Variations" chapters); (2) build from a flavor combination or inspiration (a food pairing, a place, a memory); (3) use a signature in-house ingredient (infusion, syrup, or "batch") as the anchor; (4) reverse-engineer from name/concept.
- **Stirred vs. shaken** is a deliberate technical choice: stirred drinks (all-spirit/fortified-wine combinations) are chilled and diluted gently to preserve texture and clarity; shaken drinks (containing citrus, dairy, egg, or puree) need the aeration and rapid dilution of hard shaking, and eggs/dairy get a "dry shake" (no ice) first to build foam before shaking with ice.
- **Punch requires five elements** by house rule: spirit, sugar, citrus, water, and spice — spice (via tea infusions, bitters, or fresh spice) is what technically makes it a "punch" rather than a large cocktail; the classic method is muddling sugar cubes with citrus and club soda, then stirring in the rest over ice in a pitcher before straining over a large block in a punch bowl.
- **Juleps and swizzles** get their own controlled builds: julep tins are packed with crushed ice in stages (half full → stir/churn 10 sec → two-thirds → stir again → cone above rim) until the tin frosts, with mint reserved as a bouquet garnish rather than muddled in; swizzles are built the same way in a pilsner glass, ideally swizzled with a bois lélé stick, with bitters often "swizzled on top" as a final aromatic layer rather than mixed in.
- **In-house infrastructure**: extensive use of infused spirits/vermouths (tea, chile, fruit, herb infusions), proprietary syrups (Cinnamon Bark, Demerara, Ginger, Acacia Honey, Vanilla, Cane Sugar), house bitters blends (House Orange Bitters, House Peychaud's Bitters, House Sweet Vermouth), and "batches" (premixed multi-ingredient bottles like Donn's Mix #1, Bergerac Mix, Pendennis Mix, Zombie Mix) kept behind the bar in "cheater bottles" for speed and precision.
- **Orgeat, horchata, and other house dairy/nut bases** are made from scratch (toasted almond milk-based orgeat with cognac, amaretto, and rose water).
- The daiquiri is treated as the bar's "secret handshake" — the truest test of a bartender's skill (balance of sweet/sour/booze, properly cold, not overdiluted); this belief motivates the extensive "Daiquiri Variations" chapter and the personal-spec "Family Album."

## Sample Recipes (organized by category)

### Classic-template variations

**Naked and Famous** (Joaquín Simó, 2011) — a mezcal Last Word/Paper Plane hybrid
- ¾ oz Del Maguey Chichicapa mezcal
- ¾ oz yellow Chartreuse
- ¾ oz Aperol
- ¾ oz lime juice
- Shake with ice, strain into a coupe. No garnish.

**White Negroni** (Alex Day, 2009)
- 1½ oz Ford's gin
- ¾ oz Dolin blanc vermouth
- ¾ oz Suze Saveur d'Autrefois liqueur
- Stir over ice, strain into a double rocks glass over 1 large ice cube. Garnish with lemon twist.

**Oaxaca Old-Fashioned** (Phil Ward, 2008) — one of the most influential modern classics
- 1½ oz El Tesoro reposado tequila
- ½ oz Del Maguey San Luis del Rio mezcal
- 1 teaspoon agave nectar
- 2 dashes Angostura bitters
- Stir over ice, strain into a coupe. Flame an orange twist over the drink, then drop it in.

**Angus Story Daiquiri** (Phil Ward, 2012) — three-rum daiquiri
- ¾ oz Appleton Estate V/X rum
- ¾ oz Ron del Barrilito 3-star rum
- ½ oz La Favorite rhum agricole blanc
- ¾ oz lime juice
- ½ oz Cane Sugar Syrup
- Shake with ice, strain into a coupe. Garnish with lime wedge.

**Mi Amaro** (Joaquín Simó, 2009) — Manhattan-template tequila drink
- 2 oz El Tesoro reposado tequila
- ½ oz Amaro CioCiaro
- ½ oz Carpano Antica Formula vermouth
- ¼ oz St-Germain
- Stir over ice, strain into a coupe. Garnish with grapefruit twist.

### Gin / Agave / Rum / Brandy / Whiskey — shaken and stirred

**Naked and Famous, Fresa Brava** (Phil Ward, 2009) — muddled tequila sour
- 1 strawberry
- 2 oz jalapeño-infused Siembra Azul blanco tequila
- ¾ oz yellow Chartreuse
- ¾ oz lemon juice
- ½ oz Simple Syrup
- Muddle strawberry, add rest, shake with ice, double strain into coupe. No garnish.

**Green and Red** (Thomas Waugh, 2012) — stirred cucumber cocktail
- 3 cucumber wheels (muddled)
- 1 oz Siete Leguas blanco tequila
- 1 oz jalapeño-infused Siembra Azul blanco tequila
- ¾ oz Cocchi Americano
- ¼ oz Dolin dry vermouth
- ¼ oz Alvear Festival pale cream sherry
- Muddle cucumber in mixing glass, add rest, stir over ice, strain into coupe. Garnish with cucumber ribbon.

**Widow's Laurel** (Joaquín Simó, 2009) — riff on the Widow's Kiss
- 2 oz Busnel VSOP Calvados
- ½ oz Drambuie
- ½ oz Carpano Antica Formula vermouth
- 1 teaspoon St. Elizabeth Allspice Dram
- 1 dash Angostura bitters
- Stir over ice, strain into coupe. Garnish with 3 brandied cherries.

**Jekyll and Hyde** (Thomas Waugh, 2009) — Old-Fashioned variation
- 1½ oz Eagle Rare 10-year bourbon
- ½ oz Laird's Bonded apple brandy
- ½ teaspoon Cinnamon Bark Syrup
- 1 teaspoon Demerara Syrup
- 2 dashes Bitter Truth Aromatic bitters + 2 dashes Angostura bitters
- Stir over ice, strain into double rocks glass over 1 large ice cube. Garnish with lemon and orange twists.

### Sparkling / Fortified Wine

**Bitter French** (Phil Ward, 2008) — French 75 riff
- 1 oz Plymouth gin
- ¼ oz Campari
- ½ oz lemon juice
- ½ oz Simple Syrup
- Dry champagne to top
- Shake all but champagne with ice, strain into flute, top with champagne. Squeeze grapefruit twist over and discard.

**Café Sandinista** (Joaquín Simó, 2012) — fortified-wine cocktail
- 1 oz Lustau East India Solera sherry
- ¼ oz coffee- and chile-infused Campari
- ½ oz orange juice
- ½ oz lime juice
- ½ oz Demerara Syrup
- Small pinch kosher salt
- Short shake with 3 ice cubes, strain into double rocks glass over crushed ice. Garnish with grated coffee bean.

### Punch

**Mother's Ruin Punch** (Phil Ward, 2008) — the bar's original punch, serves 4-6
- 8 white sugar cubes
- 2 oz club soda (for muddling)
- 4 oz Plymouth gin
- 2 oz Cinnamon Orange Tea–Infused Sweet Vermouth
- 4 oz grapefruit juice
- 2 oz lemon juice
- 3 oz dry champagne
- Garnish: 6 grapefruit wheels
- Muddle sugar cubes with club soda in pitcher until dissolved; add remaining ingredients (except champagne), fill three-quarters with ice, stir until cold, strain into punch bowl over a large ice block, top with champagne.

### Julep / Swizzle

**Maple Julep** (Phil Ward, 2008)
- 2 oz Old Overholt rye
- ¼ oz maple syrup
- Garnish: mint bouquet
- Build in julep tin, pack with crushed ice in stages, stirring until frosted; garnish with mint bouquet in center of ice cone.

**6th Street Swizzle** (Phil Ward, 2008)
- 1½ oz La Favorite rhum agricole blanc
- 1 oz lime juice
- ¾ oz Cane Sugar Syrup
- 2 dashes Angostura bitters
- Garnish: mint sprig, lime wheel
- Whip ingredients with crushed ice pellets, pour into pilsner glass filled with crushed ice, swizzle until cold.

### Flips / Fizzes

**Sleepy Hollow Fizz** (Brian Miller, 2008)
- 1½ oz Flor de Caña 7-year rum
- ½ oz El Dorado high-strength 151 rum
- ½ oz lemon juice
- ½ oz maple syrup
- 1 egg yolk
- 2 teaspoons pumpkin puree
- Club soda to top
- Dry shake, then shake again with ice; strain into fizz glass, top with club soda.

### Appendix staples (house syrups & infusions)

- **Cane Sugar Syrup:** 2 cups organic cane sugar to 1 cup water, cooked without boiling until dissolved.
- **Cinnamon Bark Syrup:** muddle 1 oz cassia cinnamon sticks, add 2 cups water + 2 cups superfine sugar, boil, simmer 4 min, steep overnight, strain.
- **Ginger Syrup:** ½ cup fresh ginger juice blended with 1 cup superfine sugar.
- **Grenadine (house):** 4 cups unfiltered pomegranate juice + 3 cups cane sugar, cooked, then stirred with 6 oz pomegranate molasses and orange-twist oils.
- **Orgeat:** toasted almond milk + sugar cooked together, finished with cognac, amaretto, and rose water.
- **House Peychaud's Bitters (batch):** 2 parts Peychaud's Bitters to 1 part Bitter Truth Creole Bitters.
- **House Orange Bitters (batch):** equal parts Fee Brothers West Indian orange bitters, Regans' orange bitters, and Angostura orange bitters.

This gives a representative cross-section: classic-template variations, all major shaken/stirred spirit chapters, punch, julep, swizzle, flip/fizz formats, and the house infrastructure of syrups/bitters/infusions that underlies nearly every recipe in the book.

<!-- ===== INÍCIO: death-and-co-welcome-home.md ===== -->

# Death & Co: Welcome Home — A Cocktail Recipe Book

**Authors:** Alex Day, Nick Fauchald & David Kaplan (Death & Co)
**Publisher:** Ten Speed Press / Penguin Random House, 2021 — ISBN 9781984858412

This is Death & Co's third book, and second full cocktail-recipe volume after the original *Death & Co: Modern Classic Cocktails* (2014) and the technique-focused *Cocktail Codex* (2018). It documents the company's evolution from a single NYC bar (opened New Year's Eve 2006) into a multi-city hospitality company (Death & Co New York, Denver, and Los Angeles, run alongside consulting firm Proprietors LLC), written substantially during the COVID-19 shutdown of 2020. Framed as a new-hire "employee handbook," the book is explicitly structured to walk a reader through the same onboarding arc as a rookie Death & Co bartender: philosophy and setup → ingredient literacy → menu-creation process → home application → the recipes themselves.

## Structural Overview

The book has five chapters plus appendices, meant to be read in order (each chapter builds on the last), though the authors say you can skip straight to the recipes:

1. **Preparation** — mindset, physical bar setup, tools, glassware, ice, garnishes, core technique (jiggering, shaking, stirring, straining), and an intro to cocktail-creation philosophy.
2. **Selection** — how to taste and evaluate spirits and modifiers; a spirit-by-spirit reference (raw materials, geography, categories, aging, "in cocktails" use) covering vodka, gin, tequila, mezcal, rum, American whiskey, Scotch/Irish whiskey, unaged brandy, aged brandy; then modifiers (liqueurs, amari, fortified wines) and bitters.
3. **Development** — how Death & Co actually designs a bar menu, from intellectual concept through physical testing to finalization/launch.
4. **Cocktails at Home** — adapting bar practice to the home: a curated 12-bottle/50-cocktail starter bar, "dusty bottle" ideas, batching strategies for groups, the "Freezer Bar," carbonation, and a full set of home-bartender substitution rules.
5. **Specs** — the recipe collection itself (roughly 500 recipes), organized into six named categories.

Appendices cover **Bar Prep Recipes & Techniques** (syrups/cordials, infusions, house bitters/mixes/juices/shrubs, solutions/tinctures, garnishes) followed by Resources, a recommended-reading "Bookshelf," Acknowledgments, and an Index. Scattered through the main text are "The Regulars" sidebars — interviews with bar regulars, each paired with one of their favorite recipes.

## House Philosophy & Techniques

### Core philosophy: "intention" and "Always Be Knolling" (ABK)
The book's guiding word is **intention** — having a clear vision of the end result before beginning any task, from building one drink to designing a whole menu. This is paired with **"Always Be Knolling" (ABK)**, borrowed from artist Tom Sachs's *Ten Bullets*: keep your station rigorously organized (scan for unused tools, put them away, group like objects, align everything at parallel/90°) as both a practical and mental discipline. The refrain "reset to zero" describes returning your station to its starting configuration after every drink.

### Technique progression: comprehension → fluency → speed
New skills are taught in three stages: first slow, merciless accuracy (comprehension); then repeated practice broken into isolated component motions, "isolate, then combine" (fluency); only then speed. Working fast is not the goal — muscle memory and mind-body coordination are.

### Core bar techniques (Ch. 1)
- **Jiggering:** two-handed technique, pour to a flat/level rim (not a convex meniscus — abandoned as inconsistent), ambidextrous practice.
- **Shaking:** standard is the 18-oz/28-oz tin set, 5–7 Kold-Draft-size (~1") cubes, circular ice motion (not back-and-forth piston), 10–15 seconds total (build to top speed ~5 sec, hold, decelerate). Variants: **short shake** (~5 sec, for drinks going over ice or topped with something bubbly), **whip shake** (with a few crushed-ice pebbles, until ice fully melts, for crushed-ice drinks), **dry shake** (no ice, for egg-white drinks, then shaken again with ice).
- **Stirring:** ice built as an "upside-down pyramid" in a pint glass, ~15 seconds, taste via straw or back-of-hand.
- **Straining:** julep strainer or Hawthorne for stirred drinks; Hawthorne (with "gate control") mandatory for shaken drinks; **double straining** (Hawthorne + fine mesh cone strainer) for most shaken up-drinks.
- **Rule of thumb — shaken vs. stirred:** anything with citrus, egg, or cream is shaken; anything mostly spirituous (vermouth, bitters, small amounts of sugar) is stirred.

### Ice
Categorized by function: large blocks (2½×2×2") for old-fashioned-style stirred/shaken service; Kold-Draft 1¼" cubes for shaking and collins-style service; crushed/pellet ice (Scotsman machine) for juleps, tropical drinks, swizzles, coolers. Home substitute for crushed ice: ice cubes smashed in a canvas bag with a mallet/rolling pin.

### Tasting methodology (Ch. 2)
"Smell first, taste second"; three sips with water in between (clear, introduce, contemplate); reflect and discuss using personal/memory-based language rather than jargon. A full sequence of guided "palate lessons" builds understanding via **Daiquiri variants** that isolate one variable at a time: Sweet & Sour (tart/balanced/sweet ratios), Strong vs. Weak (alcohol %), Salt (2 drops vs. 1 tsp salt solution), Bitter (incremental Campari), Temperature (frozen/room-temp/chilled), Dilution (under-/properly-/over-diluted). Spirit sections follow the same experimental pattern (e.g., three vodkas in a stripped-down vodka soda then vodka sour; three gins in a Tom Collins then a Negroni).

### Menu development process (Ch. 3)
Death & Co's menus are split into three phases: **Intellectual Development** (wireframe spreadsheet: section, cocktail, Style, Flavor Profile, Garnish, Glassware, Bartender, Ice — filled in collaboratively before any liquid is poured), **Physical Development** (recipe testing with a "five tries and you're out" rule; team tastings ~7 weeks and ~1–2 weeks before launch), and **Finalization** (naming, writing menu copy, costing, batching/station-mapping, prep lists, and a literal hour-by-hour "Menu Launch" day schedule from 3am cleanup to 3am reopening-day close).

Named creative strategies for inventing drinks: **The Batchelor** (repurposing a service batch as its own drink), **New Toy!** (built around a novel ingredient), **Liquid Lunch** (inspired by a dish), **Super Mr. Potato Head** (swapping components in a past Death & Co drink, not just a classic), **Fancy Flex** (a "luxury" section built around one expensive ingredient), **I'll Just Have a Beer** (beer-flavor-profile drinks), **Prep School Standout** (starting from a syrup/infusion idea), **Uncharted Territory** ("what if…" experiments). Also "Flavor Syllogisms" (A loves B, B loves C, does C love A?) as a brainstorming device, illustrated with a worked table of examples.

**No-ABV strategies:** purées as base (body/texture), verjus as base+balancer, nonalcoholic "spirit" replacements (Seedlip, Giffard Aperitif Syrup), no-ABV milk punch. **Low-ABV strategies:** split base (spirit + lower-proof modifier), "flip the script" (invert spirit:vermouth ratio), riffing on classics like the Bamboo.

### The six-category menu structure
Since roughly 2018, all Death & Co menus (and this book's recipe chapter) use six categories, each anchored to one of the six "root cocktails" from *Cocktail Codex*:

| Category | Root cocktail | Character |
|---|---|---|
| Fresh & Lively | Highball | Highball/spritz variations |
| Light & Playful | Daiquiri | Simple sours |
| Bright & Confident | Sidecar | Complex sours (liqueur-driven) |
| Boozy & Honest | Old-Fashioned | Old-Fashioned and variations |
| Elegant & Timeless | Martini | Martini, Manhattan, Negroni riffs |
| Rich & Comforting | Flip | Decadent/rich (flips, tiki, piña colada-style) |

Within each section, drinks run low-to-high proof, and each section is required to contain at least one no-ABV and one low-ABV option — a deliberate hospitality/wayfinding device.

### Home adaptation (Ch. 4)
**12 bottles → 50 cocktails** starter bar: Beefeater gin, Flor de Caña 4-yr white rum, El Tesoro Blanco tequila, Famous Grouse blended scotch, Elijah Craig 12-yr bourbon, Busnel VSOP calvados, Paul Beau VS cognac, Campari, Amaro Averna, Cointreau, Cocchi Vermouth di Torino, Dolin dry vermouth — plus bitters, syrups, seltzer/sparkling wine, citrus, egg/dairy/spices. "Always start with a Daiquiri."

**Dusty Bottles** framework for using underused ingredients, with five roles to try a bottle in: Gateway Cocktail, Same Same But Different, Star of the Show, Supporting Character, Unexpected Use (plus ★ for a true classic) — demonstrated across absinthe, Aperol, aquavit, blackstrap rum, cachaça, Chartreuse, crème de cacao, Islay scotch, Kalani coconut liqueur, maraschino, menthe-pastille, pear eau-de-vie, pisco, Suze.

**Batching spectrum for entertaining**, least to most complex:
- *Minimally batched* ("choose your own"): DIY stations (Old-Fashioned Your Way, High Five Your Way, Bellini/Mimosa Your Way, Bloody Mary Your Way) where guests pick a spirit and combine with a pre-made base.
- *Partially batched*: combine stable alcoholic ingredients ahead; add fragile ingredients (egg, aquafaba, effervescence) to order. Rule of thumb: shake up to 2 cocktails per shaker tin (3 if champagne-topped, since bubbly adds most of the volume).
- *Fully batched*: all ingredients combined ahead (max 6 hrs, chilled) — used for punches. Punch built as a scaled-up Tom Collins-style citrus+spirit+bubbles construction, served over one large block, not chips.
- **Carbonated cocktails**: three variables — temperature (chill everything cold before charging), clarity (fresh citrus doesn't carbonate cleanly; use acid solutions — citric/lactic/malic/phosphoric — or clarified juice instead), dilution (aim for 50–60% water/effervescent content in the final drink).
- **The Freezer Bar**: batch a spirituous drink (≥30% ABV total so it won't freeze at ~0°F/-1.75°F freezing point) plus a calculated water addition (10–15% for vermouth-based Manhattan/Martini-style; ~20% for Old-Fashioned/Negroni-style since they're diluted further on the rocks anyway), then store and pour to order. Bitters intensify in large batches — halve the recipe amount in batches over 5 drinks, then adjust to taste. Freezer batches can themselves become base spirits for spritzes, highballs, sours/fizzes/collinses, or be flavor-infused (iSi pressure-infusion or 24-hr steep) — e.g., Strawberry Negroni, Meyer Lemon Martini, Chocolate Manhattan, Celery Martini.

**Home Bartender Adaptability Rules**: measurement substitutes (1 tbsp = ½ oz), fixing a botched pour by rebalancing the other ingredients proportionally rather than starting over; ice-quantity fixes for under-chilled stirred/shaken drinks; freezing all stemware; skipping the mixing glass (build directly in an old-fashioned glass over one large cube); spirit-type and spirit-brand substitution rules (stay within style: gin for gin not genever, blanco rum for blanco not aged, etc.); glassware substitution priorities (Nick & Nora as the single most versatile glass if you can only own one stemmed glass).

## Recipe Organization Scheme

Chapter 5 ("Specs") presents roughly 500 recipes as a flat alphabetical list *within* each of the six category files (no further subdivision by spirit). Each recipe entry includes:
- Title, creator name and year
- Optional short first-person "why I made this" quote from the bartender
- Ingredient list (in order added — but note the "Working in Zones" sidebar explains bartenders actually build back-to-front from cheapest/smallest to priciest during service)
- Garnish line
- Method paragraph (shake/stir/build, glass, ice, garnish execution)

**Icon tags** mark special recipe attributes, used throughout chapter 5:
- **LOW-ABV**
- **NO-ABV**
- **FREEZER BAR** (safe to batch-and-freeze)
- **PROJECT COCKTAIL** (multi-step/equipment-heavy)
- **LOW-PREP COCKTAIL** (minimal advance prep)

A short "bitters note" applies globally: recipe bitters amounts assume standard retail dasher bottles; double the dash count if using fancier/wider dasher bottles.

## Representative Recipes by Category

### Fresh & Lively (Highball root)
**Achilles' Heel** (Matthew Belanger, 2018) — 2 oz seltzer; 1 oz Lemorton Selection calvados Domfrontais; ½ oz Amrut cask-strength single malt Indian whisky; ½ oz Henriques & Henriques rainwater madeira; ¼ oz Lejay crème de cassis de Dijon; ¾ oz fresh lemon juice; ½ oz Demerara Syrup; 2 dashes Miracle Mile Redeye bitters. Garnish: lemon wheel + grated coffee bean. Method: pour seltzer into collins glass; short shake rest 5 sec; strain in; top with ice.

**Bastille** (Tyson Buhler, 2017) — 2 oz dry sparkling wine; 1½ oz Domaine du Manoir de Montreuil calvados; ¾ oz fresh lemon juice; ½ oz Pineau des Charentes; ½ oz Suze; ½ oz Cane Sugar Syrup; 1 dash celery bitters. Sparkling wine in chilled flute; shake rest with ice, double strain in. No garnish.

**Bulldog Front** (Matthew Belanger, 2017) — 1½ oz Westbrook Gose beer; 1 oz Beefeater gin; ½ oz Krogstad aquavit; ½ oz crème de pamplemousse rose; 1 tsp Campari; ¾ oz fresh lemon juice; ½ oz Cane Sugar Syrup. Garnish: lemon wheel. Beer-and-gin shandy-style highball.

**Cloud Nine** (Tyson Buhler, 2017) — 2 oz seltzer; 1½ oz Cyril Zangs 00 apple cider eau-de-vie; ½ oz rhum agricole blanc; ¾ oz fresh lime juice; ½ oz Cane Sugar Syrup; 2 dashes absinthe; 1 dash Malic Acid Solution; 1 egg white. Dry shake, then shake with ice; double strain into fizz glass over seltzer.

**Charlemagne** (Tyson Buhler, 2018) — 1½ oz blended scotch; ¾ oz Cucumber Magnesium Syrup; 4 oz cold seltzer. Simplest highball form: build in collins glass over ice, top with seltzer, no garnish.

**Gatekeeper** (Shannon Tebay, 2019) — full-carbonation spritz: 1 oz Old Raj 110-proof gin; ½ oz Cap Corse blanc quinquina; 1 tsp apple cider eau-de-vie; ½ oz Celery Syrup; ½ tsp Malic Acid Solution; 3 oz dry sparkling wine, all chilled, charged in a carbonating bottle, rested 20 min–12 hrs, poured into a flute.

### Light & Playful (Daiquiri root)
**Atlantic Pacific** (Matthew Belanger, 2017) — 1½ oz Diplomático Planas rum; ½ oz nori-infused rhum agricole blanc; ½ oz Yuzu Shu liqueur; ¾ oz fresh lime juice; ½ oz Cane Sugar Syrup. Shake, double strain into coupe, no garnish.

**Buko Gimlet** (Matthew Belanger, 2019) — 1 oz Navy Strength gin; ¾ oz cachaça; ¼ oz Kalani coconut liqueur; ¾ oz fresh lime juice; ½ oz Pandan Syrup; ½ oz coconut water. Shake, strain over one large cube; grated lime zest.

**City Club** (Matthew Belanger, 2019) — Clover Club variant: 1 oz pisco; ½ oz basil eau-de-vie; ½ oz dry vermouth; ¾ oz fresh lemon juice; ¾ oz Strawberry Syrup; 1 egg white. Dry shake, shake with ice, double strain; overproof Jamaican rum sprayed (atomizer) on top instead of mixed in.

**Congress Park Swizzle** (Keely Sutherland, 2019) — mint muddled; 1 oz blanc vermouth; ½ oz Jamaica rum; ½ oz mango brandy; ¾ oz fresh lime juice; ½ oz Pineapple Gum Syrup. Whip-shake with crushed ice, swizzle-style service, Peychaud's dashed over mint garnish.

**Curtis Park Swizzle** (Tyson Buhler, 2018) — split-base sherry swizzle: 1 oz Singani 63 eau-de-vie; 1 oz amontillado sherry; ¾ oz House Ginger Syrup; ½ oz fresh lime juice; 2 dashes absinthe. Short shake, strain over crushed ice, mint bouquet + Angostura garnish.

**Dunmore** (Adam Griggs, 2019) — NO-ABV Daiquiri-style: 1½ oz Seedlip Spice 94; 1½ oz fresh pineapple juice; 1 oz fresh lime juice; ¾ oz Tonic Syrup; ¾ tsp mango purée; ¾ tsp Vanilla Syrup. Shake, double strain into coupe.

### Bright & Confident (Sidecar root)
**The Argument** (Matthew Belanger, 2016) — 1½ oz Lustau Solera Reserva brandy; ½ oz Smith & Cross Jamaica rum; 1 oz Donn's Mix #1; ½ oz fresh lime juice; ¼ oz Vanilla Syrup; ¼ oz Campari; 3 blackberries. Shake, double strain, no garnish.

**Benicia Park Swizzle** (Eryn Reece, 2014) — 1 oz Highland Park 12-yr scotch; 1 oz amontillado sherry; ¾ oz fresh lemon juice; ½ oz House Ginger Syrup; ½ oz Velvet falernum; ¼ oz Giffard Muroise du Val de Loire (floated). Whip-shake, pack pilsner glass with crushed ice, float the Muroise over a spoon back, mint leaf garnish.

**Bushido** (Matthew Belanger, 2017) — 1½ oz Nikka Taketsuru whisky; 2 tsp Kalani coconut liqueur; 1 tsp Laphroaig 10-yr scotch; 1 oz Donn's Mix #1; ½ oz fresh lime juice; 1 tsp Cane Sugar Syrup; 1 dash celery bitters. Shake, double strain, no garnish.

**Calypso** (Jonnie Long, 2019) — vegan egg-white-style sour: 2 oz junmai sake; ½ oz Sercial madeira; ½ oz Giffard banane du Brésil; ¾ oz fresh lemon juice; ½ oz aquafaba; ½ oz Cane Sugar Syrup. Dry shake, shake with ice, double strain over one large cube; banana leaf garnish. (Also used in Ch. 4 as the model partial-batching example: 20/5/5 oz scaled batch, then finished per-drink with fresh lemon, aquafaba, syrup.)

### Boozy & Honest (Old-Fashioned root)
**Altimeter Julep** (Jarred Weigand, 2017) — 1½ oz El Tesoro Reposado tequila; ½ oz 8-yr apple brandy; ¼ oz yellow Chartreuse; ¼ oz Douglas Fir eau-de-vie; ¼ oz maple syrup. Built and swizzled in a julep tin with crushed ice, mint bouquet + dried apple slice.

**Busy Earning** (Tyson Buhler, 2015, FREEZER BAR) — 1½ oz Redbreast 12-yr Irish whiskey; ½ oz Cruzan Black Strap rum; ¼ oz Laphroaig 10-yr scotch; 1 tsp Sarsaparilla Demerara Syrup; 1 dash Angostura. Stir over ice, strain over one large cube, orange twist.

**Calypso King** (Tyson Buhler, 2016, FREEZER BAR) — luxury-ingredient Old-Fashioned: 1 oz Navazos Palazzi oloroso rum; 1 oz Redbreast 12-yr; 1 tsp Pineapple Gum Syrup; ½ tsp House Orgeat; 1 dash tiki bitters. Stir, strain over one cube, grapefruit twist.

**Empty Nester** (Dave Anderson, 2019) — sherry cobbler riff: lemon coin + orange half wheel muddled; 1½ oz manzanilla sherry; 1 oz Bonal Gentiane-Quina; ½ oz Amaro Nonino; 1 tsp apple cider eau-de-vie; ¼ oz Cinnamon Syrup. Whip-shake, strain into julep tin over crushed ice, elaborate "cobbler shit" garnish (mint, orange, apple, powdered sugar, cinnamon stick).

**Event Horizon** (Sam Johnson, 2018) — 2 oz Siete Leguas Añejo tequila; ¾ oz Fernet-Vallet; 1 tsp macadamia nut syrup; ½ tsp macadamia nut liqueur (double-macadamia technique: syrup for sweetness, liqueur for aroma).

### Elegant & Timeless (Martini/Manhattan/Negroni root)
**A Clockwork Orange** (Matthew Belanger, 2017, FREEZER BAR) — 1 oz Tanqueray No. Ten gin; 1 oz Dolin blanc vermouth; ½ oz Mirabelle eau-de-vie; ½ oz Mandarine Napoléon; 1 tsp Suze. Stir, strain over one large cube, lemon twist.

**Alta Negroni** (Matthew Belanger, 2019, FREEZER BAR) — 1 oz St. George Terroir gin; 1 oz Cocchi Americano; ½ oz Salers Gentiane aperitif; ½ oz Pajarote Toronja Arandense & Romero Licor. Stir, strain over one cube, grapefruit wheel.

**As Islay Dying** (Matt Hunt, 2018, FREEZER BAR) — equal-parts-plus-accent Martini: ¾ oz Laphroaig 10-yr scotch; ¾ oz The Botanist gin; ¾ oz Dolin blanc vermouth; ¾ oz Dolin dry vermouth; 1 tsp Dolin Génépy des Alpes; 2 dashes absinthe. Stir, strain into Nick & Nora, lemon twist expressed and discarded.

**Business Casual** (Jon Mateer, 2019, NO-ABV) — Negroni-style with no alcohol: 1¼ oz Giffard Aperitif Syrup; ¾ oz chilled brewed black tea (stands in for gin botanicals); 1 oz Red Verjus Syrup; 1 tsp Cane Sugar Syrup. Stir, strain over one cube, orange half wheel.

**Bruno's Martini** (a Regular's favorite, from Ch. 1) — 2½ oz Blue Gin; ½ oz Dolin dry vermouth; 1 dash House Orange Bitters. Stir, strain into Nick & Nora, lemon twist expressed then dropped in.

### Rich & Comforting (Flip root)
**Arethusa** (Matthew Belanger, 2019, LOW-ABV) — low-ABV Grasshopper: 1 oz Amaro Braulio; ½ oz mezcal; ½ oz white crème de cacao; ½ oz menthe-pastille; ¾ oz heavy cream; 6–8 mint leaves. Shake, strain over one large cube, mint leaf garnish.

**Bat Country** (Jonnie Long, 2019) — served hot or cold; cold version: ¾ oz cognac; ½ oz Batavia arrack; ¼ oz Grand Marnier; ¼ oz Galliano Ristretto; ½ oz Milk Syrup; 1 oz cold brew coffee floated on top over crushed ice. Hot version swaps in hot coffee and Demerara syrup, served in an Irish coffee mug.

**Bum Leg** (Adam Griggs, 2019) — tiki-style Lime-in-a-Coconut homage: 1 oz Plantation Barbados 5-yr rum; ½ oz Smith & Cross Jamaica rum; ½ oz oloroso sherry; 1 oz unsweetened coconut milk; ¾ oz fresh lime juice; ½ oz Coco López; ½ oz Demerara Syrup; 2 dashes Angostura. Whip-shake, swizzle in tiki mug over crushed ice, lime zest + nutmeg.

**Vintage Eggnog** (Tyson Buhler, 2015) — large-format batch, makes ~30 cocktails: 50 oz sugar; 30 oz whole milk; 33 eggs; one 750ml bottle Old Grand-Dad 114 bourbon; 12½ oz Jamaica rum; 12½ oz Malmsey madeira; 12½ oz cognac; 20 oz heavy cream. Blended in stages, bottled, aged in the fridge 2 weeks to 2 years.

**Southern Nights** (Alex Jump, 2018) — ¾ oz cognac; ¾ oz Linie aquavit; ¾ oz peanut-infused oloroso sherry; ½ oz Cane Sugar Syrup; ¼ oz fresh lemon juice; 1 barspoon mascarpone cheese. Shake, strain over crushed ice, peanut brittle garnish.

## Bar Prep: Techniques & Method Sections (Appendix)

**Syrups & Cordials** — dozens of recipes, almost all by weight in grams. Two base methods dominate:
- *Cold-blend syrups* (e.g., Cane Sugar Syrup: 300g sugar : 150g water, blended until dissolved) — for stable dry sugars.
- *Sous vide (immersion-circulator) syrups* (e.g., Cinnamon Syrup, Demerara Syrup, Grapefruit Cordial, Alpenglow Cordial): ingredients vacuum/press-sealed in a bag, cooked 1–2 hrs at 130–145°F, ice-bathed, then fine-strained (with a paper filter or "Superbag" as needed for full clarity).

**Sous Vide Infusions — full method:**
1. Fill basin, set immersion circulator to target temp (typically 135–145°F: lower for delicate ingredients like fruit, higher for sturdy ones like nuts/spices).
2. Weigh ingredients into a bowl, transfer to a sealable heatproof bag, press out air by water-displacement sealing.
3. Cook in the bath for the specified time (usually ~2 hrs).
4. Ice-bath to cool (this recondenses vapor and preserves ABV).
5. Strain through fine mesh, then paper filter/Superbag if needed; refrigerate.

**Pressurized (iSi) Infusions — full method:** for fragile, fast-perishing flavors (herbs) or ingredients with wide flavor ranges (cacao nibs). Ingredients loaded into a 1-qt iSi whipper below the max line, charged with two N₂O cartridges (shaking between), rested under pressure ~10 min, then rapidly vented and strained. Because extraction is so fast there's less risk of off-flavors from long maceration; delicate infusions are best used within 1 week–1 month.

**Clarified Juices** — two methods:
- *Agar clarification*: juice combined with an agar-gelled portion of water (agar dosed at 0.2% of combined weight), the gelled agar broken into curds and squeezed through cheesecloth to release clear liquid, leaving cloudy solids behind. Used within 1 day (fragile) unless preserved in a batched/sweetened/spirited cocktail.
- *Centrifuge clarification*: juice dosed with Pectinex Ultra SP-L (a pectinase) then Kieselsol/Chitosan fining agents (each ~0.2% of weight, rested 15 min between additions), spun at 4,500 rpm for 12 minutes in balanced containers, then strained.

**Centrifuge Infusions (flavor extraction from solids):** solid + spirit blended (starting ratios by weight: ~1:4 for dry ingredients like dried fruit/graham cracker, ~1:2 for moist ones like banana/strawberry, ~1:1 for very subtle ones like watermelon), strained, dosed with Pectinex, spun at 4,500 rpm/12 min, strained again.

**House Bitters, Mixes, Juices & Shrubs** — includes House Orange Bitters (equal parts Fee Brothers, Angostura, and Regans' orange bitters blended together), Donn's Mix #1 (grapefruit juice + Cinnamon Syrup, the tiki-history base used throughout the book), Hibiscus Shrub (vinegar-steeped, reduced, then blended with sugar and water), Tepache (spiced fermented-pineapple base, 4-day room-temp ferment).

**Solutions & Tinctures** — simple percentage solutions used as seasoning/acid-adjustment tools: Citric Acid Solution (20% by weight in water), Malic Acid Solution (20%), Lactic Acid Solution (10%), Salt Solution (30%), Champagne Acid Solution (tartaric + lactic blend), Habanero Tincture (vodka-based, 24-hr steep).

**Garnishes** — dehydrator techniques (135°F, several hours) for dehydrated apple slices, citrus wheels, and pineapple wedges (soaked in salted water first to prevent browning); Campari-Infused Toasted Coconut (infuse then dehydrate the reserved solids); Toasted Oat Cream (toasted oats steeped in heavy cream 24 hrs, strained).

<!-- ===== INÍCIO: dicionario-de-sabores.md ===== -->

# Dicionário de Sabores — Niki Segnit

**Título original:** *The Flavour Thesaurus: Pairings, Recipes and Ideas for the Creative Cook*
**Autora:** Niki Segnit
**Edição lida:** Casa da Palavra Produção Editorial, 1ª ed., 2014 — ISBN13 9788577344550
**Arquivo:** "Dicionário de sabores ... Niki Segnit ... Anna's Archive.pdf" (372 páginas)

**Nota sobre duplicata:** o arquivo `524348673-Dicionario-de-sabores.pdf` (3,0MB) presente na mesma pasta é a **mesma obra/tradução** (mesmo título, mesma autora, mesmo texto de abertura conferido nas primeiras páginas). Trata-se de um duplicado do mesmo PDF/scan — não foi reprocessado integralmente por não haver diferença de conteúdo relevante. Recomenda-se manter apenas um dos dois arquivos.

---

## 1. Visão estrutural

O livro é organizado como um **tesauro de sabores**: em vez de capítulos por tipo de prato, cada capítulo é um **ingrediente**, e dentro dele há sub-verbetes para cada **parceiro de sabor** desse ingrediente (ex.: capítulo "Cordeiro" contém os verbetes "Cordeiro e açafrão", "Cordeiro e amêndoa", "Cordeiro e anchova" etc.). Cada verbete mistura trivia histórica/cultural, química de sabor básica, referências a chefs/livros de receita e, com frequência, uma receita curta e prática.

Os ~99 ingredientes-capítulo são agrupados em **16 famílias de sabor**, dispostas como uma "roda dos sabores" (apresentada logo na abertura do livro) que vai de tons torrados/terrosos a florais/frutados:

1. **Torrados** (Roasted) — café, amendoim, cacau/chocolate, castanhas torradas, pão torrado...
2. **Carnes** (Meaty) — frango, porco, morcela (blood sausage), fígado, carne bovina, cordeiro
3. **Queijos** (Cheesy) — queijo de cabra, queijo de casca lavada, queijo azul, queijo duro, queijo macio
4. **Terrosos** (Earthy) — cogumelo, berinjela, cominho, beterraba, alho, trufa, repolho, nabo-redondo, couve-flor
5. **Amostardados** (Mustardy) — repolho, rabanete, raiz-forte, mostarda, agrião
6. **Sulfurosos** (Sulphurous) — alho, cebola, aspargo, ovo
7. **Marinhos** (Oceanic) — ostra, caviar, peixe gorduroso, peixe branco
8. **Salmouras e sais** (Brine and Salt-cured) — anchova, peixe defumado, bacon, presunto cru, azeitona
9. **Verdes e gramíneos** (Green and Grassy) — ervilha, aspargo, ervas frescas
10. **Condimentos** (Spice) — canela, cravo, gengibre, açafrão, baunilha
11. **Amadeirados** (Woody) — alecrim, tomilho, especiarias amadeiradas
12. **Frutas frescas** (Fresh Fruit) — maçã, pera, damasco
13. **Frutas cremosas** (Creamy Fruit) — abacate, banana, coco
14. **Cítricos** (Citrus) — limão-siciliano, laranja
15. **Frutas silvestres e arbustos** (Berries and Bush Fruit) — framboesa, groselha, amora
16. **Florais frutados** (Fruity Floral) — rosa, violeta, elderflower

A obra funciona por **remissões cruzadas** ("Ver também X e Y") que ligam ingredientes de famílias diferentes quando compartilham compostos de sabor — é o dispositivo central do livro: mostrar que a afinidade entre dois ingredientes muitas vezes tem uma base química (ex.: ácido cáprico ligando queijo de cabra a alcaparras; DMS ligando trufa, repolho cozido e mariscos; lentionina ligando shiitake e alho).

## 2. Síntese do conteúdo central

- **Método da autora:** para cada ingrediente-âncora, Segnit lista de 10 a 30+ parceiros, ordenados alfabeticamente, cada um justificado por (a) mecanismo de sabor/aroma partilhado, (b) tradição culinária regional que já consagrou o par, e/ou (c) uma receita ou preparo testado por ela.
- **Ciência do sabor aplicada de forma leve:** compostos citados recorrentemente — DMS (dimetil sulfeto, liga repolho cozido, trufa, marisco, milho), lentionina (shiitake/alho), ácido cáprico (queijo de cabra/alcaparra), 2-metil-3-furantiol (carne cozida/caldo de galinha/atum enlatado), 1-octen-3-ona (cogumelos), cetona-2-heptanona e 2-nonanona (queijo azul), bis(metiltio)-metano (trufa branca/lagosta/shiitake/camembert).
- **Tom:** ensaístico, bem-humorado, pessoal (relatos de experiências da autora, citações literárias, anedotas de chefs), não é um livro de receitas tradicional — as receitas são secundárias, quase sempre curtas, para ilustrar o par.
- **Escopo geográfico:** cobre tradições culinárias muito amplas — britânica, francesa, italiana, indiana, do Oriente Médio, do Sudeste Asiático, escandinava, latino-americana, dos EUA — sempre citando pratos-âncora específicos (ex.: fabada asturiana, tsimmes judaico, pulgogi coreano, patlıcan kebap turco, gravlax escandinavo).
- **Estrutura de cada capítulo:** abre com um parágrafo introdutório sobre o sabor/textura característico do ingrediente e sua "personalidade" na cozinha, seguido pelos verbetes de pares em ordem alfabética.

## 3. Amostra organizada de combinações de sabor (por categoria)

### Carnes
- **Frango + alho** — frango à Provençal com 40 dentes de alho (o alho perde pungência e ganha gosto de castanha assada).
- **Morcela (blood sausage) + maçã** — clássico europeu; morcela e maçã caramelizada também aparecem com foie gras (receita do chef Andrew Pern, restaurante The Star).
- **Morcela + hortelã** — favas com morcela e hortelã fresca (inspirado em *habas con morcilla*, cozinha espanhola).
- **Fígado + bacon** — combinação clássica: fígado magro + bacon gordo; recomenda-se deixar o fígado de molho no leite antes de cozinhar.
- **Fígado + cebola** — universal: *fegato alla veneziana* (Itália), fígado tandoori com cebolas (Índia), fígado empanado sobre cebola frita (Polônia).
- **Carne bovina + alcaparra** — molho para rosbife frio com azeite, limão-siciliano, salsa e alcaparras.
- **Carne bovina + trufa** — Tournedos Rossini (filé + foie gras + molho madeira + lascas de trufa); carpaccio italiano com trufa branca ralada.
- **Carne bovina + ovo** — beef tartare, com gema crua, alcaparra, chalota, mostarda e anchova.
- **Cordeiro + açafrão** — chelow kebab iraniano (espetinhos de cordeiro com arroz de açafrão).
- **Cordeiro + anchova** — anchovas inseridas em incisões no pernil antes de assar; a anchova "se dissolve" na carne intensificando o sabor sem deixar gosto de peixe.
- **Cordeiro + damasco** — *mishmishiya*, tajine de cordeiro e damasco do livro árabe do século XIII *The Baghdad Cookery Book*.
- **Cordeiro + hortelã** — analisado com ressalva: franceses estranham, mas a hortelã "corta" o odor forte característico do cordeiro (ver molho *paloise*, variação do *béarnaise* com hortelã no lugar do estragão).

### Queijos
- **Queijo de cabra + alcaparra** — ligados pelo ácido cáprico, presente em ambos.
- **Queijo de cabra + alho** — pizza de queijo de cabra e alho (receita do restaurante Orso, Londres); *foudjou* francês (queijo de cabra fresco e maturado com alho, ervas e conhaque).
- **Queijo de cabra + damasco** — damasco seco recheado com queijo de cabra: doce, salgado e "remete à carne de cordeiro" via ácido caprílico.
- **Queijo de casca lavada + cominho** — clássico do queijo *munster* (Alsácia), tradicionalmente servido com batatas cozidas e grãos de cominho.
- **Queijo de casca lavada + maçã** — Pont l'Évêque com maçã ácida ou sidra; *celtic promise* lavado em sidra.
- **Queijo azul + abacaxi** — combinação "TGRWT" (They Go Really Well Together) do blog Khymos.

### Terrosos
- **Cogumelo + castanha** — tagliatelle com farinha de castanha e cogumelos porcini secos (norte da Itália); sopa de porcini e castanha.
- **Cogumelo + queijo azul** — polenta com gorgonzola e porcini (clássico do Trentino); risoto de cogumelos finalizado com queijo azul.
- **Berinjela + alho** — *baba ghanoush* (berinjela assada, alho cru, tahine, azeite, limão, salsa).
- **Cominho + coentro em grão** — combinação onipresente na Índia, Oriente Médio e Norte da África; base do *dukkah* egípcio (cominho, coentro, gergelim, avelã).
- **Alho + manjericão** — molho de tomate clássico (o alho realça o dimetil-sulfeto do tomate enlatado; o manjericão devolve frescor "verde").
- **Trufa + repolho** — ligados pelo composto DMS; sopa de repolho trufada (Marco Pierre White).
- **Repolho + porco** — *chou farci* francês (repolho recheado com carne de porco, vitela e barriga de porco, temperado com *quatre-épices*).
- **Nabo-redondo (rutabaga) + noz-moscada** — purê escocês *neeps*, acompanhamento tradicional do haggis.
- **Couve-flor + chocolate** — prato experimental de Heston Blumenthal (risoto de couve-flor com carpaccio de couve-flor e gelatina de chocolate).

### Marinhos e salmouras
- **Ostra + limão-siciliano** — debate entre puristas (sem limão) e a autora (a favor: "o suco de limão limpa o paladar").
- **Ostra + salsa** — Ostras à Rockefeller (Antoine's, Nova Orleans, 1899), molho verde de receita guardada a sete chaves.
- **Caviar + chocolate branco** — parceria estudada por Heston Blumenthal a partir de compostos de sabor compartilhados; comparada à combinação de lagosta com baunilha.
- **Caviar + ostra** — ambos com notas cinza/ferrugem por se alimentarem no fundo do oceano (segundo Harold McGee).
- **Peixe gorduroso + dill** — *gravlax* escandinavo (salmão curado com açúcar, sal e dill).
- **Peixe gorduroso + hortelã** — usada em *laab pla* (Tailândia) e como guarnição vietnamita.
- **Anchova + azeitona** — *pissaladière* de Nice (pão coberto com cebola caramelizada, anchova e azeitona).

## 4. Observações finais

O valor do livro está menos nas receitas (curtas e ilustrativas) e mais no **mapa de afinidades** que ele constrói: qualquer ingrediente pode ser consultado como ponto de partida, e a extensa rede de remissões cruzadas permite montar cardápios ou explorar substituições com embasamento tanto histórico/cultural quanto (informalmente) químico. É uma referência de consulta pontual, não de leitura linear — adequada para brainstorming de pratos e harmonizações.

<!-- ===== FIM: dicionario-de-sabores.md ===== -->
