# Little Book of Training

**Fonte:** `LittleBookofTraining.pdf` — Indie Bartender, 30 páginas.

Manual de "train the trainer" voltado para o papel de **In Store Trainer (IST)**: quem forma novos bartenders dentro de uma casa. Não é um livro de receitas — é um manual de metodologia de ensino aplicada a bar.

## Estrutura

1. Seu papel como IST
2. Certificação de IST
3. Cinco pontos-chave do treinamento
4. Como as pessoas aprendem
5. Aprendizagem adulta e estilos de aprendizagem
6. Cinco métodos de treinamento
7. Construindo um plano de treinamento
8. Habilidades do treinador
9. O Livro de Melhores Práticas (exercícios)
10. Pour-testing
11. Conhecendo seu bar
12. Mantendo a cabeça erguida
13. Bartending com duas mãos
14. Aprendendo coquetéis (e lembrando deles)
15. Estimulando interação
16. Superando barreiras de idioma
17. Atendimento de múltiplos pedidos

## O papel do IST

Processo de aplicação: bartender certificado avisa o gerente → avaliado na review trimestral contra habilidades/comportamentos exigidos → gaps viram plano de desenvolvimento → candidato se qualifica → vaga divulgada, entrevista com Bar Manager + L&D Manager → aprovado inicia certificação.

**Responsabilidades-chave:** montar planos de treinamento com tempo adequado para cada etapa (do setup ao fechamento), definir datas de teste, corrigir provas com 100% de precisão, corrigir spot tests semanais com 100% de precisão, ensinar todas as habilidades técnicas, assistir o GM na análise de necessidade de treinamento trimestral.

**Perfil exigido (person specification):** conhecimento profundo de produto (história, venda sugestiva, todas as receitas do cardápio sem erro, sensibilidades de bebida/fruta/mix), políticas de saúde e segurança, produtividade (pontualidade, multitarefa, precisão), compostura sob pressão, comunicação clara, trabalho em equipe, foco no hóspede, priorização e atitude positiva ("we can do this").

## Certificação de IST

Baseada nos "Four Key Points of Bartending": *"servir consistentemente drinks de alta qualidade para um número variável de hóspedes através de conhecimento de produtos, habilidades e interação."*

Requisitos: pour test final aprovado, teste "Top 130 & Mixers" com nota mínima de 98%, sessão de treinamento individual entregue (tema livre + tema não relacionado ao trabalho + tema escolhido pelo avaliador), certificação de front bar.

Valores da empresa (Indie Bartender): People, Energy, Passion, Pride, Excellence, Rock 'n' Roll.

## Cinco pontos-chave do treinamento

1. **Planejamento** — 90% do treinamento é planejamento, só 10% é o tempo de treino em si. Entrar despreparado = esquecer pontos e desperdiçar tempo.
2. **Comunicação** — dar e receber feedback do treinando; manter contato com o Training Department; registrar sessões.
3. **Flexibilidade** — cada pessoa aprende diferente; não existe um único jeito certo.
4. **Feedback** — o mais imediato possível é o mais valioso; equilibrar positivo e negativo; nunca focar só no erro (senão desmotiva).
5. **Follow-up** — toda meta combinada precisa de data de checagem; falta de follow-up é a maior causa de treinandos perderem prazo.

**Comunicação efetiva** exige 4 elementos: mensagem recebida → entendida → aceita → alguma resposta obtida.

## Como as pessoas aprendem — 4 estágios

1. Inconscientemente incompetente (não sabe que não sabe)
2. Conscientemente incompetente (sabe que não sabe)
3. Conscientemente competente (consegue fazer, mas pensando)
4. Inconscientemente competente (virou hábito automático)

Ao treinar, é preciso às vezes levar o treinando de volta ao estágio 3 ("conscientemente competente"), porque só pensando no processo passo a passo é possível explicá-lo a outra pessoa.

## Estilos de aprendizagem (modelo VARK)

- **Aural** (ouvir/falar): repetir em voz alta, ensaiar com um parceiro, evitar música com letra, usar rimas.
- **Visual** (ver): PowerPoint, cores, diagramas, flashcards, sentar de frente pro quadro.
- **Reading & writing** (ler/escrever): copiar informação repetidamente, anotações, handouts.
- **Kinaesthetic** (fazer): participar, mexer objeto na mão, quebrar tarefa em pedaços pequenos, usar as mãos ao explicar.

Aviso do próprio livro: VARK é guia de preferência, não caixa fechada — a maioria das pessoas mistura estilos.

## Cinco métodos de treinamento

1. **Lecture** (auditivo) — só passar fatos; risco de virar "verbal babble"; usar exemplos, variar tom de voz, resumir com frequência, checar entendimento com perguntas.
2. **Demonstration** (visual+auditivo) — "lecture com props": IST explica o procedimento, depois demonstra.
3. **On the Job Training (OJT)** (cinestésico+visual) — mais eficaz para habilidades físicas. 4 passos: (1) trainer conta e faz, (2) aprendiz conta e trainer faz, repetir quantas vezes precisar, (3) aprendiz conta e aprendiz faz, (4) aprendiz pratica até desempenhar a tarefa com eficiência.
4. **Guided discussion** — melhor forma de recapitular no início ou revisar no fim de uma sessão; perguntas abertas, não dominar a conversa, saber lidar com objeções.
5. **Role play** — simular situações (boas e ruins); IST assume papel e observa; feedback focado em comportamento, nunca na "qualidade da atuação".

## Construindo um plano de treinamento

Mínimo 3 dias úteis por semana com o treinando. 5 passos: **What** (objetivo específico), **Why** (gerar interesse), **Level** (nível atual do treinando), **Materials** (pour-test bottles, Exacto-Pour, handouts), **Method** (escolher com base nos 4 anteriores). Guardar planos usados — reaproveitáveis para futuros treinandos.

## Habilidades do treinador

- **Escuta efetiva:** dar atenção total, não interromper — frustrações não-verbalizadas do treinando aparecem na linguagem corporal.
- **Uso de perguntas:** abertas (não fecham em sim/não, promovem discussão) vs. dirigidas (guiam para resposta específica, forçam conclusão). Técnica de *prompting*: repetir parte da resposta, reforçar com gestos, resumir o que foi dito.
- **Lidando com objeções:** focar a objeção → reformulá-la em voz alta ("Se entendi bem, você acha que...") → tentar resolver com humor → se necessário, ser mais direto → se não resolver, guardar para discutir num intervalo.
- **Gerenciando discussão em grupo:** se sair do tópico, resumir/interromper com pergunta/usar posição física para retomar controle; se alguém não participa, fazer pergunta que sabe que a pessoa consegue responder; se há um "disruptor" persistente, pedir justificativa e envolver o grupo (peer pressure).
- **Dando feedback — DO:** ser específico citando palavras/comportamento exato, manter autoestima do treinando, balancear positivo/negativo. Cuidado com a palavra "MAS" (apaga o elogio anterior) — trocar por "E" muda o efeito ("você está indo bem E eu gostaria de ver X mais rápido").
- **Dando feedback — DON'T:** embaraçar na frente de hóspedes, feedback vago, focar só no "certo alternativo", advinhar motivação.
- **Recebendo feedback — DO:** ver como oportunidade, ouvir sem discordar, pedir exemplos, repetir para confirmar entendimento, agradecer. **DON'T:** se defender, levar para o pessoal, mudar de assunto.
- **Reforço:** aprendizado recompensado é retido melhor. "Reforço positivo" (confirmar mesmo quando a resposta está errada, seguido da resposta certa) funciona; punição só ensina que a resposta estava errada, sem indicar o caminho certo.
- **Sobrecarga de informação:** meta de certificação é 6-8 semanas; sinais de sobrecarga = "lost puppy syndrome", perda de interesse, irritação, repetição do mesmo erro. Remédio: falar motivacional, esclarecer o que já sabe, dar pausa, voltar à zona de conforto, planos diários com metas alcançáveis.
- **Dicas gerais de apresentação:** contato visual, não se encolher, humor sem sarcasmo/piada étnica, chegar cedo, usar o nome do treinando, frases diretas ("Eu quero, eu preciso..." — conotam autoridade), respeitar padrão de uniforme.

## Livro de Melhores Práticas — exercícios de treino

- **Perfect Pour @ 126 BPM:** treinar pour-test no ritmo de 126 batidas por minuto (metrônomo ou música nesse BPM), líquido deve começar a sair exatamente no início da batida. Funciona pros 3 estilos de aprendizagem simultaneamente (visual = bater compasso na bancada; auditivo = ouvir; cinestésico = bater o pé). Músicas sugeridas: Haddaway "What Is Love", Pink "So What", Talk Talk "It's My Life", Madonna "Celebration", Cascada "Evacuate the Dance Floor", The Jacksons "Can You Feel It", Aretha Franklin "Respect". **Cuidado:** é só um estabilizador de ritmo, não pode virar muleta — proibido usar em pour-test final.
- **Bounce the Ounce:** 4 copos no chão, treinando faz bounce pour de agachado até de pé.
- **Bounce, Bounce, Bounce:** copos espalhados pelo bar (estação 1, back bar, estação 2, cafeteira) — treina mobilidade e consciência espacial.
- **Pour Like an Egyptian:** 6 copos Collins empilhados em pirâmide (3-2-1) com guardanapo cobrindo metade de cada copo; objetivo é fazer bounce pour subindo e descendo a pirâmide sem molhar os guardanapos.
- **Know Your Bar:** treinando escreve de memória o speed rail e o back bar, recita da esquerda pra direita e vice-versa; depois, IST cita um fato do produto e o treinando identifica o produto e sua posição.
- **Think About Me and Find Me:** IST chama uma garrafa em voz alta enquanto o treinando está no meio de um pour — treina pour preciso enquanto procura o próximo ingrediente.
- **Aim High (cabeça erguida):** treinando escolhe um ponto fixo (ex.: ventilador de teto) e olha pra ele depois de cada micro-tarefa.
- **The Colours Exercise:** treinando trabalha numa estação com bar de água; trainer levanta cartões coloridos por até 2 segundos, treinando deve nomear a cor ao final da rodada junto com nome/preço do drink. Escala: 2 ou menos drinks = 1 cor; 3-5 drinks = 2 cores; 5+ = 3 cores.
- **The Cuddly Toy Exercise:** um objeto (bichinho de pelúcia etc.) é colocado em pontos aleatórios do campo de visão do treinando durante o turno; cronômetro mede quanto tempo até ele notar.
- **Blind Bounce Pouring:** IST demonstra 3 pours consecutivos olhando para o treinando (não para os copos), depois o treinando repete.
- **Musical Bartenders:** trainer faz pedido, começa a andar, para e o treinando precisa cumprimentar e anotar novo pedido sem perder o anterior.
- **Two-Handed Bartending / Forced One-Handedness:** treinando coloca uma mão no bolso de propósito pra sentir a diferença de velocidade; alternativa (mencionada, mas "pode ficar bagunçado"): lubrificar a mão dominante.
- **Aprendendo coquetéis — 8 técnicas de memorização:** (1) acrônimos com a inicial de cada ingrediente; (2) rimas; (3) associação visual (cor do drink = pista do ingrediente); (4) contar o número de ingredientes (Zombie = 7, Singapore Sling = 8); (5) agrupar flashcards por família (iced teas, coolers, daiquiris); (6) apontar diferenças dentro de um grupo (todas as "Coolers" levam laranja+cranberry, exceto duas); (7) pouco e frequente — 10 por vez; (8) recitar a receita de trás para frente.
- **Encorajando interação:** por que apertamos as mãos (símbolo histórico de confiança — sempre com palma aberta, deixando o hóspede "dominante"); usar o nome do hóspede; "Punch and Judy theory" (nunca dá pra encher um bar instantaneamente — cresce aos poucos, uma pessoa de cada vez); "diga o que está pensando" (treinando narra o que está fazendo em vez de ficar em silêncio pensando na receita); técnicas de memorizar nomes (associar rosto→nome, repetir o nome de volta, repassar mentalmente os nomes dos hóspedes na estação, "mapa do bar" anotado na maquininha); perguntas abertas sobre o dia/planos da noite do hóspede.
- **Superando barreiras de idioma — "10 Terms":** dar 10 termos de bar (ex.: bar caddy, jigger, bounce mat, drip mat, hedgehog) para o estrangeiro aprender da noite pro dia; gesto de boa vontade: o IST aprende a mesma lista no idioma do treinando.
- **Sense of Urgency / In the Weeds:** simular uma situação de "afogamento" — enquanto o treinando faz uma pausa, sujar a estação dele de propósito (hortelã, purê, grenadine na água de enxágue) e dar um prazo (até a música acabar) pra arrumar.
- **Multiple Order Taking — "Post It":** copos highball no balcão, cada um com post-it representando uma pessoa/pedido (ex.: Long Island Iced Tea, Diablo, Piña Colada, Caipirinha); treinando lê os copos, monta os pedidos, cronômetro mede tempo; começa com 2-3 pedidos e sobe até 4-5.

## Ver também

- Complementa manuais de treinamento mais extensos como `match-bar-group-bartenders-manual.md` (carreira/produto) e `mad-dog-staff-manual.md` (cultura de atendimento) — este livro foca especificamente na *metodologia de ensino*, não no conteúdo técnico de bar em si.

<!-- ===== FIM: little-book-of-training.md ===== -->

<!-- ===== INÍCIO: lowder-classic-recipe-training-manual.md ===== -->

# Lowder's Classic Recipe Training Manual

**Source file:** `LowderClassicRecipeTrainingManual.pdf` (44 pages)
**Actual title on document:** "Lowder's Easy List of 178 (Modern) Classics to Know" — 2016, by Chris Lowder (Indie Bartender), foreword signed "@GetLowderNow"

**Note on naming:** despite the filename, this PDF's own title page is "Lowder's Easy List of 178 (Modern) Classics to Know" — the same title as the separate `LowdersEasyListof178ModernClassicsToKnow.xlsx` file in this project. This PDF is the narrative training-manual version (with prose notes per spirit and full recipes), while the xlsx (summarized separately in `lowders-178-modern-classics-list.md`) is a condensed spreadsheet list. They are companion documents from the same author/series, not duplicates — the PDF contains far more instructional text and only a subset of full recipes plus a name-only reference list.

## Structural overview

The manual is a training curriculum, not a full bar book. It has two major parts:

**Part 1 — Core recipes by spirit category (with teaching notes).** For each major spirit, Lowder gives a short essay on how that spirit behaves in cocktails, then exactly six "starter" recipes (three shaken/citrus, three stirred/boozy) — except for smaller categories (Tiki, Pisco, Sherry, Apple Brandy) which get fewer. Categories covered in order:
1. Gin
2. Vodka
3. Tequila/Mezcal
4. American Whiskey (Bourbon + Rye)
5. Irish Whiskey
6. Scotch
7. Rum (white + dark)
8. Tiki (7 recipes)
9. Cognac/Brandy
10. Pisco & Apple Brandy (combined)
11. Sherry (3 recipes)

**Part 2 — "102 More Drinks Everyone Should Know."** An alphabetized reference list (Core Call Drinks A-Z, then Highballs, Martinis, and Call Shots) giving recipe/build only, with no teaching prose — a fast-reference appendix for after the core 6-per-spirit list is mastered.

## Author's philosophy (from the Foreword)

- Written because junior/intermediate bartenders "share a common struggle": writing a bar recipe manual takes forever, managers don't have time, and there's too much disparate info to self-assemble.
- Explicitly **not** trying to be exhaustive or to list "the most delicious cocktails ever invented" — it's a curated *starter kit*.
- Core teaching principle: the **80/20 rule** — 80% of guest orders come from roughly 20% of the possible recipe universe, so mastery should start with a small, high-frequency core rather than memorizing an entire cocktail encyclopedia (explicitly likened to memorizing a Spanish dictionary vs. becoming fluent).
- **Why six drinks per spirit:** three shaken/citrus + three stirred/boozy per spirit is deliberately small and achievable, using ingredients with minimal disparate prep beyond basic juices/syrups.
- Bars/teams are explicitly invited to customize and add to the list — but only *after* mastering the base list, done together with the lead bartender so the whole team edits in sync ("Don't carve ice if you can't cook rice").
- No backstories/attribution are given for the classic recipes (author states he didn't invent any of them) — but he explicitly encourages bartenders to separately research the history of drinks that excite them, since "storytelling texture... can really bring a bar experience to life."
- The 102-drink appendix exists as a secondary goal list — not for immediate memorization, but to master over time or keep as quick reference behind the bar.

## Notable philosophy asides embedded in the spirit sections

- **On vodka & "fake Martini" guest requests (the manual's longest rant):** Bartenders should not sneer at requests for non-classic "Martinis" (Appletini, Chocolate Martini, etc.) by saying "we don't make those." The guest isn't citing a recipe — they're saying "I want to feel comfortable and have something I recognize, made well." Recommended response pattern: accept the request, then elevate it with quality ingredients (e.g., fresh-pressed apple juice instead of neon sour mix for an Apple Martini) and frame it as "I can absolutely make you one — mine uses fresh-pressed apple juice, so it won't be neon green, but it'll be the best Apple Martini you've had. Is that OK?" Vodka drinkers are called "the most fun guests" because they're often the least flavor-educated, giving bartenders the biggest opportunity to impress and win regulars.
- **On tequila/mezcal:** underrepresented in classic cocktails purely because agave spirits arrived in the US cocktail scene too late (after Prohibition) to enter the classic canon — not because they don't work; savory/grassy tequila needs savory/grassy partner flavors. Añejo tequila cocktails are rare mainly due to cost (analogous to rarely seeing an 18-year single malt cocktail).
- **On American whiskey:** bourbon (corn-based, sweeter/softer) generally works better shaken; rye (spicier, higher tannin) holds up better stirred against vermouths/liqueurs without getting lost.
- **On Irish whiskey:** "tragically underrepresented"; mild bottles (Jameson, Bushmills) can get lost shaken, but new premium releases (Redbreast, Connemara, Green Spot) open more cocktail possibilities — at higher cost, so price-awareness matters.
- **On Scotch:** high price is the real limiter, not flavor versatility. Standard technique: rinse the glass with an intense peated/Islay Scotch (e.g., Laphroaig) and build the actual cocktail with a cheaper blended Scotch — gives a balanced smoky accent without the cost of an all-Scotch pour. "A little bit goes a long, long way."
- **On rum:** an extremely broad flavor category; recipes deliberately avoid brand-specificity so bartenders can substitute based on availability, and even blend rum styles ("what one rum can't do, three can"). Stirred rum drinks are rare/tricky (rum's silky richness plus syrup can turn "flabby") but excellent when balanced.
- **On Tiki:** author's own working definition — a Tiki drink blends many syrups/juices/spirits (usually rums) into something rich, boozy, and fun. Crushed/cracked ice is used because these drinks are viscous and rich and benefit from extra dilution/chill; Tiki mugs are functional (ceramic insulates against ambient heat, keeping the drink tasting good longer), not just decorative.
- **On Cognac/Brandy:** priced for sipping, so bars typically stock only one grade (VS = younger/juicier, or VSOP = older/drier/tannic) — know your well cognac's profile since it changes how much sugar/citrus to balance with. Shaken cognac drinks were popular in the 1800s–early 1900s but became rarer as cognac got pricier; many old "cognac sours" from historic books aren't worth the good cognac — the Sidecar is called out as an enduring exception.
- **On Apple Brandy/Pisco:** grouped together for brevity, not because they're related. American apple brandy is drier/spicier (American oak); French apple brandy/calvados is juicier, with aged VSOP/Hors d'age bottlings closer to cognac. Important distinction flagged: **Applejack ≠ apple brandy** — applejack is a blend of apple brandy and American whiskey, a different product; the author suggests blending your own apple-brandy-plus-whiskey mix rather than buying commercial applejack if that specific flavor is wanted. For pisco, Peruvian is the author's preference (richer/silkier) over Chilean.
- **On Sherry:** author is enthusiastic ("Sherry is awesome") despite only including three recipes; sherry is salty/dry/savory/acidic — great as a seasoning ingredient in small doses but tricky as a cocktail base since it can turn a drink thin. Must know sherry classification (bone dry to syrupy sweet) before using it, and sherry/vermouth should always be refrigerated since lighter styles like Manzanilla oxidize within about a week.
- **On Falernum (Rum/Tiki section):** homemade is strongly preferred over the standard commercial John D. Taylor's — likened to homemade vs. store-bought mayonnaise ("once you start making it on your own, you will never turn back").

## Core recipes captured (representative, full ratios in source)

All recipes are given with build technique, glassware, and garnish. Selected house-standard techniques flagged in the text:

- **Manhattan** (2.5 oz bourbon or rye / 1 oz Italian Rosso vermouth / 3 dashes Angostura, stir/strain, brandied cherries) — **must** offer a choice of bourbon or rye, and a choice of coupe or rocks glass.
- **Old Fashioned** (2.5 oz bourbon or rye / <.5 oz Demerara syrup 1:1 / 3 dashes Angostura / .5 dash aromatic bitters, short stir, large rock, lemon+orange twists) — same "must offer whiskey choice" rule.
- **Margarita** (2 oz blanco tequila / .75 oz Cointreau [Combier preferred] / .75 oz fresh lime / barspoon simple syrup 1:1) — must offer a half-rim of salt only (never full rim) and a choice of coupe or rocks glass (rocks always gets a short straw); "Cadillac" variant swaps Grand Marnier for Cointreau.
- **Mai Tai** — must use house-made orgeat or a premium bottled one (Tiki Adam Kolesar or Small Hands Foods); never use bottled almond syrup ("wrong viscosity").
- **Penicillin** (2 oz blended Scotch / .75 oz lemon / .35 oz ginger syrup / .35 oz honey syrup 2:1, float .25 oz peated Scotch, NO STRAW) — appears twice (once in the Scotch section, once in the 102-drink appendix) with slightly different ratios, both floating peated Scotch on top with no straw so the aroma hits first.
- **Sazerac** — absinthe-rinsed *chilled* small rocks glass; twist expressed and discarded (no garnish left in glass).
- **East Side / "Old Maid"** — flagged as functionally the same build on different ice sizes, and noted to work with "any white spirit," not just gin.
- Full recipe details for Gin, Vodka, Tequila/Mezcal, Bourbon, Rye, Irish, Scotch, White/Dark Rum, Tiki (Beachbum, Fog Cutter, Jet Pilot, Painkiller, Scorpion, Three Dots & a Dash, 1934 Zombie), Cognac (Champs-Élysées, Coffee Cocktail, Georgia Julep, De La Louisiane, Japanese Cocktail, Vieux Carré), Pisco (Frantic Atlantic, Pisco Punch, Pisco Sour), Apple Brandy (American Trilogy, Grandfather, Widow's Kiss, Applejack Rabbit, Jack Rose, Philadelphia Fish House Punch), and Sherry (Adonis, Bamboo, Sherry Cobbler) are captured verbatim in the PDF with exact mL/oz measures, build order, glass, and garnish.

## The "102 More Drinks" appendix — categories

- **Core Call Drinks (A–Z):** ~65 named classics (Airmail, Amaretto Sour, Americano Highball, Aperol Spritz, Arsenic & Old Lace, Aviation, Bee's Knees, Bellini, Bijou, Black Russian, Bloody Mary, Boulevardier, Bramble, Brandy Alexander, Brooklynite, Caipirinha, Champagne Cocktail, Chartreuse Swizzle, Chrysanthemum, Clover Club, Corn 'n Oil, Daiquiri, East Side, French 75, Gimlet, Gin Rickey, Godfather/Godmother, Grasshopper, Hanky Panky, Hemingway Daiquiri, Hot Toddy, Irish Coffee, Japanese Cocktail, Kir, Kir Royale, Last Word, Long Island Iced Tea, Mai Tai, Manhattan, Margarita, Martinez, Mimosa, Mint Julep, Mojito, Moscow Mule/Mules, Negroni, Old Cuban, Old Fashioned, Old Maid, Paloma, Penicillin, Pimm's Cup, Piña Colada, Pineapple Daiquiri, Queen's Park Swizzle, Ramos Gin Fizz, Rob Roy, Rome With A View, Rusty Nail, Sazerac, Sidecar, Singapore Sling, Southside, Southside Fizz, Stinger, Ti' Punch, Tom Collins, Vieux Carré, White Russian, and more).
- **Bloody Mary** is flagged "à la minute if you really have to," with a make-ahead shelf-stable spice-mix recipe given (700 ml Worcestershire, 300 ml hot sauce, 120 ml ground black pepper, 100 ml smoked salt, dosed at >.5 oz per cocktail) for volume service; Caesar variant splits tomato juice with Clamato.
- **Highballs (12):** Bay Breeze, Cape Codder, Cuba Libre, Fuzzy Navel, Greyhound, "Highball" (Scotch+ginger ale), Madras, Presbyterian, Screwdriver, Sea Breeze, Seven & Seven, Shirley Temple — each with a one-line personality tag in quotes (e.g., Greyhound = "Very Graceful," Sea Breeze = "Very cool & gusty").
- **Martinis:** Gin family (Gibson, Gin Martini, Dry Gin Martini, Dirty Gin Martini), Vodka family (Vodka Martini, Dry Vodka Martini, Dirty Vodka Martini), and "Other" flavored martinis (Apple, Chocolate, Espresso, French, Grapefruit, Lychee) — note that classic Martinis get orange bitters *only* when garnished with a twist, never when garnished with an olive.
- **Call Shots** ("make drinks, not judgments"): B-52, Blow Job, Irish Car Bomb, Kamikaze, Lemon Drop, Melon Ball, Mind Eraser, Sex on the Beach — each with a one-line nickname. Closing tip: for a guest who wants "a really great shot," recommend breaking a lesser-known 3-ingredient sour (Gold Rush, Brooklynite, Bee's Knees) into shot glasses instead of straight liquor shots — one sour recipe serves about three people and is framed as both more responsible and a better flavor introduction than standard shots.

## Training-progression takeaways

1. Master 6 recipes per major spirit (36 total core drinks) before moving to the 102-drink reference list.
2. Do not customize/expand the list until the full core has been mastered as a team.
3. Learn the "why" behind each spirit's typical treatment (shaken vs. stirred tendencies, cost sensitivity, dilution needs) rather than memorizing recipes as arbitrary formulas.
4. Use guest-service judgment (especially on "fake" cocktail names and neat vs. flavored requests) as an extension of technical training, not a separate skill.
5. The 102-drink appendix is a "keep behind the bar for reference" tool, not a memorization requirement.

<!-- ===== INÍCIO: lowders-178-modern-classics-list.md ===== -->

# Lowder's Easy List of 178 (Modern) Classics to Know

**Source file:** `LowdersEasyListof178ModernClassicsToKnow.xlsx` (single sheet: "Modern Classics", 208 rows, 5 columns)

## Structural overview

A flat, spreadsheet-format cocktail database with exactly **178 named cocktails**, organized under **30 category headers** (in column A, present only on each category's first row). Columns are:

| Column | Content |
|---|---|
| A — CATEGORY | Spirit + technique grouping (e.g. "Gin Shaken", "Rye Stirred", "TIKI"), populated only on the first row of each group |
| B — COCKTAIL NAME | Drink name |
| C — INGREDIENTS OZ | Full ingredient list in US fluid ounces, lettered a/b/c… |
| D — INGREDIENTS ML | Same ingredient list converted to milliliters |
| E — METHOD | Build technique / glass / ice / garnish, condensed into a single slash-separated line |

This is the spreadsheet counterpart to `LowderClassicRecipeTrainingManual.pdf` (2016, Chris Lowder / Indie Bartender) — same author, same title, same underlying recipe set (the PDF's "6-per-spirit core list" + its "102 More Drinks Everyone Should Know" appendix add up to exactly these 178 rows). The xlsx strips out all of the PDF's teaching prose/philosophy and keeps only the pure data table, in a format bartenders can filter/print/laminate directly, with both oz and ml columns for international use.

**Data quality note:** the source file has minor mojibake in curly apostrophes/accents (e.g. "Poet's Dream" renders as "Poet�s Dream", "Piña Colada" as "Pi�a Colada") — cosmetic only, does not affect ingredient or method data.

## Full category breakdown (30 categories, 178 drinks)

### Core spirit sections — 3 recipes each unless noted (these mirror the PDF's "6 drinks per spirit" teaching set, split into Shaken/Stirred sub-tables)

- **Gin Shaken (3):** Corpse Reviver No. 2, London Maid, White Lady
- **Gin Stirred (3):** Hanky Panky, Rolls Royce, Tuxedo No. 2
- **Vodka Stirred (3):** Poet's Dream (Vodka Version), Vesper Martini, Vodka Martini
- **Vodka Shaken (3):** Apple Martini, Cosmopolitan, Espresso Martini
- **Tequila Shaken (3):** El Diablo, Infante, Tommy's Margarita
- **Tequila Stirred (3):** Augie March, Oaxacan Old Fashioned, Rosita
- **Bourbon Shaken (3):** Gold Rush, Kentucky Buck, Paper Plane
- **Bourbon Stirred (3):** Fancy Free, Grandfather, Preakness Cocktail
- **Rye Shaken (3):** New York Sour, Ward Eight, Whiskey Smash
- **Rye Stirred (3):** American Trilogy, Old Pal, Red Hook
- **Irish Shaken (3):** Castle to Castle, Irish Breakfast, Wild Eyed Rose
- **Irish Stirred (3):** Blackthorn, Improved Whiskey Cocktail, Tipperary Cocktail
- **Scotch Stirred (3):** Bobby Burns, Prince Edward, Tattletale
- **Scotch Shaken (3):** Mamie Taylor, Morning Glory Fizz, Penicillin
- **Rum Stirred (3):** Chet Baker, Dominicana, El Presidente
- **White Rum Shaken (3):** Airmail, Daisy de Santiago, Mary Pickford
- **Dark Rum Shaken (3):** Brooklynite, Jungle Bird, Royal Bermuda Yacht Club
- **TIKI (7):** Beachbum, Fog Cutter, Jet Pilot, Painkiller, Scorpion, Three Dots & A Dash, Zombie (1934)
- **Cognac Shaken (3):** Champs-Élysées, Coffee Cocktail, Georgia Julep
- **Cognac Stirred (3):** De La Louisiane, Japanese Cocktail, Vieux Carré
- **Pisco Cocktails (3):** Frantic Atlantic, Pisco Punch, Pisco Sour
- **Apple Brandy Stirred (3):** American Trilogy, Grandfather, Widow's Kiss
- **Apple Brandy Shaken (3):** Applejack Rabbit, Jack Rose, Philadelphia Fish House Punch
- **Sherry Cocktails (3):** Adonis, Bamboo, Sherry Cobbler

*(Subtotal: 76 core recipes)*

### Reference/appendix sections (mirrors the PDF's "102 More Drinks Everyone Should Know")

- **Core Call Drinks (69):** Airmail, Amaretto Sour, Americano Highball, Aperol Spritz, Arsenic & Old Lace, Aviation, Bee's Knees, Bellini, Bijou, Black Russian, Bloody Mary ("à la minute if you really have to"), Boulevardier, Bramble, Brandy Alexander, Brooklynite, Caipirinha, Champagne Cocktail, Chartreuse Swizzle, Chrysanthemum, Clover Club, Corn 'n Oil, Daiquiri, East Side, French 75, Gimlet, Gin Rickey, Godfather/Godmother, Grasshopper, Hanky Panky, Hemingway Daiquiri, Hot Toddy, Irish Coffee, Japanese Cocktail, Kir, Kir Royale, Last Word, Long Island Iced Tea, Mai Tai, Manhattan, Margarita, Martinez, Mimosa, Mint Julep, Mojito, Moscow Mule (and ALL Mules), Negroni, Old Cuban, Old Fashioned, Old Maid aka London Maid, Paloma, Penicillin, Pimm's Cup, Piña Colada, Pineapple Daiquiri, Queen's Park Swizzle, Ramos Gin Fizz, Rob Roy, Rome With A View, Rusty Nail, Sazerac, Sidecar, Singapore Sling, Southside, Southside Fizz, Stinger, Ti' Punch, Tom Collins, Vieux Carré, White Russian
- **Highballs (12):** Bay Breeze, Cape Codder, Cuba Libre, Fuzzy Navel, Greyhound, "Highball" (Scotch + ginger ale), Madras, Presbyterian, Screwdriver, Sea Breeze, Seven & Seven, Shirley Temple
- **Martini — Gin (4):** Gibson, Gin Martini, Dry Gin Martini, Dirty Gin Martini
- **Martini — Vodka (3):** Vodka Martini, Dry Vodka Martini, Dirty Vodka Martini
- **Other [flavored Martinis] (6):** Apple Martini, Chocolate Martini, Espresso Martini, French Martini, Grapefruit Martini, Lychee Martini
- **Call Shots (8):** B-52, Blow Job, Irish Car Bomb, Kamikaze, Lemon Drop, Melon Ball, Mind Eraser, Sex on the Beach

*(Subtotal: 102 reference recipes — matching the PDF's appendix title exactly)*

**76 + 102 = 178**, confirming the spreadsheet is a complete, non-overlapping-in-count merge of the PDF's core teaching list and its extended reference appendix (a few names, like Penicillin, Manhattan-family drinks, and "American Trilogy"/"Grandfather", legitimately appear in both a core spirit section and the Core Call Drinks list, since they qualify under more than one spirit heading — e.g., American Trilogy and Grandfather are shared between Rye Stirred and Apple Brandy Stirred).

## Data format detail (per recipe)

Every recipe row follows the same schema, e.g. for **Corpse Reviver No. 2**:
- Ingredients (oz): .75 oz. London Dry Gin / .75 oz. Cointreau / .75 oz. Cocchi Americano / .75 oz. Fresh Lemon
- Ingredients (ml): 25ml London Dry Gin / 25ml Cointreau / 25ml Cocchi Americano / 25ml Fresh Lemon
- Method: "Shake/fine strain/absinthe-rinsed coupe/no garnish"

The Method column consistently encodes, in fixed order: mixing technique (shake/stir/build/swizzle/muddle), straining, glassware, ice type, and garnish — enabling the sheet to be read as a compact recipe card for each of the 178 drinks without needing prose explanation.

## Practical use

This file functions as the pure "answer key" recipe database referenced by the training manual — suited for printing as a laminated bar reference, importing into a POS/recipe app, or filtering by category/spirit for staff study sheets, since it carries no narrative content, only structured ingredient and build data for all 178 drinks.

<!-- ===== INÍCIO: mad-dog-staff-manual.md ===== -->

# The Mad Dog — Staff Manual

**Source file:** `Copyofmaddogstaffmanual.pdf` (14 pages)
**Title page:** "The Mad Dog — what we do to make our guests happy, OR the staff manual" — September 2020, The Mad Dog Social Club

## Structural overview

A short, plainspoken staff manual (not a recipe book) for a single cocktail bar called The Mad Dog. It has no chapters/numbering; it reads as a continuous set of policy sections followed by an appendix. Rough structure:

1. Welcome / mission statement and core philosophy
2. List of specific staff responsibilities
3. Uniform and cleanliness
4. Punctuality
5. Dropbox (internal file sharing) and Rota (shift schedule)
6. Breaks/pauses
7. Complaints handling
8. Staff visiting as guests / serving friends & regulars
9. Music
10. Age and guest behavior (no minors, overservice)
11. "Relations with guests" — floor etiquette
12. "Service routine" — the numbered 7-step guest journey
13. "Features that make the difference between a poor job and a good job"
14. Opening/closing/cleaning procedures pointer (to a separate "Bar Book")
15. General notes of service and drink preparation (pour sizes, build order)
16. "Always simple | Never basic" philosophy statement
17. Reservations and reception management
18. Mad Dog Policy block: staff drinks, cigarettes, keep busy, phones, take-away, payment methods, outside food
19. Opening hours, shift end routine, dogs policy, behavior outside the venue
20. Useful information (emergency password, phone numbers, nearby transport, recommended bars/restaurants)
21. Conclusions
22. Appendix: "Supplements for training" — deeper reservation management rules and a "Floor: tips & tricks" section on active vs. reactive work, "pockets of time," priority levels, "never empty-handed," and pacing

## Core philosophy

- **Mission statement (bolded as the fundamental task):** "Provide professional, informed, courteous service to every guest at any time."
- Deliberately uses "guest" instead of "customer" — guests are to be welcomed as into one's own home.
- No hard distinction between "bartender" and "waiter" — both floor and bar roles are expected to reach a shared skill level; the manual uses "bartender" for both for simplicity.
- The manual teaches "our way," not generic bartending — it explicitly is not trying to teach the job from scratch, and invites feedback at monthly staff meetings.
- Explicitly separates *technique* (teachable) from *personality* (not teachable) — staff should be outgoing without being intrusive, friendly, active, energetic, and above all proactive.
- Signature philosophy line: **"Always simple | Never basic."** Meaning: focus on what matters, avoid ostentation. Example given: a Gin & Tonic — a highball, good gin, good tonic, clear ice, and a stirrer — "simple but not at all basic... this is true elegance, the rest is ostentation."
- "A complaint is a challenge to do our best, not a lost battle." No guest should ever leave with a bad experience.
- "The real host treats the worst customer like the woman in his life."

## Staff responsibilities (verbatim list)

- Maintain personal cleanliness, bar cleanliness, and personal spaces (locker/changing room)
- Check shifts regularly and be flexible when required
- Follow opening, closing and cleaning procedures as specified (in a separate "Bar Book")
- Interact with guests, be attentive and proactive to their needs
- Serve drinks always following house standards and recipes
- Be accountable for payments made
- Inform the manager of drops in standards, missing references, or complaints
- Follow rules on doses served and opening hours
- Do cleaning/bar-backing when needed
- Be fully responsible for one's section and aware of each guest's situation (who has had a drink, who is waiting, etc.)

## House standards & operations

**Uniform:** Not provided by the venue; staff buy their own, subject to manager approval. Comfortable but not scruffy; long-sleeved shirt recommended for men. Wash hands on arrival and use "amuchina" (hand sanitizer) every time money is touched if bartending.

**Punctuality:** Treated as the first sign of professionalism; lateness must be reported immediately to the shift manager.

**Dropbox:** Used to share menu, shifts, training docs, etc. — staff must register and share their email with the manager.

**Rota** (shift schedule): distributed in print and via Dropbox; days-off requests go through written communication (WhatsApp) to the manager.

**Breaks:** Dinner break ~45 minutes; evening breaks possible if coverage is arranged with a colleague. On returning from a cigarette break: wash hands and take a mint.

**Complaints:** Inform the manager immediately; the manager has authority to comp drinks etc. If the manager is absent, side with the guest and ensure no one leaves unsatisfied.

**Staff as guests:** 50% staff discount for the staff member plus two guests on days off; must follow the same rules as regular guests (don't clear tables, don't go behind the bar, don't self-serve).

**Music:** Controlled by the manager via tablet; staff can suggest playlist ideas. Staff may be asked to control the amplifier during live concerts.

**Age/behavior policy:** No minors under any circumstance. Overserved guests: stop serving them, discreetly and always ultimately at the manager's discretion (may be escorted out in exceptional cases). One customer in the bathroom at a time.

### Relations with guests (floor etiquette)

- Welcome guests confidently, make eye contact, smile, say good evening
- Engage solo guests at the counter — many prefer the bar precisely for the interaction; memorize preferences for return visits
- **Acknowledge any newly arrived guest within 60 seconds**, even when slammed, so they don't feel ignored
- When surrounded at the counter, take 2-3 orders simultaneously to find overlaps and speed service; suggest a round of beers/G&Ts if truly slammed
- Small but important attentions: change a wet coaster/towel, rearrange a table while guests are at the toilet/smoking, remove garnishes a drunk guest has discarded
- If a guest groans about a drink, ask what's wrong and fix it — don't let dissatisfaction go unaddressed
- Style of service: **polite but not formal, elegant but relaxed, friendly but not intrusive**
- Replace spilled drinks free of charge, no questions asked

### The 7-step service routine

1. **Welcome all guests within 30 seconds of arrival** — even a quick hello if busy
2. **Bring water while briefly explaining the menu** — mention any 86'd items, note classics are available; bring an English menu for non-Italian guests
3. **Take the order and write it down** — read the guest (how they speak, what they want), adapt vocabulary/register; don't be afraid to suggest premium spirits; never make a guest feel unsophisticated for a request — always offer alternatives instead of a flat "we don't have that"; crouch to eye level when guests are seated (attributed to Sasha Petraske); for large groups, repeat the order back; warn of >20 minute waits; **no drink is made without a written order**
4. **Serve drinks and do the checkback** — always with a coaster, changed each drink; no fixed serving order to the table; checkback should ask specifically ("how are your drinks? are they to your liking?") rather than generic "is everything okay?"; if a drink is disliked, replace it without discussion, with a smile, never make the guest feel judged; use the water refill as a natural excuse to return to the table
5. **Clear the table** — remove the empty glass with its coaster once finished, wait a few minutes then offer another drink; water bottle kept topped up
6. **Take payment** — verify all drinks are on the bill, present to the correct table, thank guests
7. **Farewell** — first and last impressions matter most; sincerely thank departing guests

### "What separates a poor job from a good job" (verbatim list)

- Always work at the same pace (80% of maximum = consistency + sufficient speed) on both slow and busy nights
- Always decorate the drink as intended
- Keep the bar top clean even on busy nights
- Always ask if guests want another drink
- Recognize regulars and use their names
- Communicate proactively when something is about to run out
- Never waste time — stay active even while chatting with guests
- Never touch the rim of glasses — hold from the lowest point
- Read guest needs in advance (e.g., offer to point out the smoking room if they stand without their jacket)
- Escort guests to services when possible
- Good service anticipates needs seconds in advance and is "invisible"

## Drink preparation standards

- Style: simple, aimed at showcasing quality raw ingredients, in line with "always simple, never basic"
- **All drinks are tasted with tasting straws and served with a metal straw**
- **Pour sizes:** Vermouth, aperitifs, liqueurs, amari = 75 ml unless otherwise specified; wine = 150 ml; Port and Sherry = 100 ml; all other spirits = 50 ml
- Recipes are written in mL
- **No free pouring or "throwing"** (flair/inconsistent techniques) — precision only
- Bottles always held label-facing the guest
- Preference for classics/twists over full "do-it-yourself" bespoke creations — "if these drinks have stood the test of time, there must be a reason"; any bespoke drink should use low-turnover stock
- All cocktails made by every bartender to the same specs/recipe/garnish for consistency
- Soda and beers poured in front of the guest
- Drinks waiting too long on the pass go into the freezer
- Ask for help rather than let service quality slip when overwhelmed; long orders split between two bartenders
- **Drink-making routine:** prep shaker and garnish first (garnish BEFORE making the drink); build order = Bitters → Juices → Modifiers (liqueurs, syrups) → Base spirit (cheapest ingredient first to minimize waste on a mistake); double shake/double stir standard; pour drinks starting with those served over ice; maximize two-handed multitasking during busy periods

## Reservations & floor management

- Reservations taken by phone/email: name, time, headcount, and (for live music nights) whether they want the music room. Reservation held 20 minutes past the booked time before reassignment.
- Marked at start of evening on the reservation sheet/agenda, unmarked on arrival, tracked with a special card.
- Appendix expands with granular rules: sofa/table combos on holidays (2-hour cap), large groups (10+) arrival/departure windows, staggering busy-day bookings, seating priority (best tables to inconvenienced regulars first on weekdays; fill to capacity on holidays), walk-ins seated on tables reserved 2+ hours out to generate "double shifts," releasing tables 15 minutes before the next booking to allow cleaning, confirming reservations tactfully before turning away queuing walk-ins, and placing a "reserved" card immediately when a table is about to turn over.

## Floor tips & tricks (appendix — "active vs. reactive work")

- Floor work is *reactive* (respond to unpredictable guest events); bar work is *active* (constant, even-paced production at 80% speed). The floor "filters" unpredictability so the bar can stay steady.
- **"Pockets of time":** moments where the guest can wait (a little) vs. moments where they cannot. Guest arrival and menu delivery cannot wait; water delivery and order-taking (up to 30s) can be slightly delayed; once an order is placed, the wait shifts to the bartender, freeing the server; once the bell rings, drink delivery is immediately top priority (perishable — put in freezer if excessively delayed).
- **Priority-level table** (guest arrival = 3, bring menu = 3, bring water = 1, take order = 2, deliver order = 3, take payment = 2, clear table w/ no one waiting = 1, clear table w/ someone waiting = 2, satisfy a colleague's request = 2).
- **"Never empty-handed":** always carry something useful in each direction between points (e.g., bring drinks to table 1, water to table 2, menu to table 3 on the way out; clear table 4 on the way back).
- **Pacing:** avoid batching two orders together if possible since ~30 seconds matters more to the bartender than to waiting guests; a floor lead can deliberately accelerate table ordering ahead of a known big table (e.g., 10:30pm) to get the bar "in rhythm" beforehand, or deliberately slow/dilute incoming orders if the bartender is already overwhelmed.

## Operational policies

- Staff alcohol only with manager authorization ("staff shot"); staff must finish closing duties and confirm they're not needed before sitting down; standard after-work drink is a beer or G&T.
- Smoking only on the fire escape, with coverage arranged and hands washed + mint afterward; no cigarette butts on the stairs.
- Staff must always be doing something — ask for a task if idle.
- Phone use during service minimized and kept out of guest sight.
- No take-away drinks served.
- No checks or foreign currency accepted as payment.
- No outside food allowed in the room.
- **Hours:** Tuesday–Saturday, 21:00–02:00. Closing sequence: 1:45 last call announced; 2:00 drink production stops, music/lights fade up gradually; 2:30 guests asked to finish drinks (if authorized by manager), then close-out checks performed.
- Dogs welcome on a leash.
- Staff represent the venue 24/7, even off-duty, including as guests of other bars.
- Emergency evacuation password: "Carlina."
- Local reference info: Mad Dog phone, taxi number, nearest station (Porta Nuova, 20 min walk), nearest taxi stand (Piazza Vittorio, 10 min), and lists of neighboring recommended wine bars, cocktail bars, daytime bars, and restaurants (all within ~15 minutes on foot) — used for guiding guests when the Mad Dog can't accommodate them or for staff recommendations.

## Closing statement

The manual explicitly disclaims being an exhaustive "vademecum" — its goal is to instill the "Mad Dog mentality" (values + working style) so that staff can use common sense in situations not directly covered. Final line: "Be professional, courteous, smile and enjoy doing it: after all, our industry is also the entertainment industry."

<!-- ===== FIM: mad-dog-staff-manual.md ===== -->

---

<!-- ===== INÍCIO: match-bar-group-bartenders-manual.md ===== -->

# The Match Bar Group Bartender's Manual

**Author:** Kevin Armstrong (Group Bars Manager / Group Head Bartender), with contributions from Dale DeGroff, JD, Sasha Petraske, and other Match Bar Group senior staff
**Edition referenced:** 2nd March 08 Draft V6
**Scope:** Internal operations, training and standards manual for The Match Bar Group — a London bar group whose sites include Match EC1, Match Bar (W1), Sosho, The Player, Milk & Honey, Trailer Happiness and Harlem.

## Structural overview

The manual runs to 35 numbered sections (roughly 250 printed pages) organized into five broad movements:

1. **Ethos & service philosophy** (§1–4): Introduction; "The Zen of Bartending" (Dale DeGroff on service); Drinks ethos; Service standards including JD's "20 commandments."
2. **Organisational structure & career ladder** (§5–9): Bar Back → Junior Bartender → Bartender → Senior Bartender job profiles, pay bands, and requirements; Head Bartender/Manager notes; Training (First Tuesday program); Safety; Personal presentation/hygiene.
3. **Craft fundamentals** (§10–19): Equipment and tool usage; Glassware; Drink-creation techniques (shaking, stirring, building, rolling, throwing); Free pouring vs. jiggering; Tasting; Practical/skill requirements (economy of motion, formulation of the round); "The next level" (balance, dilution, presentation, back-bar design); Ice; Basic ingredients — fruit, then liquids/juices/premixes.
4. **Standards & recipes** (§20–23): Standards (station set-up, opening/closing checklists, par levels, bill service, cleaning rotas); Useful shift information; **Dale's 120 classics** (alphabetical classic-cocktail reference); **Match Bar Group Recipes & Originals** (house signature drinks).
5. **Category knowledge & appendices** (§24–35): Beer; Wine; Champagne & sparkling wine; Site specifics (addresses/timelines for each bar); The Law; How to win drinks competitions; Bars we respect; Match facts; Alumni; Glossary; Appendix (shot-count sheet).

---

## 1. Ethos and service philosophy

The manual opens with an H.L. Mencken quote (1911) elevating bartending to a dignified trade, then Dale DeGroff's essay "The Zen of Bartending," which frames the bar (unlike the dining room) as **shared space that the bartender, not the guest, sets the tone for** — guests get latitude a server in a restaurant would never extend them, and the bartender's job is to read mood, offer discreet theatre (e.g. flaming citrus), and "turn difficult guests into friends."

**JD's "three most important qualities"**: Knowledge, Knowledge, and Personality — in that order; personality is worthless without knowledge.

**JD's 20 commandments** (condensed): make eye contact; offer a cocktail menu; put down coasters (signals "this guest is being served"); offer a sincere greeting; know everything about the product range; upsell knowledgeably (never fake enthusiasm); be efficient (take order → make drinks → sell drinks, never chat mid-prep); be organised (juggle 2 orders, not more); be technically proficient; prioritize a round (long iced drinks first, martinis last, since they warm/deteriorate fastest); secure payment immediately; check back on guests; be thoughtful/proactive (pour wine for guests rather than let them spill); offer food; keep the bar clean (always handle glasses by the base); work as a team; offer a sincere farewell; be as well-presented as the drinks; never serve the drunk/unruly/underage; be helpful, cool and take pride.

**Cutting off a guest** (§4.10) — an escalation ladder: offer water/cool-down time → "busy, busy, busy" avoidance technique → enlist a soberer friend (empower them with free water) → propose food/sit-down → suggest 5 minutes outside → call the manager → security (SIA-certified only) as last resort → police if needed. Staff must never lose their temper, and any decision by a manager must be backed by the whole team even if disputed.

**Dispense service** (§4.11): the strongest/fastest bartender should run dispense (not the weakest, as many groups do), and pre-mixing high-volume drinks (e.g. 20 Cosmopolitans) for dispense-only service is acceptable even though it wouldn't be for guests watching at the bar.

---

## 2. Career ladder and training

Each level (Bar Back, Junior Bartender, Bartender, Senior Bartender) has a full job profile: department, line manager, salary band (era-specific, e.g. Bar Back £5.52–£6/hr rising to Senior Bartender £8–£8.50/hr), general responsibilities (presentable, polite, uphold standards, protect equipment, dedication to training) and role-specific duties that scale in depth — e.g. a Bar Back must know ingredient locations and juice/syrup recipes but not full cocktail specs; a Bartender must be "fully proficient" in all recipes and able to mentor Juniors.

**Becoming a Senior Bartender** is an 8–12 month process requiring, among other things:
- A recitable company-ethos statement
- A written Senior Bartender exam (ethos, spirits production, cocktails, wine, beer, champagne, history)
- A practical exam (round production, both-hand pouring, economy of motion, cleanliness, final drink quality)
- 3 months of pour-testing at **≥90% accuracy** (95% required for Head Bartenders)
- 2 full weeks working at Sosho and 2 full weeks at Milk & Honey (to absorb Sasha Petraske's dilution/ice methodology)
- 4 weeks of GP (stock/budget) training plus a GP exam
- 1 week cash-up training
- Attendance at Dale DeGroff's introductory seminar
- 7 of 8 "First Tuesday" sessions with a >60% pass rate
- Site-specific operational knowledge and a final interview with JD
- Optional add-ons: WSET Spirits/Wine, licensing course, first aid, an individual research project

**First Tuesday program** (§7): the core training institution — a compulsory, 2-hour (3–5pm) monthly session at The Player, one drinks category per month (e.g. Tequila with Tom Estes, Bourbon with Mark Ridgewell), no topic repeated within a year, 11 sessions/year (December reserved for the end-of-year exam). Each session opens with a test on **last month's** material (forcing spaced repetition), followed by a guided tasting. Lateness = no admission; persistent lateness = disciplinary action. Sample exam questions are reproduced at Entry, Bartender, and Senior Bartender difficulty (e.g. "Name the 6 Classic Malts of Scotland," "How do you make a Mary Pickford?", "What is 'Black Tot Day'?").

**Safety (§8)**: knife discipline; the flat-top bar spoon's welded end is flagged as a genuine injury risk if used to muddle (two documented hand-piercing incidents — use a muddler); Boston glass tempered glass can still fracture under muddling pressure; broken glass must never enter regular bins; any hint of glass or blood contamination voids an entire ice well immediately, no exceptions.

---

## 3. Equipment and technique

### Core tools (§10)
- **Boston glass** (16oz tapered mixing glass): the primary vessel. Technique notes — build the "eye line" (learn how full the glass needs to be for each glass size) by practicing with water; never ice the glass first (distorts eye line, starts diluting while you're distracted); add cheapest ingredients first (limits loss on a mistake).
- **Boston can** (~30oz): forms the seal with the glass ("Boston shaker"/shaker set); can be "snapped" directly onto an ice well as a scoop-and-seal shortcut; doubles as a small-batch syrup vessel; the "smelly can" fix is a shake of lemon juice, water and a wedge with ice.
- **Toby shaker**: smaller (~16oz), used for one-handed shaking and cap-shaking in Catalina/sling glasses; over-shakes easily.
- **Cobbler (3-piece) shaker**: not used at Match, but common at Milk & Honey; built-in strainer.
- **Flat-top bar spoon** (Bonzer): stirring, layering (pour down the twisted stem over the disc), a ~5ml ladle measure — **never** for muddling (safety risk).
- **Red-top bar spoon**: alternative with more flex, usable for hand-cracking ice.
- **Hawthorn strainer**: used only with the Boston can; spring tension is adjustable via a small metal tab for a tighter/looser pour.
- **Julep strainer**: for stirred/straight-liquor drinks poured from the Boston glass; historically was drunk *through*, not just used to strain.
- **Tea/fine strainer**: used sparingly — only when Hawthorn/Julep can't catch seeds or poor ice; removes the aerated bubbles that make a freshly shaken drink "feel alive" on the first sip (a deliberate trade-off, not a default step).
- **Muddler**: used to release flavor that shaking/hand-squeezing can't (e.g. limes in a Caipirinha); avoid over-bashing citrus skins (releases bitter oils).
- **Canelle knife / citrus zester**: rind extraction for garnish, e.g. citrus knots.
- **Speed pourer** (Spill Stop brand preferred): only fitted to bottles used daily or on the cocktail menu; replace when rusted (taints product) or leaking.
- **Lewis/Louis bag**: canvas bag + muddler/rolling pin for dry, slow-melting crushed ice (Mint Juleps, Mojitos).
- Standard straw/stirrer set: 6" sip straw, 8"/11" jumbo clear straws, 6" plastic ball stirrer (Ascot Wholesale) — no bendy or coloured straws except M&H's metal straws.

### Glassware (§11)
17 house glass types listed (Inverness/Endeavour rocks small/large, Catalina tall cooler ["dangerous"], Squall/Hurricane, Signature martini/Elegance Coupette, Kentucky martini, Signature wine/flute, Islandi/American whiskey shot, Trigger/Icon Tankard, Brandy Balloon, Weiss goblet, Volcano bowl). Rules: match glass shape/volume to the recipe (adjust the recipe, not the glass, when they mismatch); always serve at correct temperature (frozen for stirred/no-ice drinks, chilled otherwise); stock glassware proportionally to actual usage (mostly highballs, not martini glasses); handle stemware by the base — most breakage happens during polishing.

### Drink-creation techniques (§12)
- **Shaking**: as much ice as possible, firmly, **7–10 seconds** (standardized to the house Hoshizaki ice cube). Grip the shaker so the Boston can's seam faces the customer (so a failed seal sprays the bartender, not the guest). Never let a shaken drink sit — serve immediately. Technique for separating the can/glass: left hand grips the straight seam, right palm strikes at ~135° from the index finger, sharply.
- **Cap shaking**: quick technique using the can/Toby shaker as a lid over a glass already built — house example is the Match Spring Punch.
- **Stirring**: **15–20 seconds** in a fully-iced Boston glass, red-top spoon preferred, motion from the wrist only; hold the glass by its base (not the bowl) to avoid warming it; a Thermos or wine cooler used as the mixing vessel was measured (Bar 05 "Soulshakers" test) to produce Martinis **3–5°F colder** than a standard Boston glass.
- **Rule of thumb**: any drink with fresh juice is shaken; pure liquor/liqueur drinks are stirred; **exception — Bloody Mary/tomato-juice drinks are rolled, never shaken** (shaking breaks tomato juice's molecular structure and thins its texture).
- **Building**: ingredients mixed directly in the serve glass (highballs like Dark & Stormy); never build a cocktail directly into a martini glass.
- **Rolling**: gentle wave-motion transfer between two vessels — used for Bloody Marys and other texture-sensitive juices.
- **Throwing**: aerating a stirred-style drink (Martini, Manhattan) by pouring between two Hawthorn-capped cans held at arm's length, waterfall-style.
- **Blue Blazer**: flaming spirit poured between two long-handled heatproof vessels — flagged as high-risk showmanship, "you almost certainly will never need it."

### Free pouring vs. jiggering (§13)
House rule: if unsure of any recipe, always jigger first to learn the taste, then graduate to free pouring. Jiggering is actually faster than two-handed free pouring only when using one hand or pouring from a jug/unspouted bottle. The house teaches **counting to 5 for a 25ml pour** (so every count = 5ml, letting a 7-count = 35ml etc.) rather than the more common 4-count, because it's evenly divisible. All staff are expected to master **two-handed free pouring** and must pass a **free-pour exam** to progress levels; a "cut" (how you end a pour) should be understated and graceful, not showy — the pop/bounce pour (a quick downward jerk that creates a temporary air lock) is named as the most effective way to stop a pour cleanly.

### Practical skills (§15)
- **Economy of motion**: batch repeated ingredients across a round (e.g. handle lime juice once for 3 drinks, not three times).
- **Knowing the specs / "the window"**: house recipes are built to the widest common denominator of taste — not too sweet/sour/strong — so a drink is unlikely to be sent back while still showcasing the base spirit.
- **Formulation of the round**: sequence drink production so everything finishes together, accounting for how fast each drink deteriorates (iced long drinks first, martinis/stirred drinks last, served immediately since they're "the most volatile").
- Presentation should never slip under pressure — quoting Dale DeGroff: "I say there's always time" for garnish.

---

## 4. Balance, dilution and ice (the house "theory")

**Kevin on Balance** (§16.2) — the manual's clearest statement of craft philosophy, using the **Daiquiri** as the reference case:

> House Daiquiri spec: **50ml Havana Blanco : 15ml fresh lime juice : 15ml simple syrup**

A 5ml swing in lime vs. sugar (10ml relative deficit) is enough to tip a "harmonious" drink into an unpleasantly sour one — and most customers can't articulate why a drink is off, they just won't reorder it (sometimes for 6 months). Because the house sugar syrup is made 1:1 (see below), it balances almost exactly with equal parts fresh lemon/lime — simplifying recipe design versus higher-brix commercial syrups. Rules of thumb given: total sweet+sour should never exceed the spirit volume (50ml spirit balances with roughly 10–25ml sour + 10–25ml sweet, more than that and strength disappears); non-simple-syrup sweeteners (honey, agave, Triple Sec) don't balance 1:1 and need adjustment; sugar is also used to tame tannin (e.g. in a New York Sour) and to cut bitterness/heat.

**Dilution** (§16.3, crediting Sasha Petraske of Milk & Honey): dilution has two dimensions — the water content at time of serving, and the *rate* of further dilution once on ice. Sasha's rule: better to serve slightly under-chilled with correct water content than perfectly cold but over-diluted. Full-glass ice service is partly about this — more ice = slower ongoing dilution after the drink reaches the table. M&H (not Match generally) uses hand-cracked, super-frozen slab ice for the same reason.

**Ice** (§17): Match uses **Hoshizaki** machines exclusively, producing large cubes at **28mm × 28mm × 32mm** — chosen because that size balances shaking, stirring, dilution rate and glass fit. Governing factors for ice quality: purity/water quality (clarity), cube size/surface-area-to-volume ratio (bigger cubes melt slower), and density/temperature (denser, colder ice = stronger bonds = slower melt; ice density ≈0.93 vs water's 1.0). House rules: fill glasses to the brim with ice unless asked otherwise; bartenders' hands never touch service ice (except bespoke hand-cracked prep); never reuse ice; any contamination (glass, blood, even a customer spitting in the well) means immediate full disposal.

---

## 5. Ingredients and house recipes for mixes

**Juice policy** (§19.1): lemon and lime are **always** squeezed fresh on-site daily and sieved; other juices (orange, grapefruit, cranberry, pineapple, apple) may be bought in from trusted suppliers (e.g. Eager) when fresh production isn't practical/cost-effective. Old and new juice batches are never combined.

**Standardized syrup/mix recipes** (§19.5–19.6) — the manual's explicit "you will be tested on these":

| Item | Ratio / recipe |
|---|---|
| **Sugar (simple) syrup** | 1:1 caster sugar : cold water (cold water only — warm water spoils faster and tastes worse) |
| **Honey syrup** | 2:1 honey : water (2 jars honey @340g + 340ml cold water per batch) |
| **Brown sugar syrup** | 1:1 brown sugar : cold water; unstable, lasts only 2–3 days before fermenting ("tastes like beer" — bad news for Caipirinhas) |
| **Tea syrup** | 1:1 brewed tea : sugar; brew fresh leaves (never bags) under 3 minutes to avoid tannin; only syrup made with hot water; keeps only a few days refrigerated |
| **Egg white mix** | 6–8 fresh egg whites + 50–75ml chilled water, lightly beaten to thin for pouring |
| **Match Spring Punch (MSP) mix** | 3.4L OJ/grapefruit carton base + 2 bottles Wyborowa vodka + 560ml crème de cassis + 560ml crème de framboise + 560ml sugar syrup |
| **Zombie mix (5 rums)** | 3.4L juice carton base + 1 bottle each: Goslings, Havana Blanco, Bacardi 8, Wood's 100, Doorly's 5yr |
| **Iced Tea mix** | 3.4L juice carton base + 1 bottle each: Wyborowa vodka, Havana Blanco, Tanqueray gin, Gran Centenario Plata tequila, Triple Sec |

A vitamin-C tablet can extend a syrup's shelf life by up to 5 days. Fresh-juice/puree shelf-life notes are itemized in detail: lemons/limes 7–14 days unrefrigerated for juicing, 10–21 days refrigerated for garnish use, garnish-cut fruit only ~24 hours; mint 4 days at 4–10°C, discolored leaves discarded; soft berries 2–4 days; stone fruit 2–3 days (few more chilled); pineapple/apples don't survive overnight once cut; olives/cherries/onions keep weeks to months in their brine.

---

## 6. Standards (§20) — daily operating procedure

- **Station set-up**: arrange the ice well/speed rail/shelving to minimize bartender movement; frequently-used items (napkins, mint for Mojitos) must be within arm's reach; station layout should evolve with sales trends (e.g. swap cognac for rum in the rail if cognac outsells it).
- **Opening checklist**: verify prior close-down, check refrigeration/electrical equipment, confirm deliveries, chill stock, taste-check all carried-over juices/purees (discard rather than "marry up" old with new), squeeze juices, cut garnish, fully stock stations, check float, brief staff on bookings/pre-orders.
- **Closing checklist**: marry up part-bottles, clear/store fruit, dispose of all ice (never held overnight), full station and peanut-rail deep clean (fruit-fly hazard), clean all bar tools, restock wine/beer/champagne for maximum next-day chill, cash up, log handover communication.
- **Par levels**: explained via a worked vodka-sales example (5-7-5-6-8-6-7-5-9-7 bottles/week) showing why par should sit above the historical average (e.g. 10–12, not 7) to absorb spikes without over-stocking.
- **Bill service**: totals hidden under a fold or business card; discretionary service charge applies to table service only; bar-service charge only added above £100; disputes go to the Head Bartender/manager.
- **Staff drink policy**: one free house beer or house wine per shift, post-shift only; further drinks at a set discount (e.g. 25%); drinking on shift is a dismissible offense (tasting a shaken cocktail excepted).
- **Cleaning rota**: daily items folded into open/close; weekly/monthly items (glass-washer salting, fridge deep-cleans, back-bar polishing) scheduled ahead of the busiest nights.

---

## 7. Recipe sections

### Dale's 120 classics (§22)
An alphabetical reference of historic classic cocktails, each with a short provenance note and an exact recipe. Representative entries with exact specs:

- **Alexander** — 25ml gin or brandy, 20ml dark cacao, 40ml heavy cream, shaken, nutmeg garnish.
- **Aviation** — 40ml gin, 25ml maraschino liqueur, 20ml lemon juice, shaken.
- **Bacardi Cocktail** — 40ml Bacardi Light, 20ml lemon juice, 20ml simple syrup, 5ml grenadine (notably: legally, per a 1936 NY Supreme Court ruling, only Bacardi rum may be used).
- **Bee's Knees** — 40ml gin, 25ml honey syrup, 20ml lemon juice.
- **Bellini** — 50ml white peach puree built first, topped gently with 90ml prosecco (no stirring — preserves effervescence).
- **Bloody Mary** — 50ml vodka, 2 dashes Worcestershire, 4 dashes Tabasco, salt/pepper, 5ml lemon, 120ml tomato juice; rolled, never shaken.
- **Caipirinha** — 50ml cachaça, 25ml brown sugar syrup (or a cube), half a lime muddled.
- **Cosmopolitan** — 40ml citrus vodka, 20ml Cointreau, 10ml lime juice, 15ml cranberry juice; history traced through Ocean Spray's 1960s "Harpoon" promotion, the Odeon (NYC) and Fog City Diner (SF).
- **Old Fashioned**, **Manhattan**, **Margarita**, **Martini** (extra dry / dirty variants), **Negroni**, **Mint Julep**, **Moscow Mule**, **Sazerac**, **Long Island Iced Tea**, **Zombie** are all present with full specs and short histories.

Full name index extracted from the section: Absinthe Drip, Alexander, American Beauty, Americano Highball, Angel's Tip, Aviation, Bacardi Cocktail, Bahama Mama, Banshee, Bee's Knees, Bellini, Between the Sheets, Bijou, Black Rose, Black Russian, Black Velvet, Blood and Sand, Bloody Mary, Blue Blazer, Bobby Burns, Brandy Flip, Brandy Milk Punch, Bronx, Bull's Blood, Bull Dog, Bull Shot, Caipirinha, Cape Codder, Champagne Cocktail, Champagne Pick-Me-Up, Claret Lemonade, Clover Club, Port Cobbler, Coffee Cocktail, Cosmopolitan, Collins (Tom/John/Vodka), Cuba Libre, Dark and Stormy, Dirty Mother/White Mother, Dubonnet Cocktail, El Presidente, Flamingo, Flame of Love Martini, French Martini, Gibson, Gimlet, Golden/Ramos/Silver Fizz, Gin Rickey, Golden Cadillac, Grasshopper, Greyhound, Harvey Wallbanger, Horse's Neck, Hot Buttered Rum, Hurricane, Jack Rose, Kamikaze, Kir, Long Island Iced Tea, Manhattan, Margarita, Martini (extra dry/dirty), Million Dollar Cocktail, Mimosa, Mint Julep, Monkey Gland, Moscow Mule, Negroni, Pimm's Cup, Presbyterian, Rob Roy, Rusty Nail, Sazerac, Salty Dog, Scarlett O'Hara, Scorpion, Sea Breeze, Slow Comfortable Screw, Sours, Southside, Stinger, Tequila Sunrise, Vesper, White Lady, Zombie.

### Match Bar Group Recipes & Originals (§23)
The house's own signature and site-created cocktails, again with exact specs and creator attribution:

- **Mojito** (the group's biggest seller): **50ml Havana Club Especial, 20ml lime juice, 20ml simple syrup, 6–8 mint leaves**, built in a 12oz highball, mint bruised (not muddled) with a bar spoon, crushed ice, swizzled.
- **Match Spring Punch** ("our signature cocktail," co-biggest seller with the Mojito): 50ml MSP mix (vodka/framboise/cassis/syrup) + 25ml lemon juice + 10ml raspberry puree, cap-shaken in a Catalina glass over cubed ice, topped with **Cava** for a two-tone layered effect.
- **Old Fashioned (house spec)**: 50ml bourbon, 7ml simple syrup, dash Angostura, stirred 90 seconds over cubed ice in a 9oz rocks glass, large orange twist.
- **Mara** (Sam Jeveons, 2004): 40ml Havana Club Blanco, 10ml Benedictine, 10ml ginger-lemongrass cordial, 5ml grenadine, 15ml lemon juice, 15ml egg white — shaken, raspberry garnish.
- **Match Coffee** (Michael Butt, Winter 2003 menu): 25ml vodka, 20ml dark crème de cacao, 10ml simple syrup, filter coffee, cayenne pinch, 60ml cream floated on top.
- **Melon Dramatic** (2003 Woodford competition winner): 50ml Woodford Reserve, 10ml Midori, 10ml apple juice, dashes lemon/lime/Angostura, mint, topped with ginger beer.
- **Millennium Cocktail** (Dale DeGroff, for the millennium — his take on the East India Cocktail): 30ml Courvoisier VSOP, 30ml pineapple juice, 25ml orange curaçao, dash Angostura.
- **Miss Martini** (Giovanni Burdi): 25ml vodka, 25ml raspberry puree, 25ml half-and-half, 10ml Chambord, 10ml simple syrup.
- **Mitch Martini** (Giovanni Burdi): 40ml Zubrówka, 25ml apple juice, dash passionfruit syrup, dash crème de pêche.
- **Moreno** (Sam Jeveons): 50ml Havana 7yr, 5ml orange curaçao, 5ml apricot brandy, 5ml moscovado simple syrup, 5ml green Chartreuse — stirred.
- **Nicola Six Punch** (Kevin Armstrong, 2006): 50ml Stoli Razberi, 20ml lemon juice, 20ml simple syrup, 25ml summer berry cordial, muddled cucumber/strawberry, topped with rosé Champagne.

The full name index for this section spans roughly 90 house drinks: American Beauty, Anejo Highball, Anniversary Fizz, B&B Sour, Big Mak, Bitter Summer, The Brazilian, Breakfast Club, Bueller, Caiprissima D'Uva, Cajun Cocktail, Calamity Jane, Cat's Eye, Claret Gin Cup, Cosmopolitan (Match version), Cowboy Martini, Cubanada, Deep Blue, Devil's Share, Enzoni, Epiphany, Esquire Martini, Gin & Tonic al Fresco, Gin Re-Fashioned, Grapefruit Julep, Hawaiian Stone Sour, Hedgerow Sling, Hong Kong Fuey (and "Reloaded"), Japon Cocktail, Joe Crow, Kamomilla Fizz, Lady Boy Cocktail, Lemon Twist, Little Old Reserve, Long Mango, Lucky Swizzle, Mara, Match Coffee, Match Spring Punch, Melon Dramatic, Millennium Cocktail, Miss Martini, Mitch Martini, Mojito, Moreno, Nicola Six Punch, Old Fashioned, Old Flame (Dale's "novice Negroni"), Peach Debonnaire, Pepino's Gun, Perfect Agave, Pineapple Fix, Pinot Peach Smash, Rathbone Gardens, Red Devil, Rum Bogul Bogul, Rumshack Punch, Silver Angel, Sir Thomas, Smoke on the Water, Smokey Martini, Space Gin Smash, Spacific Martini, South Beach Cocktail, Stolipolitan, Strawberry Bee, St Lawrence, Suburban, Tequila Treacle, Texas Rattlesnake, The Sullivan, Thomas Equinus, Velvet Fog, Vodka Espresso, Whiskey Peach Smash, Woodford Fire and Ice, Zombie.

---

## 8. Beer, wine, champagne and glossary (§24–34)

Shorter category-education sections following a consistent template: definition → raw materials/manufacture → styles (Lager vs Ale; grape varieties; Champagne method) → history/origins → glossary → a "summary and crib sheet" for quick staff reference. These sections read as First-Tuesday-style teaching material rather than operational standards, covering topics such as "the Brewed Under Licence abomination" (a critique of UK lager licensing), the Parker effect on wine buying, and a "five things you need to remember about champagne" cheat sheet.

The **Manual Glossary** (§34) defines house jargon precisely, e.g.: *Eye line* (learned fill-level judgment using the Boston glass), *Cut* (how a pour is ended — "often directly proportionate to ego"), *Double frozen* (re-freezing ice to toughen its outer layer and slow melt), *Window* (the balance range a recipe should fall inside), *First Tuesday*, *Alumni* (the list of Match bartenders who've passed through the group, described as an honor "second only to... meeting God").

---

## Notes on extraction

The PDF's page-image rendering was unreliable for this file (pages silently dropped by the Read tool), so the manual was extracted with `pdftotext -layout` (poppler) into a plain-text file and read in chunks from there — this worked cleanly and the summary above is built directly from that full-text extraction, sampled comprehensively across all 35 sections (with the two ~90-entry recipe lists — Dale's 120 Classics and Match Originals — indexed by name via grep across the full text rather than read line-by-line, since their recipe format is highly repetitive).
