<!-- ===== INÍCIO: meehans-bartender-manual.md ===== -->

# Meehan's Bartender Manual

**Author:** Jim Meehan; photography by Doron Gild; illustrations by Gianmarco Magnani; foreword by Peter Meehan
**Publisher:** Ten Speed Press, first edition, California, 2017
**ISBN:** 9781607748625

## Structural Overview

Jim Meehan (co-founder of PDT in NYC, formerly of the Pegu Club and Gramercy Tavern) wrote this as a comprehensive operations-and-recipe manual explicitly modeled on century-old texts like Charles Mahoney's *Hoffman House Bartender's Guide* and Harry Johnson's *Bartenders' Manual* — a genre he says hadn't been attempted in over a hundred years. It differs from Death & Co-style recipe books by devoting roughly half its length to bar operations (history, design, service, hospitality, career) rather than just ingredients/technique/recipes. Structure (8 chapters + front/back matter):

1. **History** — punch → ice/julep era → birth of "mixology" (1856) and Jerry Thomas → Prohibition/bartender diaspora to Europe & Cuba → Repeal and decline → post-WWII "Age of Efficiency" (vodka, tiki) → 1960s–70s "Dark Ages" → 1987 craft cocktail revival (Dale DeGroff/Rainbow Room) → Sasha Petraske/Milk & Honey → the author's own entry into the trade (2002, PDT era).
2. **Bar Design** — location/concept/branding/interior design/décor/environment (lighting, music, HVAC)/the physical bar (dimensions, ice wells, workstation ergonomics). Includes annotated floor plans of five real bars: NoMad Bar and Library (NYC), Dead Rabbit Taproom (NYC), Lidkoeb (Copenhagen), Bryant's Cocktail Lounge (Milwaukee), Drink (Boston).
3. **Tools & Techniques** — built around one deliberately over-complicated eleven-drink table order (Absinthe Drip, Mint Julep, Pisco Sour, Michelada, Margarita, Green Thumb, Negroni, Bloody Mary, Piña Colada, Irish Coffee, Jimmie Roosevelt), walking through mise en place, delegating between "point" and "service" bartenders, tools (shakers, mixing glasses, barspoons, strainers, jiggers), ice, glassware, shaking/stirring/muddling/throwing-rolling physics (leaning heavily on Dave Arnold's *Liquid Intelligence*), egg whites, dilution, straining, garnishing, and service choreography.
4. **Distillery Tour** — a cross-category walk through production (growing/harvesting → milling/mashing → fermentation → distillation → barrel maturation → blending → filtration → bottling → tasting), illustrated with real distillery visits (Bruichladdich, Yamazaki, Maker's Mark, Woodford Reserve, Cameronbridge/Tanqueray, St. George Spirits, Bacardi's Tultitlán bottling hall, Chichicapa mezcal palenque, Leopold Brothers).
5. **Spirits & Cocktails** (the book's core, ~250 pages, subdivided into the categories below) — "one hundred recipes... my cocktail canon," each with **Origin / Logic / Hacks** essay-format entries plus a modern spec (exact ounces, glass, garnish), preceded by a substantial historical/production primer for each spirit family.
6. **Cocktail Menu** — menu design as business plan: space/cost constraints, core vs. seasonal vs. limited-run recipes, the "Mr. Potato Head" and "Jenga" theories of recipe development, cocktail naming, going global, the printed menu (physical design, theft, pricing psychology), transparency about spirits-company sponsorship.
7. **Service** — full front-of-house choreography: greeting, seating, the "in and the out" of menu dialogue, pour/clear/garnish mechanics (2 o'clock garnish, 7 o'clock straw, open-handed service), noise discipline, check presentation, privacy/confidentiality, conflict de-escalation, cutting off intoxicated guests.
8. **Hospitality** — distinguished from "service" as an instinct vs. a trained skill; management philosophy (excellence vs. perfection, per Danny Meyer), building/retaining teams (sprinters vs. marathoners), company culture, bartender self-care and burnout, career trajectory beyond the bar.
9. **Epilogue: Beyond Bars** — the modern bartending career path (media, brand ambassadorship, competitions, entrepreneurship), homework/continuing education, networking, conventions (Tales of the Cocktail, Bar Convent Berlin), the "startender" critique.
10. **Bibliography** (~90 titles) and **Subrecipes** appendix (10 house syrups/batters: simple, Demerara, honey, vanilla, and agave syrups; ginger wort; lime cordial; whipped cream; horchata; Tom and Jerry batter).

Throughout, roughly 50 sidebar interviews (a paragraph of pull-quote each) with named industry figures — bartenders (Dale DeGroff, Audrey Saunders, Sasha Petraske's peers, Dave Arnold, Jeff Bell, Julie Reiner, Jack McGarry), historians (David Wondrich, Dave Broom), master distillers/blenders (John Glaser, Hans Reisetbauer, Beppe Musso, Dan Farber), and others (photographer Daniel Krieger, sommelier Jordan Salcito) — function as a kind of oral-history Rolodex of the 2000s–2010s cocktail renaissance.

## The Spirits & Cocktails Chapter — Category by Category

Each spirit section opens with an origin/production essay, then delivers cocktails in the essay format described above. Selected specifics:

**Aperitifs** — vermouth (Carpano's 1786 Turin trademark; French "blanc" vs. dry vs. Italian rosso; author uses Dolin blanc for most pre-Prohibition "French vermouth" calls, arguing the era's French vermouth was the sweeter Chambéry style, not dry Noilly Prat); quinquinas (Dubonnet, Lillet, Byrrh — created for the French Foreign Legion to mask quinine); Campari/Aperol and the aperitivo bitters family; absinthe blanche/verte production (bain-marie pot still, 65–72% ABV); champagne/prosecco sourcing; sherry/madeira/port. Recipes: Absinthe Drip, Champagne Cocktail, Kir, Venetian Spritz, Torino Torino, Sherry Cobbler, Pimm's Cup, Bamboo, Nurse (original), Michelada.

**Vodka** — Polish/Russian origin dispute (word first appears 1405), U.S./EU legal definitions of "neutral," Western vs. Eastern-style production, the flavored-vodka boom (Stoli Limonaya 1986, Absolut Citron 1988). Recipes: Vesper (Bond's 3:1 gin-to-vodka ratio, Lillet blanc), Moscow Mule (Jack Morgan/John Martin/copper-mug marketing origin story), Parkside Fizz (original), Bloody Mary (Fernand Petiot/King Cole Bar 1934), Peaches and Cream (original), Gold Coast (original, Swedish punsch), Tatanka, Czechmate (original), Cocktail Culture (original, yogurt lassi), Cosmopolitan (Toby Cecchini, Odeon 1988).

**Gin and Genever** — full genever/gin etymological and legal history (Dutch genever → English gin craze → 1751 Gin Act → 19th-c. unsweetened London dry → Prohibition bathtub gin → 1987 Bombay Sapphire/vodka-style marketing → 2004 Simon Ford/Plymouth-led classic-gin revival → "new Western" gins from 2006). Recipes: Martini, Gin and Tonic, Gimlet, French 75, Aviation, 20th Century, Last Word (Murray Stenson/Zig Zag Café 2002 revival story), Ramos Gin Fizz, Martinez, Corpse Reviver #2, Old Friend (original), White Lady, Negroni, Hans Solo (original, barrel-aged gin), Hanky-Panky (Ada Coleman/Savoy).

**Rum and Cachaça** — sugar/slave-trade history of the Triangle Trade explicitly discussed; Jamaican "hogo" dunder fermentation; navy-strength/grog (Admiral "Old Grog" Vernon, 1740); rhum agricole AOC (Martinique, 1996); Cuban column-still ron (Bacardí, 1862); cachaça categories (classica/ouro/premium). Recipes: Green Tea Punch (original, per David Wondrich's *Punch*), Mojito, Hemingway Daiquiri, Green Thumb (original), Caipirinha, Piña Colada (Caribe Hilton, 1954), Ti' Punch, El Presidente, Daiquiri, Five Island Flamingo (original), Mai-Tai, Airmail, East India Negroni (original), Plátanos en Mole Old-Fashioned (original).

**Tequila and Mezcal** — Denominación de Origen states, mixto vs. 100% agave, jimador harvesting, horno/tahona vs. autoclave/diffuser processing, Chinaco/El Tesoro/Patrón import history (1980s), Ron Cooper/Del Maguey coining "single-village mezcal," Phil Ward's Oaxaca Old-Fashioned popularizing mezcal (2007, Death & Co). Recipes: Paloma, Tommy's Margarita (Julio Bermejo, ~1987–88), Margarita, Rosita, 21st Century (original, Pegu Club), Witch's Kiss (original), El Diablo, Mezcal Mule (original), San Miguel (original), Mechuga (original).

**Whisk(e)y** — broadest historical sweep: Whiskey Rebellion, Dr. James Crow's sour mash, Lincoln County Process, Coffey still (1830), Scotch blending (Andrew Usher, 1853), Bottled-in-Bond Act (1909), Japanese whisky origins (Yamazaki, 1923; Taketsuru/Nikka). Category breakdowns: Irish, Scotch (blended vs. single-malt), American (bourbon/rye/Tennessee), Canadian, Japanese. Recipes: Highball, Rosy Cheeks (original), Stone Fence, Brown Derby, Mint Julep, Blood and Sand, Hot Whiskey, Irish Coffee, Bobby Burns, Brooklyn, Manhattan, Boulevardier, Sazerac, Old-Fashioned Whiskey Cocktail, Toronto, Brown Bomber (original), Purple Rain (original), Cameron's Kick, For Peat's Sake (original, w/ Jeff Bell), George Washington (original), DuBoudreau Cocktail (original).

**Brandy and Eau de Vie** — cognac production and classification (VS/VSOP/XO, brouillis/bonne chauffe, paradis cellars), pisco (Peru: puro/acholado/mosto verde), calvados AOC, American applejack (Laird's), eau de vie (Reisetbauer's process detailed at length). Recipes: Pisco Sour, Pisco Punch, Sidecar, Jack Rose, Champs-Élysées, Newark (original), Rose, Jimmie Roosevelt, French Maid (original), Japanese Cocktail (Jerry Thomas, 1860).

**Liqueurs** — etymology of "alcohol" and "liqueur," monastic origins, Bénédictine's 27-botanical two-year production process detailed, EU sugar/ABV minimums by category. Recipes (mostly rich/nightcap-oriented): Coffee Cocktail (Jerry Thomas — cognac, port, whole egg), Rusty Nail, Stinger, Grasshopper, Tom and Jerry, After Nine (original), Café Arroz (original, horchata + tequila), Milk Punch, Black Flip (original).

Every recipe entry follows the same three-part rhetorical structure: **Origin** (sourced to specific historic bar guides — Jerry Thomas 1862/1887, Harry Craddock's *Savoy Cocktail Book* 1930, Harry McElhone's *ABC of Mixing Cocktails* 1922, David Wondrich's *Imbibe!* as the recurring authority), **Logic** (why the proportions/ingredients work, often citing Dave Arnold's *Liquid Intelligence* science), and **Hacks** (substitution guidance and named brand recommendations).

## Cross-Cutting Technique Notes (Chapter 3)

- Shaking: per Dave Arnold, shake time (8–12 seconds) matters more than technique for temperature; aeration depends on ice size and force. Boston shaker sealed at an angle (5–7 o'clock), shaken over one shoulder with the larger tin facing the guest so leaks spray backward.
- Stirring: middle/index/thumb grip near the spoon's top, back of the bowl against the glass, ice moved gently clockwise; dilution/chill depend on ice size and stir speed/duration.
- Dry-shake-then-shake-with-ice sequence for egg white drinks to emulsify before dilution.
- "Throwing" (aerating by pouring between two tins raised overhead) traced through India → Morocco → Basque cider culture → Jerez venencia sherry service → Boadas (Barcelona, 1938) → modern Bloody Mary prep.
- Ice hierarchy: Kold-Draft/Hoshizaki 1.25" cubes for shaking/stirring, Scotsman pebble ice for swizzles/juleps/blender drinks, Clinebell block ice for clear rocks/spheres.
- Garnish placement standardized at 2 o'clock (right-handed guest) with straw at 7 or 5 o'clock; default to right-handed unless cues (watch position, water glass side) suggest otherwise.
- The "Mr. Potato Head" theory (credited to Phil Ward) and the author's complementary "Jenga" analogy: swapping a same-class ingredient (base spirit) is safe; swapping an ingredient that carries two functions (e.g., Cointreau's sweetness *and* proof) requires rebalancing the whole structure or the drink "topples."

## Operations Content (Chapters 2, 6, 7, 8) — Key Specifics

- Bar top: 38–42" high, 32–38" wide including bumper/drip tray; stool seats 24–32" high with a footrest rung 18" below seat.
- Backbar set 40–50" from the inner counter edge — enough for a barback to slip behind a working bartender without forcing them to turn around.
- Menu economics: fixed-price cocktail menus (PDT model) stabilize a variable cost (liquor) against fixed costs (rent/payroll); PDT's $7 hot dogs subsidize $16 cocktails to lower average check perception.
- Staffing philosophy: "sprinters" (opening-team risk-takers) vs. "marathoners" (steady second-wave hires); one year to train + one year to "pay it back" as the informal tenure expectation.
- Career arc essay (Epilogue) traces the post-2000s professionalization of bartending into media, brand ambassadorship (Simon Ford, Charlotte Voisey held up as models), competitions (Diageo World Class, Bacardi Legacy, Speed Rack), and entrepreneurship (Small Hand Foods, The 86 Co., Jack Rudy, Aviation Gin) — while cautioning against "startenders" who skip the craft apprenticeship.

## Relation to Other Titles in This Collection

Meehan's overlaps in period and cast of characters with *Death & Co* (both ~2014–2017, both citing Phil Ward's Oaxaca Old-Fashioned, David Wondrich, and Dave Arnold as touchstones) but is structured as an operations manual with essayistic history first and recipes second, whereas *Death & Co* is organized recipe-forward around ingredients/tools/technique/creation-methodology. Meehan's is also unusual among this shelf's bar books for spending nearly half its page count on things outside the glass — real estate, floor plans, staffing, menu economics, and career sociology of bartending as a profession.

<!-- ===== FIM: meehans-bartender-manual.md ===== -->

# Modernist Cuisine at Home — Reference Summary

**Nathan Myhrvold with Maxime Bilet** (The Cooking Lab, 2012, ISBN 978-0-9827610-1-4). Photography by Nathan Myhrvold, Melissa Lehuta, and The Cooking Lab Photography Team. The home-cook distillation of the 6-volume *Modernist Cuisine* project, focused on affordable equipment, precise temperatures, and reproducible ratios.

Extraction method: `pdftotext -layout` on the 420 MB source PDF (text layer was clean; no OCR/page-image fallback needed).

---

## 1. Full Structural Table of Contents

**Front matter:** Forewords · Our Culinary Journeys · The Story of This Book · What Is Modernist Cuisine? · About the Recipes (incl. food-safety/"lightly cooked" philosophy, p. xii–xxv)

### PART ONE: STOCKING THE MODERNIST KITCHEN

- **Ch. 1 — Countertop Tools** (p.5): digital scales, digital thermometers, silicone mats/molds, microplanes, blenders/food processors, mixers/frothers, ice-cream makers, blowtorches, Jaccard tenderizers, injectors, sieves/strainers, whipping siphons, juicers, dehydrators
- **Ch. 2 — Conventional Cooking Gear** (p.22): stoves, pots/pans, pressure cookers (buyer's guide), conventional ovens, combi ovens, microwave ovens, grills
- **Ch. 3 — Cooking Sous Vide** (p.48): Four Simple Steps, getting the air out, bags/rigid containers/jars, vacuum sealers, sealing-problem fixes, cooking & reheating, water baths, improvised water baths
- **Ch. 4 — Ingredients** (p.69): unusual ingredients, foraged foods

### PART TWO: THE RECIPES

- **Ch. 5 — Basics** (p.83): stocks, sauces (gravies, hollandaise, mayonnaise, curry sauces, ketchup, marinara), oils/fats, condiments (confits, pickles, leathers), brines/marinades, spice mixes
- **Ch. 6 — Breakfast Eggs** (p.141): egg textures, scrambled eggs, omelets, eggs sunny-side up, deviled eggs
- **Ch. 7 — Salads and Cold Soups** (p.155): gazpacho, minestrone, vichyssoise, quinoa/chickpea/lentil salads, tuna confit
- **Ch. 8 — Pressure-Cooked Vegetable Soups** (p.177): caramelized carrot soup and variants, pressure-cooked vegetables/barley
- **Ch. 9 — Steak** (p.186): cuts, grades, premium beef varieties, sous vide steak, low-temp oven steak, cooler method, grilled steak/pork chop/lamb skewers
- **Ch. 10 — Cheeseburger** (p.206): grinding meat, hamburger patty, white sandwich bread, cheeseburger, milkshake, meatloaf sandwich
- **Ch. 11 — Carnitas** (p.219): pressure-cooked carnitas, achiote paste, braised meat/poultry variations (tajine, fricassee, vindaloo, short-rib wraps, braised duck), chicharrón, refried bean foam, pork belly adobo
- **Ch. 12 — Braised Short Ribs** (p.229): potato puree, pork belly B.L.T., lamb curry/shanks
- **Ch. 13 — Roast Chicken** (p.246): pincushion/extra-juicy/deep-fried/spatchcock/combi-oven chicken, sous vide chicken, turkey confit, sous vide turkey breast
- **Ch. 14 — Chicken Wings** (p.249): sous vide Buffalo wings, Korean/skinless/yakitori wings, puffed chicken skin, wing sauces, skewer variations
- **Ch. 15 — Chicken Noodle Soup** (p.266): aromatic broth infusion technique, pho/Thai/goulash/Chinese/tortilla soup variants, fresh egg noodles + regional noodle variants, pressure-cooked carrots and leeks
- **Ch. 16 — Salmon** (p.276): fragrant sous vide salmon, kitchen-sink poaching, toaster-oven baking, crispy fish skin
- **Ch. 17 — Shellfish** (p.288): lobster roll, killing/shelling lobster, shucking mussels/clams, mussels marinière, chowder, snails
- **Ch. 18 — Pizza** (p.295): Neapolitan/quinoa/buckwheat/rustic/whole-wheat/no-knead doughs, poolish, breadsticks/pretzels, classic pizza variations, toppings
- **Ch. 19 — Mac and Cheese** (p.308): six cheese-mix variations, baked & "fat-free" versions, cheese crisps, perfectly melting cheese slice, grilled cheese sandwiches
- **Ch. 20 — Risotto and Paella** (p.320): cooking grains science, pressure-cooked paella del bosco, vegetable/pressure-cooked risotto, 8 flavor variations
- **Ch. 21 — Cornmeal** (p.335): pressure-cooked polenta, corn juice grits, shrimp and grits, tamales, hush puppies
- **Ch. 22 — Dishes for the Microwave** (p.342): eggplant Parmesan, bok choy, potato salad, black cod, beef jerky, crispy beef strands, instant sponge cakes
- **Ch. 23 — Custards and Pies** (p.358): crème brûlée/pot de crème/flan, lemon posset/curd, panna cotta, crème anglaise, sabayon, gelato, pie crusts, pastry creams, cream pies

**Back matter (roman numerals):** Further Reading · Glossary of Cooking Terms · Reference Tables (temperature/weight/volume conversions) · Contributors · Acknowledgments · Step-by-Step Procedures & Tables of Best Bets index · Index

---

## 2. Kitchen Basics — Key Concepts

- **Two essential tools:** an accurate digital scale and a digital probe thermometer. The book insists on weighing (not measuring by volume) for all precision work, especially emulsifiers/gelling agents (volume measurement can be off by up to 15%).
- **Sous vide, four simple steps:** (1) season/prep food, (2) seal in a bag, (3) cook in a temperature-controlled water bath, (4) finish (sear/glaze/etc.).
- **Pressure cooking:** standard working pressure used throughout the book is a **gauge pressure of 1 bar / 15 psi**; start timing once full pressure is reached; depressurize quickly by running tepid water over the lid rim for most vegetable/quick recipes.
- **Lightly cooked food:** recipes cooked below FDA/USDA pasteurization temperatures are flagged "lightly cooked." Serve immediately; never hold as leftovers (pathogens multiply quickly below 50 °C/120 °F). Not recommended for immunocompromised, elderly, or very young diners.
- **Refrigeration safety:** keep the fridge at or below 5 °C/40 °F. It's actually safer to cool covered hot food briefly at room temperature (≤4 hours) or in an ice bath than to overload the fridge with hot pans.
- **Sous vide bag air removal:** water-displacement method for zip-top bags is used throughout as the default (vacuum sealers reserved for long-cook items to prevent flotation/oxidation).

---

## 3. Reference Tables

### 3.1 Sous Vide Time/Temperature — Tender Cuts of Meat (core temperature)
*(temperatures in **bold** are the authors' preferred targets)*

| Ingredient | Rare | Medium-rare | Pink | Medium | Note |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Beef filet | 50 °C/122 °F | — | — | **54 °C/129 °F** | — |
| Beef flank | — | 53 °C/127 °F | 56 °C/133 °F | 62 °C/144 °F | cut thinly against grain; kalbi marinade tenderizes; hold 56 °C/133 °F for 3 h |
| Beef rib eye | — | 56 °C/133 °F | 59 °C/138 °F | 62 °C/144 °F | — |
| Beef strip steak | 54 °C/129 °F | **56 °C/133 °F** | 58 °C/136 °F | 60 °C/140 °F | dry-aged ideal |
| Lamb loin, rack | 52 °C/126 °F | **55 °C/133 °F** | 57 °C/135 °F | 62 °C/144 °F | coat with olive oil to mute lamb flavor |
| Pork loin chop | 54 °C/129 °F | 57 °C/135 °F | 59 °C/138 °F | 62 °C/144 °F | brine before cooking |
| Pork tenderloin | n/a | 56 °C/133 °F | 59 °C/138 °F | 61 °C/142 °F | — |

### 3.2 Sous Vide / Pressure-Cook — Tough Cuts of Meat

| Ingredient | Tender, yielding (sous vide) | Tender, flaky | Very flaky | Pressure-cook (min, 1 bar/15 psi) | Note |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Beef short rib | 58 °C/136 °F, 72 h | — | 88 °C/190 °F, 7 h | 50 | bone-in best; texture best carved right before serving |
| Beef brisket | 60 °C/140 °F, 72 h | 62 °C/144 °F, 72 h | 70 °C/158 °F, 72 h | 40 | use the "nose" (fattier end) for pressure-cooking |
| Oxtail, jointed | 60 °C/140 °F, 100 h | 65 °C/149 °F, 48 h | 70 °C/158 °F, 24 h | 70 | remove meat from bone while warm |
| Lamb shank | 62 °C/144 °F, 48 h | 65 °C/149 °F, 24 h | 85 °C/185 °F, 5 h | 60 | remove silverskin before cooking |
| Lamb shoulder (cubes) | 62 °C/144 °F, 48 h | 70 °C/158 °F, 18 h | 88 °C/190 °F, 8 h | 40 | coat with 5% olive oil + 0.1% thyme to mute lamb notes |
| Pork belly | 65 °C/149 °F, 36 h | — | — | 30 | cure with brine first for firm texture |
| Pork shoulder/fresh ham (cubes) | 60 °C/140 °F, 72 h | 65 °C/149 °F, 36–48 h | 75–84 °C/167–183 °F, 4–7 h | — | use more marbled, darker muscle |
| Pork ribs | 60 °C/140 °F, 48 h | — | — | 35 | serve with Grilling Spice Mix |

Braised Short Ribs recipe (below) demonstrates the trade-off directly: **58 °C/136 °F for 72 h → medium-rare steak-like texture; 62 °C/144 °F for 72 h → fork-tender but still pink.**

### 3.3 Sous Vide — Tender Poultry (breast)

| Ingredient | Medium-rare | Hold to pasteurize | Medium | Hold to pasteurize |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Chicken breast | n/a | — | 60 °C/140 °F | 20 min |
| — (alt.) | — | — | 65 °C/149 °F | 5 min |
| Duck breast | 54 °C/129 °F | 2 h 17 min | 58 °C/136 °F | 30 min |
| Quail breast | 50 °C/122 °F | 12 h | 56 °C/133 °F | 35 min |
| Squab breast | 54 °C/129 °F | 2 h 17 min | 58 °C/136 °F | 30 min |
| Turkey breast | n/a | — | 56 °C/133 °F / 62 °C/144 °F | 35 min / 8 h |

### 3.4 Sous Vide — Tough Poultry (legs/thighs/wings)

| Ingredient | Tender & juicy | Braised texture | Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| Chicken leg/thigh | 65 °C/149 °F, 1.5 h | 68 °C/154 °F, 3 h | for fried chicken: cook sous vide, coat 50/50 potato starch+Wondra, deep-fry 195 °C/385 °F, 3 min |
| Duck leg | 65 °C/149 °F, 8 h | 82 °C/180 °F, 5 h | duck confit: brine legs 12 h, cook sealed with 12 g/1 Tbsp duck fat |
| Turkey leg | 60 °C/140 °F, 48 h | 65 °C/149 °F, 24 h | brush with BBQ sauce, finish on grill |
| Turkey/chicken wing | 62 °C/144 °F, 18 h | 80 °C/176 °F, 8 h | — |

### 3.5 Sous Vide — Shellfish (core temp, min)

| Ingredient | Prep | Barely cooked | Tender | Firm | Note |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Clams | blanched/shucked | 48 °C/118 °F, 10 min | 56 °C/133 °F, 8 min | 65 °C/149 °F, 5 min | cook in own juice |
| Lobster claws | shelled | 54 °C/129 °F to core | 60 °C/140 °F to core | 65 °C/149 °F to core | best cooked in butter |
| Lobster tails | shelled | 49 °C/120 °F to core | 54 °C/129 °F to core | 59 °C/138 °F to core | sear 10 sec/side in hot pan |
| Mussels | blanched/shucked | 62 °C/144 °F, 10 min | 65 °C/149 °F, 10 min | 68 °C/154 °F, 7 min | may look translucent |
| Oysters | shucked | 45 °C/113 °F, 10 min | 48 °C/118 °F, 10 min | 52 °C/126 °F, 7 min | — |
| Scallops | shelled | 42 °C/108 °F to core | 50 °C/122 °F to core | 54 °C/129 °F, 4 min | — |
| Shrimp/prawns | peeled | 48 °C/118 °F to core | 60 °C/140 °F, 7 min | 80 °C/176 °F, 4 min | — |

Lobster prep: blanch tails 90 sec / claws 4 min in boiling water then ice bath; freezing method: 30 min in freezer to "put to sleep," then blanch whole lobster 2 min, separate tail/claws, blanch claws 2 min more.

### 3.6 Fish — Salmon (core temperature)

- **Rare (preferred):** 45 °C/113 °F — ~25 min for 2.5 cm/1 in fillets in a 46 °C/115 °F bath
- **Medium:** 48 °C/118 °F
- **Firm:** 51–52 °C/124–126 °F
- Kitchen-sink method: set tap water 2–5 °C (5–9 °F) above target temp; a large pot of water holds temperature ~1 h; a cooler holds it up to 5 h.
- Toaster-oven method: calibrate oven, set 90–110 °C/200–225 °F; cook to 45 °C/113 °F core, 38–40 min.
- Crispy fish/chicken/pork skin: cook skin sous vide (fish 5 h; chicken 12 h; pork 18 h), then deep-fry at **185 °C/365 °F**, 1–3 min, or bake at 165 °C/330 °F, 20–45 min for chips.

### 3.7 Egg Coagulation (core science, Ch. 6)

- Egg **yolk** proteins begin coagulating above **62 °C/144 °F**, progressively firming to fully hard-cooked at about **80 °C/176 °F**.
- French Scrambled Eggs are cooked sous vide at **72 °C/162 °F** for ~35 min, then blended smooth and aerated in a whipping siphon.
- A yolk cooked at 72 °C/162 °F for a set time becomes fully spherical/firm — the basis of the book's "modernist deviled egg" (whole cooked yolk sauced with a puree of the white).

### 3.8 Vegetables — Microwave (1,100 W reference oven)

| Technique | Ingredients | Prep | Method | Time |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Root vegetables | beets, carrots, celery root, potatoes, turnips | whole | vacuum seal or plate+wrap; medium beets 6 min then rub off skin | 3–7 min |
| Leafy greens | bok choy, broccoli, cabbage | whole | bag/plate with a little water | 2–5 min |
| Bulbs/fibrous veg | artichoke, cardoon, celery, fennel, leeks, onions | — | bag with 50 mL/¼ cup water, or plate with 2 mm water under wrap | 2–5 min |
| Potato salad | fingerlings + 15 g water | — | microwave 1,100 W ~4 min, slice into coins | 4 min |
| Fish fillets (black cod etc.) | 450 g/2 fillets | bagged w/ aromatics | 800 W | 3¾–5 min |

### 3.9 Grain Cooking Times (Ch. 20, risotto/paella base)

**Pressure-cook to al dente (1 bar/15 psi) vs. conventional stove top:**

| Grain | Pressure-cook (min) | Stove top (min) |
|---|---|---|
| Bomba rice | 7 | 22 |
| Forbidden rice | 12 | 25 |
| Risotto rices (Arborio, Vialone Nano, Carnaroli) | 6 | 18–20 |
| Short-grain Japanese rice | 4 | 13–15 |
| Farro | 15 | 22–25 |
| Pearl barley | 20 | 28–30 |
| Quinoa | 4 | 15–18 |
| Steel-cut oats, rinsed | 7 | 12–15 |

**Parcooking times** (for make-ahead, chilled on a frozen sheet pan, finished later 2–5 min):

| Grain | Pressure cooker (min) | Stove top (min) |
|---|---|---|
| Bomba rice | 4 | 8 |
| Forbidden rice | 7 | 17 |
| Risotto rices | 3 | 6 |
| Short-grain Japanese rice | 2½ | 4 |
| Farro | 10 | 20–22 |
| Pearl barley | 12 | 25 |
| Quinoa | 2 | 7 |
| Steel-cut oats | 5 | 7 |

### 3.10 Thickener & Gelling-Agent Ratios (Ch. 5 "Sauces")

| Agent | Ratio (per 100 g liquid) | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Xanthan gum | 0.1–0.15 g | thin sauce |
| Xanthan gum | 0.2–0.3 g | thick, ketchup/dressing-like |
| Agar powder | 0.25 g | thin broth |
| Agar powder | 0.4–0.5 g | moderately thick sauce |
| Agar powder | 0.9–1.1 g | puree-thick gel |
| Wondra (dusted, whisked not blended) | 4–5 g | classic gravy thickening |
| Liquid soy lecithin | 1–2 g | stabilizes/emulsifies a jus or pan sauce |

Quick-set fluid gel method: chill the hot, hydrated agar gel in an ice-water bath while blending with an immersion blender until it sets below **30 °C/86 °F**, yielding a pourable fluid gel.

Troubleshooting table (condensed) — Common thickening/gelling problems:
- **Lumps ("fish eyes")** → agent dispersed unevenly; dust it evenly while blending, or pre-mix with sugar/oil.
- **No set despite swollen granules** → steam caused early clumping or agent hit hot liquid before full dispersion; keep powder away from steam until dispersed.
- **Granules won't swell/dissolve** → too little gelling agent/starch weighed; use an accurate scale.
- **Gummy Wondra sauce** → Wondra was blended (not whisked) or overworked; whisk gently, don't over-blend.
- Enzymes in fresh kiwi/pineapple/papaya block gelatin — use cooked, canned, or frozen fruit instead.
- Gelatin tolerates up to 36% alcohol but tastes best at ≤10%; agar is the alcohol-tolerant, heat-stable (up to 85 °C/185 °F) choice for cold or boozy applications.

### 3.11 Gelatin Bloom-Strength Reference (Ch. 23)

| Type | Bloom rating | g per sheet |
|---|---|---|
| Bronze | 125–155 | 3.3 |
| Silver | 160 | 2.5 |
| Gold | 190–220 | 2.0 |
| Knox powdered (US standard) | 225 | n/a |
| Platinum | 235–265 | 1.7 |

Conversion formula: **M_B = M_A × (Bloom_A ÷ Bloom_B)**. Example: 2.6 g Knox (225 Bloom) ≈ 2.6 × 225 ÷ 160 = 3.7 g silver gelatin. Substitution shortcut: 1.3 g bronze or 1.1 g silver gelatin per 100 g liquid replaces Knox powdered gelatin.
Vegetarian panna cotta substitute: 0.8 g agar + 0.65 g xanthan gum, or 0.65 g iota + 0.5 g kappa carrageenan (per the recipe's liquid base).

### 3.12 Sodium Citrate Emulsified-Cheese Ratio (Ch. 19)

- **Mac and Cheese sauce:** 11 g sodium citrate + 265 g water/milk (≈4% of liquid) melts 285 g grated cheddar into a smooth, non-breaking sauce.
- **Perfectly Melting Cheese Slice — scaling rule:** weigh the liquid + grated cheeses together and use **2.8% of that total weight** in sodium citrate. Example: 750 g of finished cheese slices → 21 g sodium citrate.

### 3.13 Emulsifier/Fat Ratios — Mayonnaise & Hollandaise

- **Modernist Mayonnaise:** 75 g pasteurized egg yolk (67 °C/153 °F, 35 min sous vide) : 45 g water : 25 g Dijon : 300 g neutral oil : 18 g lemon juice : 10 g white wine vinegar (yolk = 100% baseline, oil = 400%).
- **Sous Vide Hollandaise:** wine/shallot/vinegar reduction blended with 75 g egg yolk, cooked sous vide at **65 °C/149 °F for 30 min**, then emulsified with 225 g melted butter (300% of yolk weight). 63 °C/145 °F bath → lighter foam; 67 °C/153 °F → denser foam when siphon-aerated with N₂O.

### 3.14 Weight, Volume & Temperature Conversion Notes

- °C→°F: multiply by 1.8, add 32. °F→°C: subtract 32, multiply by 5/9 (0.56).
- Grams↔ounces: divide/multiply by 28.35. 1 lb = 453.59 g/16 oz; 1 kg = 2.2 lb.
- Book policy: **all recipes are developed and tested by weight**, not volume — volume figures given are secondary/approximate (rounding alone can shift 20 mL between 3½ tsp and 1½ Tbsp depending on the source weight).
- Pressure unit note: gauge pressure of 1 bar = 15 psi is the pressure *above* ambient atmospheric (1.013 bar/14.5 psi at sea level) — this is the pressure-cooker standard used in every recipe.

---

## 4. Recipe Sampler by Category (exact ingredients & procedures)

### 4.1 Basics — Sauces & Emulsions

**Onion Fluid Gel** (yield 500 g/2 cups)
- Sweet onion 635 g (127%) · shallots 155 g (31%) · neutral oil 100 g (20%) · whole milk 500 g (100%) · **agar powder 3.5 g (0.7%)** · salt to taste
- Sweat onions/shallots in oil; bag with milk, cook sous vide 88 °C/190 °F for 1 h; sieve to make "onion milk"; whisk cold agar into 500 g onion milk, boil 1–2 min to hydrate, chill to set, then puree until smooth and fluid. Agar gels tolerate reheating up to 85 °C/185 °F without breaking (unlike gelatin).
- **Variation — Egg-Yolk Fluid Gel:** vacuum-seal 150 g/10 yolks, sous vide 80 °C/176 °F for 35 min; blend with 150 g flavored cream/stock; season with lemon.

**Pressure-Cooked Drippings** (yield 200 g/1 cup)
- Chicken wings 450 g (100%) + ground chicken thighs 450 g (100%) + soy sauce 9 g + honey 9 g, packed into 3 canning jars, lids loosened ¼ turn, pressure-cooked 1 bar/15 psi for 1 h in 2.5 cm of water. Strain; thicken with xanthan gum as needed (or a few cubes of cold butter); 1–2 g liquid soy lecithin further stabilizes the emulsion.

**Modernist Mayonnaise** (yield 450 g) — see §3.13 for ratios. Variations: **Aioli** (replace 150 g oil with Garlic Confit oil + 50 g confit garlic), **Bacon Mayonnaise** (rendered bacon fat + 0.7 g xanthan gum + 65 g crumbled bacon), **Rouille** (+59 g pimentón dulce + 0.5 g cayenne), **Tartar Sauce** (+35 g minced gherkins + 15 g parsley).

**Sous Vide Hollandaise** (yield 265 g/5 cups foam) — see §3.13. Aerated via 1 L whipping siphon + 2 N₂O cartridges; can be held warm in the siphon in a 55 °C/131 °F bath up to 1.5 h. Variations: Crustacean Hollandaise (crustacean butter), Garlic Hollandaise (+40 g garlic), Spicy Hollandaise (sesame oil + Korean Wing Sauce).

**Redeye Gravy** (yield 200 g/1 cup): sweet onion 80 g caramelized in pork fat, deglazed with Brown Pork Stock 500 g + maple syrup 15 g, reduced by two-thirds (~10 min), infused off-heat 5 min with whole French-roast coffee beans 25 g, finished with butter 35 g whisked in off the boil.

**Red Wine Glaze** (yield 100 g/⅓ cup, for Braised Short Ribs): ground beef 1.3 kg browned; mirepoix (onion/carrot/leek 500 g each) sautéed 30–35 min; tomato paste 50 g; red wine 750 g/1 bottle reduced to syrup (10–12 min); beef knucklebones 1.3 kg + water 1 kg + herbs pressure-cooked 1 bar/15 psi for 2 h; strained and reduced 45–60 min to a syrupy glaze.

**Blue Cheese Sauce** (600 g): Wondra 12 g + sodium citrate 4 g (1%) whisked into cold whole milk 200 g, simmered, then 400 g crumbled blue cheese blended in gradually with an immersion blender — sodium citrate again prevents breaking.

**Korean Wing Sauce** (280 g/1 cup): gochujang 135 g (100%) : sugar 50 g (37%) : soy sauce 30 g (22%) : Shaoxing wine 27 g (20%) : toasted sesame oil 20 g (15%) : garlic 10 g : ginger 8 g — whisked together and warmed.

**Chinese Garlic Chili Condiment** (145 g): neutral oil 432 g (288% of garlic) fries garlic 150 g until golden (~6 min), then scallion whites 40 g (~4 min); combined with jalapeño 33 g and salt 8 g. Serve within 30 min for crisp texture.

### 4.2 Eggs

**French Scrambled Eggs** (380 g, 4–6 servings): whole eggs 200 g (100%) + egg yolks 60 g (30%) + melted butter 60 g (30%) + whole milk 60 g (30%) + salt 4 g (2%), blended smooth, bagged, cooked sous vide at **72 °C/162 °F for ~35 min**, then re-blended and optionally aerated in a whipping siphon.

### 4.3 Beef & Steak

**Sous Vide Steak** (4 servings, 1 kg/2 strip steaks): preheat bath **55 °C/131 °F**; bag steaks with a little oil; cook to core temp **54 °C/129 °F, ~50 min**; deep-fry in 225 °C/437 °F oil ~30 sec to crisp crust, or blowtorch-sear; finish with melted butter and Maldon salt.

**Modernist Hamburger Patty** (4 patties, 200 g each): rib eye 360 g + short-rib meat 360 g + hanger steak 80 g, ground separately through a 4.5 mm plate; **salt exactly 0.8% of meat weight (6.4 g here), folded in exactly 1 hour before cooking**; formed into 2.5 cm-thick square patties; sous vide **55 °C/131 °F to core 54 °C/129 °F, ~45 min**; deep-fried in 220 °C/428 °F oil ~30 sec/side for crust. Firm variation adds 15 g egg yolk per 400 g meat.

**Braised Short Ribs** (4 servings, 600 g): bone-in/boneless short ribs 1.2 kg, vacuum-sealed, cooked sous vide **62 °C/144 °F for 72 h** (or 58 °C/136 °F/72 h for a steak-like medium-rare texture); sliced from bone, basted 1 min in simmering Red Wine Glaze (200 g, 17% of meat weight).

**Potato Puree** (4–6 servings, 630 g): Yukon Gold potatoes 500 g cut 2.5 cm, sous vide **70 °C/158 °F for 35 min** (gelatinizes/stabilizes starch without fully cooking), then simmered in fresh water 25 min, riced, mixed with **butter 200 g (40%) and cream/milk 125 g (25%)** — roughly a 2:5 potato-to-fat+liquid ratio (classic French recipes go up to 1:2). Never use a food processor (ruptures starch granules → gluey texture).

### 4.4 Pork

**Pressure-Cooked Carnitas** (6–8 servings, 1 kg): pork shoulder cubes 1.3 kg + Brown Pork Stock 460 g, pressure-cooked **1 bar/15 psi for 30 min**; liquid reduced to syrup (~25 min) with achiote paste 15 g + chipotle powder; pork returned and glazed 6 min. **Achiote Paste:** annatto seeds 78 g, Pressure-Cooked Garlic Confit 40 g, habanero 25 g, black pepper 7 g, coriander/cumin seeds, orange juice 124 g, white wine vinegar 119 g, lemon juice 50 g, tequila 4 g. Sous vide variation: 65 °C/149 °F for 36 h.

### 4.5 Poultry

**Turkey Confit** (4 servings, 450 g): turkey legs 900 g cured with salt 40 g (4.4%) + sugar 4 g (0.4%), then sous vide at **60 °C/140 °F** sealed with 150 g (17%) cold duck fat and rosemary. Duck fat may be substituted with butter or neutral oil.

### 4.6 Fish & Shellfish

**Fragrant Sous Vide Salmon** (4 servings, 600 g): brine (1 kg water : 50 g salt 5% : 40 g sugar 4%) for 3–5 h (or halve salt/sugar and brine 24 h for a gentler cure); sous vide bath **46 °C/115 °F**, cook to core **45 °C/113 °F, ~25 min** for 2.5 cm fillets; basted in butter (80 g) with Fish Spice Mix for 30 sec/side. Firmer variant: cook to 51 °C/124 °F.

**Crispy Fish Skin**: scaled and cleaned skin vacuum-sealed, sous vide 5 h, iced, cut into 5×7.5 cm pieces, deep-fried at **185 °C/365 °F for 1–3 min**. Chicken skin: sous vide 12 h; pork skin: 18 h, before frying.

**Lobster prep ("How To Kill and Shell a Lobster"):** cut through the head cross behind the eyes; blanch tails 90 sec, claws 4 min, then ice bath; freeze-first alternative puts live lobster to sleep 30 min before a 2-minute kill-blanch.

**Shellfish sous vide bath preheat:** set 1 °C/2 °F above the highest target temperature in the batch, then cook to individual core temps per §3.5 table.

### 4.7 Pasta, Grains & Pizza

**Fresh Egg Noodles** (4 servings, 640 g): all-purpose flour 420 g (100%) : water 125 g (30%) : egg 50 g (12%) : olive oil 25 g (6%) : vital wheat gluten 16 g (4%, Bob's Red Mill) : salt 4 g (1%). Mixed 8 min in a stand mixer, rested 1 h+ vacuum-sealed, rolled to 2 mm sheets, cut, boiled in water salted at **0.5–0.75 g salt per 100 g water** for 30 sec (firm) or 60 sec (tender).

**Pressure-Cooked Carrots and Leeks**: 2.5 cm carrots/leeks on a rack above 1 cm water, pressure-cooked **1 bar/15 psi for 5 min**.

**Neapolitan Pizza Dough** (800 g, four 30–35 cm pizzas): Antico Caputo 00 flour 500 g (100%) : water 310 g (**62% hydration**) : honey 10 g (2%) : salt 10 g (2%) : vital wheat gluten 2.5 g (0.5%) : active dry yeast 2.5 g (0.5%). Mixed 5 min, rested 10 min, mixed 5 min more, divided into 200 g balls, proofed 1 h at room temp (or overnight refrigerated). Variations: Quinoa dough (250 g AP + 250 g quinoa flour, gluten increased to 5 g); Buckwheat dough (substitute 150 g buckwheat flour, gluten to 5 g); Breadsticks (55 g dough pieces, baked 220 °C/430 °F, 12–15 min).

**Risotto/Paella method** (see §3.9 table): sauté aromatics, toast rice 1–2 min until translucent, add stock/wine, pressure-cook 1 bar/15 psi to al dente, quick-depressurize. Parcook-and-freeze technique allows finishing in 2–5 min at service time.

### 4.8 Cheese

**Mac and Cheese** (5 servings, 800 g): water or milk 265 g (93%) + sodium citrate 11 g (4%) simmered, then white cheddar 285 g (100%) blended in gradually; tossed with 240 g (84%) boiled elbow macaroni.

**Perfectly Melting Cheese Slice** (500 g/12–14 slices): cold wheat beer or water 115 g (57.5%) + sodium citrate 14 g (7%) simmered; Gruyère 200 g (100%) + sharp cheddar 180 g (90%) melted in gradually; poured onto a warm sheet, chilled 2 h+, cut into 7.5 cm rounds. Scaling rule in §3.12.

### 4.9 Vegetables & Microwave

**Microwaved Black Cod with Scallions and Ginger** (2 servings, 450 g): black cod fillets 450 g topped with scallion/ginger 25 g+10 g and Shaoxing wine 5 g, microwaved at 800 W until opaque and flaking, **3¾–5 min**; finished with soy-sesame mixture and 190 °C/374 °F hot peanut oil drizzled over just before serving. Substitutable with halibut, snapper, rockfish, trout, black sea bass, pomfret, or sole.

**Microwaved Potato Salad**: fingerling potatoes 400 g + water 15 g microwaved at 1,100 W until tender, ~4 min; sliced, dressed with red onion, Bacon Mayonnaise, Dijon mustard.

### 4.10 Custards, Creams & Desserts

**Coffee Crème Brûlée** (4 servings, 330 g): heavy cream 150 g + whole milk 150 g cold-infused overnight with whole dark-roast coffee beans 80 g (retains volatile aromas lost by hot-infusing); strained 200 g blended with egg yolks 80 g (53%), sugar 50 g (33%), salt 0.3 g; sous vide bath **82 °C/180 °F**, custard cooked to core **80 °C/176 °F, ~1 h**; chilled 2 h; torched sugar crust. Variations: Pot de Crème (skip torching), Bain-Marie version (162 °C/325 °F oven, 25–30 min), Flan (substitute whole milk for cream + whole eggs for yolks).

**Sous Vide Lemon Curd** (6–8 servings, 850 g): egg yolks 120 g (30%) cooked sous vide **65 °C/149 °F for 35 min**, iced; blended with a sugar 300 g (75%) + water 40 g + citric acid 8 g (2%) syrup, then emulsified with butter 400 g (100%) cubed in gradually. Substitutes: grapefruit/mandarin/orange/lime juice for fruit-curd variants.

**Raspberry Panna Cotta**: milk bloomed with **Knox powdered gelatin (225 Bloom)** per the ratio in §3.11, blended with cream/raspberry purée/sugar/salt, strained, set 5–8 h refrigerated (~60 g per serving, 6 servings).

**Working with Gelatin** sidebar: full Bloom conversion table and formula reproduced in §3.11.

---

## 5. Glossary — Representative Sample (full glossary spans pages IV–XI)

- **Amylose/amylopectin** — the two glucose polymers in starch; amylose gels better, amylopectin thickens/binds water better (basis of why short-grain rice works for risotto but long-grain doesn't).
- **Bloom** — gelatin gel-strength rating (see §3.11).
- **Calcium chloride / calcium lactate** — mineral salts used to firm bean/pickle skins and in Modernist spherification gels.
- **Carrageenan** — seaweed-derived gelling alternative to gelatin: iota = soft/elastic gel, kappa = firm/brittle gel, lambda = thickener only (no gel).
- **Combi oven** — oven combining convection (fan-driven hot air) with injected steam.
- **Core temperature** — the temperature at the thickest part's center, monitored by probe thermometer; central concept of the whole book.
- **Gauge pressure** — pressure above ambient atmosphere (1 bar/15 psi standard used in every pressure-cooker recipe here).
- **Sodium citrate** — citric-acid salt; emulsifying salt central to the book's melting-cheese technique (mac & cheese, cheese slices).
- **Wondra** — instant-dissolving flour used as a modern substitute for classical roux thickening (4–5 g per 100 g liquid).
- **Xanthan gum** — bacterial-fermentation thickener used in tiny doses (0.1–0.3 g/100 g) with no perceptible flavor impact.

---

## 6. Food Safety Notes (About the Recipes, p. xxiv–xxv)

- USDA/FDA guidelines are temperature-only; true pathogen kill requires **time-and-temperature combinations** — the book calculates both.
- Example correction: USDA's recommended pork cooking temp dropped from 71 °C/160 °F to **63 °C/145 °F** after review.
- "Lightly cooked" recipes (fish, some eggs) fall below pasteurization guidelines by design, for texture — eat immediately, discard leftovers, avoid serving to immunocompromised/elderly/very young.
- Refrigerator target: below **5 °C/40 °F**.

# Modernist Cuisine — Thickening and Emulsification (Volume 4 excerpt)
### Nathan Myhrvold, Chris Young, Maxime Bilet (The Cooking Lab)

Condensed technical reference synthesized from a 29-page excerpt of *Modernist Cuisine, Volume 4: Ingredients and Preparations*, comprising the chapter **Thickeners** (printed pp. 1–19, i.e. book pages ~1-6 through 1-19) and the opening of the chapter **Emulsions** (printed pp. 199–212, book pages ~15-199 through 15-212). This file captures the full section structure plus a comprehensive synthesis of actual reference tables — thickener classes, viscosity values, gelatinization temperatures, HLB values, emulsifier ratios, and droplet-size/tool parameters — meant as a usable kitchen-science reference, not a narrative summary.

---

## STRUCTURE (as printed)

### Chapter: THICKENERS
- Making a Liquid Thicker (intro) — p.6
- Thick and Thin (viscosity basics) — p.6
- Traditional Thickeners (table) — p.7
- THE TECHNOLOGY OF: Measuring Viscosity — p.8
- Through Thin and Thick: The Viscosity of Common Fluids (table) — p.8
- The Importance of Mouthfeel — p.9–10
- Setting Flavors Free (flavor release) — p.10–11
- THE VARIOUS METHODS OF: Thickening by Reduction — p.11
- HOW THICKENING WORKS — p.12–13
- Thickening Oil — p.13
- STRATEGIES FOR THICKENING — p.14
- What Matters for Thickening (table) — p.15
- MODERN METHODS OF THICKENING — p.18
- Modern Thickeners (full method/ingredient/application/note table) — p.19
- STARCHES — p.20–22
- Dispersion — p.20
- Gelatinization — p.22
- HOW TO: Disperse a Thickener — p.24
- [Note: recipes referenced but not included in this excerpt: pregelatinized starch paste p.29, Ultra-Sperse 3 / xanthan applications p.30, etc.]

### Chapter: EMULSIONS
- Oil and Water Don't Mix (intro: continuous phase, dispersed phase, O/W vs W/O) — p.199
- HOW EMULSIFICATION WORKS — p.200

## 1. VISCOSITY FUNDAMENTALS

- **Viscosity** = a fluid's resistance to shear forces; measured in **centipoise (cP)** or Pascal-seconds (Pa·s = cP ÷ 1,000).
- Reference fluid: **water = 1 cP at 20 °C / 68 °F**.
- Water's viscosity is strongly temperature-dependent: **1.8 cP at 0 °C/32 °F**, drops to **0.5 cP at 60 °C/140 °F**, and **0.3 cP just below boiling** — a 6.4× range across its liquid temperature span. This is why body/mouthfeel for a consommé served hot can drop by more than half versus room temperature.
- **Newtonian fluids**: flow rate directly proportional to force applied (water, milk, cream, oils, corn syrup, honey, molasses, chocolate syrup, liquid nitrogen).
- **Non-Newtonian fluids**: viscosity changes with applied force.
  - **Shear-thinning** (e.g., ketchup, yogurt/thixotropic, tomato paste, peanut butter/plastic): resist initial force, flow more easily once moving.
  - **Shear-thickening** (e.g., puffed cracker dough): the opposite.
  - **Plastic fluids** (tomato paste, peanut butter, lard): behave like solids until a yield stress is exceeded.
- **Detectable particle size in the mouth**: 7–10 ten-thousandths of an inch (≈ 7–10 microns). Below this, particles read as smooth; at/above, as gritty.

### Viscosity of Common Fluids (centipoise at 20 °C/68 °F unless noted)
| Fluid | Type | Centipoise |
|---|---|---|
| liquid nitrogen | Newtonian | 0.2 (at –196 °C/–321 °F) |
| water | Newtonian | 1 |
| milk | Newtonian | 2 |
| blood | Newtonian | 10 |
| cream | Newtonian | 20–40 |
| vegetable oil | Newtonian | 50–100 |
| corn syrup and honey | Newtonian | 2,000–3,000 |
| molasses | Newtonian | 5,000–10,000 |
| chocolate syrup | Newtonian | 10,000–25,000 |
| yogurt | thixotropic | 25,000 |
| ketchup | shear-thinning | 50,000–70,000 |
| puffed cracker dough | shear-thickening | 100,000–120,000 |
| tomato paste or peanut butter | plastic | 150,000–250,000 |
| lard | plastic | 1,000,000–2,000,000 |

- Lowest known-viscosity fluid: liquid helium below –270 °C/–454 °C becomes a quantum superfluid with **viscosity of exactly 0 cP**.

## 3. WHAT MATTERS FOR THICKENING (selection table, p.15)

| Factor | Range of values | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Serving temperature** | cold (≤5 °C/41 °F) | chilled |
| | neutral (~20 °C/68 °F) | room temperature |
| | hot (≥50 °C/120 °F) | hot |
| **Clarity** | clear/transparent | consommé, coffee |
| | opaque | milk; white or brown sauces |
| **Viscosity** | very thin (10–20 cP) | orange juice |
| | thin (20–75 cP) | cream |
| | medium (75–200 cP) | olive oil |
| | thick (200–1,200 cP) | egg yolk |
| | very thick (1,200–5,000 cP) | honey, yogurt |
| | paste (5,000–20,000 cP) | mustard, mayonnaise |
| | nearly solid (50,000–200,000 cP) | peanut butter |
| **pH** | alkaline (≥8) | native egg white |
| | neutral (6–8) | most vegetables and meats |
| | mildly acidic (3–6) | fruit juices, purées |
| | very acidic (≤3) | lemon juice, vinegar |
| **Flavor release** | slow and long-lasting | fat |
| | fast and short-lived | gelatin |
| **Mouthfeel** | creamy | cream sauce |
| | sticky | syrup |
| | slippery | raw fish |
| **Syneresis (weeping)** | low | yogurt |
| | moderate | apple sauce, flan |
| | high | chawanmushi, gazpacho |

- Practical anti-weeping formula cited: **0.2% xanthan gum + 0.5% Ultra-Sperse 3** added to a purée like gazpacho prevents syneresis.
- Emulsions typically break if heated above **70 °C / 158 °F** (see §7).
- Gums like cellulose gum and guar gum work at concentrations low enough to avoid adding opacity; xanthan or modified hydrocolloids can be used for a clear thick liquid.

| hydrocolloid fluid gel | agar | | |
| hydrocolloid fluid gel | gellan gums (high- and low-acyl) | | |

- Hydrocolloid gums thicken at low concentrations: **0.1%–0.5%**.
- Xanthan gum: gentle body increase at low dose up to full paste-like thickness across a range; pairs synergistically with **locust bean gum** and **guar gum** (guar alone cannot gel but shows synergistic thickening combined with xanthan).
- N-Zorbit (modified tapioca starch): **adsorbs oil** rather than dissolving — turns oil into a slurry-to-solid mass; used to powder oils into boxed cake mixes.
- Modified starches Ultra-Sperse 3 and Ultra-Tex 8: tapioca-based, pregelatinized and stabilized — thicken directly with no heat required.

---

## 5. STARCH SCIENCE (p.20–22)

- Two polymer types in natural starch: **amylose** (linear chains; better for gelling) and **amylopectin** (branched; better at water-binding/thickening).
- Typical composition: **corn and wheat starch ≈ 25% amylose / 75% amylopectin**; **tapioca starch ≈ 18% amylose / 82% amylopectin**. Waxy corn (discovered early 1900s China) is almost all amylopectin.
- Corn/rice starch granules are small → smoother cooked appearance; potato starch granules are large → grainier, mottled texture.
- **Dispersion** = evenly distributing starch granules before hydration (prevents "fish eyes"/lumps). Cold water preferred (slower absorption = more working time). Wheat flour forms lumps easily even in cold water and carries raw flavor — hence the classical **roux** (fat-coating of granules) to prevent clumping and hide raw taste.
- Roux cooking levels: **blond** (lightly cooked, just removes raw taste), **medium/dark** (longer cooked for color/flavor; less thickening power, but flavor — not thickness — is the primary reason to darken it).
- **Gelatinization**: heat + water break the crystalline bonds in starch, allowing water penetration; the starch converts from ordered/crystalline to disordered/amorphous, and the liquid thickens or gels.
  - Example: **potato starch begins gelatinizing at 58 °C/136 °F**, continuing up to **65 °C/149 °F**.
  - Modified/"instant" starches (e.g., Ultra-Sperse 3, Ultra-Tex 8, Wondra) are pre-gelatinized by the manufacturer and thicken without further cooking/heat.
- **How To Disperse a Thickener** (4-step method, p.24):
  1. Bring liquid to correct temperature (most — not all — thickeners disperse best cold).
  2. Sprinkle powder evenly over the surface (mix multiple dry powders together before adding).
  3. Blend for **at least one full minute** for even distribution; avoid overmixing, which can undermine some thickeners' performance.
  4. If using a heat-capable blending tool (e.g., Thermomix), can proceed directly to hydration without transferring vessels.
- Cellulose gums (e.g., methylcellulose) are the notable exception: they disperse best in **hot** water, not cold.

---

## 6. EMULSIONS — FUNDAMENTALS (p.199–200)

- Definitions: **continuous (bulk) phase** = the phase that surrounds; **dispersed phase** = droplets suspended within it.
- **O/W (oil-in-water)**: continuous phase is water, dispersed phase is oil — e.g., milk, cream, mayonnaise.
- **W/O (water-in-oil)**: continuous phase is oil, dispersed phase is water — e.g., butter (butterfat continuous, with some crystallized fat stabilizing the water droplets).
- Rarer types: double emulsions **O/W/O** and **W/O/W** (droplets nested within droplets within a continuous phase) — used in cosmetics, not yet common in food.
- Emulsions are inherently **meta-stable**: given enough time they separate into component liquids.
- A droplet's surfactant coating is called a **micelle**.

---

## 7. COMMON EMULSIONS — full table (p.201)

| Food | Emulsifier or stabilizer | Note |
|---|---|---|
| beurre blanc | casein from butter | very fragile without an additional emulsifier |
| butter | casein from dairy | less fragile than beurre blanc |
| chocolate | lecithin and/or casein (from milk powder), sometimes polyglycerol polyricinoleate | ganache/milk chocolate use sodium caseinate; lecithin traditionally added to chocolate |
| Mountain Dew and other sodas | gum arabic | emulsifies flavor compounds in soft drinks |
| espresso crema | coffee polysaccharides | lipids/colloids forced into emulsion under pressure, stabilized by extracted polysaccharides |
| frankfurter | myosin protein from meat | when overcooked, proteins denature and emulsion breaks down |
| hollandaise | lecithin from egg yolk | stability depends on droplet size |
| ice cream | casein, egg yolk, semisolid fat droplets | complex emulsion + foam stabilized by dairy proteins, lecithin, and semisolid fat droplets trapping air bubbles |
| mayonnaise | lecithin from egg yolk | stability depends on droplet size |
| milk and cream | casein | homogenization reduces particle size |
| vinaigrette | polysaccharides and proteins from mustard | fragile |

- General O/W emulsifier rule of thumb: **the volume of emulsifier should be about 5% of the volume of the oil phase** (starting point, significant exceptions apply).

---

## 8. BANCROFT'S RULE & OIL CONTENT BY EMULSION (p.202)

Bancroft's Rule (Wilder Dwight Bancroft, 1913): the nature of an emulsion (O/W vs W/O) is primarily determined by whether its emulsifier is more soluble in oil or in water. Water-soluble emulsifier → O/W; oil-soluble emulsifier → W/O. Mixing order matters: mix emulsifier into the phase it's most soluble in first, then add the dispersed phase while blending (this is why traditional mayonnaise mixes lecithin-carrying egg yolk into lemon juice first, then beats in oil).

**Oil content of common O/W emulsions (illustrates rule's limits since ratio alone doesn't determine type):**
| Emulsion | Oil (fat) content |
|---|---|
| milk | ~3.25% oil (milk fat) by weight |
| heavy whipping cream | ~36% oil |
| mayonnaise | typically >80% oil |
| butter | ≥80% oil, yet is W/O (not O/W) |

---

## 9. HLB VALUES (p.204)

Hydrophilic-Lipophilic Balance (HLB) — numeric scale **0–20** describing a surfactant's water/oil solubility ratio.

| Surfactant type | HLB range | Example |
|---|---|---|
| antifoaming agent | 0–3 | oleic acid (1.0) |
| W/O emulsifier | 4–6 | mono- and diglycerides; sorbitan monostearate (4.7) |
| wetting agent | 7–9 | sorbitan monolaurate / Span 20 (8.6) |
| O/W emulsifier | 8–18 | soy lecithin (8.0); polysorbate 60/Tween 60 (14.9); sucrose monolaurate (15.0) |
| detergents | 13–15 | Octoxynol-9 |
| solubilizer/hydrotrope | 10–18 | sodium xylene sulfonate |

- Soy lecithin HLB = 8.0; modified lecithins can range widely. Mono-/diglycerides can range **HLB 1–10** depending on modification.

---

## 10. MICROEMULSIONS AND NANOEMULSIONS (p.205)

| Type | Droplet size | Stability | Optical appearance |
|---|---|---|---|
| macroemulsion (most food emulsions) | 0.1–100 microns (µm) | thermodynamically unstable (but kinetically stable enough to serve) | opaque/turbid |
| nanoemulsion | 20–100 nanometers (0.02–0.1 µm) | thermodynamically unstable but can last long enough | transparent |
| microemulsion | 5–50 nm (0.005–0.05 µm) | thermodynamically **stable**, forms spontaneously | transparent |

- Nanoemulsion example: Mountain Dew and other sodas use gum arabic to nanoemulsify flavor oils.
- Microemulsions are not common in food but are used to encapsulate oil-soluble vitamins in some nutritional products.

---

## 11. OPTICS OF EMULSIONS (p.203)

- **Mie scattering**: large particles relative to light wavelength scatter all colors equally → opaque/white appearance (fog, clouds, cream, milk with added fat, glass beads).
- **Rayleigh scattering (Tyndall effect)**: very small particles comparable in size to light wavelength scatter some colors preferentially → bluish cast (nonfat milk, the sky).
- Cream: high fat-droplet concentration → strong Mie scattering → very white. Nonfat milk: fat mostly removed → faint Rayleigh/Tyndall bluish tint from milk protein particles.

---

## 12. METHODS AND TOOLS OF EMULSIFYING (p.206, 208)

- **Primary emulsion**: first-stage dispersal of one phase into another — coarse droplets.
- **Secondary emulsion**: further mechanical reduction of droplet size in an existing emulsion — finer, more stable, longer-lasting.
- Droplet size / gap benchmarks:
  - Standard blender: emulsion droplets down to about **10 microns / 0.0004 in**.
  - Rotor-stator homogenizer: blade gap as close as **25 microns / 0.001 in**; reduces most droplets to about **3 microns** diameter.
  - High-pressure homogenizer: finest droplets, **averaging less than 0.1 micron**; can be run multiple passes for further reduction; usable only for secondary emulsions.
  - Ultrasonic homogenizer: best for creating a fine **secondary** emulsion via mechanical vibration/probe; higher overheating risk, force concentrated near the probe tip.
- General rule: more emulsifier + smaller droplets = more stable, longer-lasting emulsion (with exceptions).
- Overheating risk: friction from mechanical emulsifying generates heat; mitigate by starting with very cold ingredients, using ice for part of the water phase, or processing in bursts with cooling breaks.
- **Phase inversion**: with the right mechanical agitation and temperature, an O/W emulsion (cream) can invert to a W/O emulsion (butter) — a classic example of controlled phase inversion in traditional cooking.

### Tools for Making Emulsions — full table (p.208)
| Equipment | Power | Example uses | Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| handheld whisk | low | light mayonnaise, vinaigrette | readily available; produces large droplets, so emulsions are not stable; best for quick/cold, immediate-use emulsions |
| electric whisk | medium | light mayonnaise, vinaigrette | faster than a handheld whisk |
| hand blender | medium | beurre blanc, hollandaise | sufficient for everyday use with small quantities; can be used over a heat source |
| household blender | medium | mayonnaise, vinaigrette | sufficient for everyday use |
| commercial blender | high | mayonnaise, vinaigrette | handles larger quantities better than household blenders |
| rotor-stator homogenizer | very high | restructured cream, complex oil emulsion, ultrastable beurre blanc, microemulsion | forms extremely stable emulsions; best all-around bet for fine emulsions |
| ultrasonic homogenizer | very high | ultrastable mayonnaise, ultrastable vinaigrette | best for secondary emulsion when long-term stability needed |
| high-pressure homogenizer | extremely high | ultrastable ice cream, restructured cream | best for secondary emulsion when long-term stability needed |

---

## 13. HOW EMULSIONS FAIL (p.210–212)

| Failure mode | Mechanism | Occurs in |
|---|---|---|
| **Creaming** | oil phase (lower density) floats to the surface | O/W emulsions only |
| **Sedimentation** | dispersed water droplets (heavier than oil) sink | W/O emulsions (equivalent of creaming) |
| **Flocculation** | droplets clump due to small electrostatic charge, chemical reactions, or excessive heating, forming "flocculation complexes"; increases creaming risk | any |
| **Coalescence** | droplets merge into larger droplets, reducing stability as size grows | any |
| **Ostwald ripening** | small droplets lose material (oil, or alcohol-solubilized components) to larger droplets over time via dissolution/redeposition at the droplet surface, so big droplets grow at the expense of small ones; the process continues until full separation | rare in cooking except cream liqueurs, ouzo/anise liqueurs, or other alcohol-containing microemulsions |

- Most food emulsions **break if heated above 70 °C / 158 °F**, with many exceptions.
- A **centrifuge** accelerates creaming/sedimentation by magnifying gravitational force by thousands of times — used deliberately to break emulsions (e.g., clarifying stock by removing fat) or to separate phases (e.g., breaking milk to make nonfat milk).
- Certain filtration techniques can also separate emulsion phases.

---

## 14. THICKENING OIL (p.13)

- Most thickening addresses water-based solutions; thickening **oil** itself requires different approaches:
  - Emulsify the oil with water or another liquid (an emulsion is inherently thicker than either pure phase — e.g., mayonnaise).
  - Use a thickener that works **directly on oil** without needing an emulsion first — e.g., **N-Zorbit** (tapioca-derived) adsorbs oil, turning it from thin liquid to slurry to solid mass depending on dose; used to convert oils to powder form for products like boxed cake mixes.
  - Mix an oil with another oil/fat of a higher melting point (e.g., blending deodorized, flavorless cocoa butter into olive oil yields an olive-oil-flavored fat that stays **solid at room temperature and remains "quite thick" even at 40 °C / 104 °F**).

# Modernist Cuisine: The Art and Science of Cooking — Volume 1: History and Fundamentals

**Authors:** Nathan Myhrvold, with Chris Young and Maxime Bilet
**Photography:** Ryan Matthew Smith and Nathan Myhrvold
**Publisher:** The Cooking Lab, Bellevue, WA — First edition, 2011
**ISBN:** 978-0-9827610-0-7

Modernist Cuisine is a five-volume, ~2,400-page reference work (plus a spiral-bound kitchen manual) that set out to document, explain, and scientifically ground the "Modernist" culinary movement — the sous-vide-and-hydrocolloids style of cooking associated with elBulli, The Fat Duck, and their successors. Volume 1 is the foundation volume: it tells the history of how cooking evolved into this movement, and it lays down the microbiology, food-safety science, nutrition science, and physics that the later, more technique-focused volumes build on. Nathan Myhrvold — former Microsoft CTO, physicist (PhD in mathematical physics, postdoc under Stephen Hawking), and self-taught cook with a "Grand Diplôme" from La Varenne — assembled a team of Fat Duck alumni (Chris Young and Maxime Bilet) and a dedicated photography/science staff over roughly three years to produce it.

The five volumes of the full work are:
1. **History and Fundamentals** (this volume) — history; microbiology; food safety; food and health; heat and energy; physics of food and water.
2. **Techniques and Equipment** — traditional cooking methods, modern ovens, sous vide, and the "Modernist kitchen" (extraction, infusion, juicing, filtering, concentrating, cutting, drying, cryogenic freezing).
3. **Animals and Plants** — meat and seafood science, and plant foods.
4. **Ingredients and Preparations** — thickeners, gels, emulsions, foams, wine, and coffee.
5. **Plated-Dish Recipes** — full restaurant-style recipes organized by main ingredient.

---
