## GENERAL NOTES & CONVENTIONS (pp. viii, 1–9)

- **Salt**: recipes use fine salt unless noted. Weight per Tbsp varies by brand/type: Diamond Crystal kosher 9.6 g/Tbsp (2.9 g/tsp); La Baleine sea salt coarse 18.74 g/Tbsp; La Baleine fine 16.63 g/Tbsp; Morton kosher coarse 17.63 g/Tbsp; Morton sea salt coarse 17.63 g/Tbsp; Morton Mediterranean sea salt fine 16.33 g/Tbsp; Morton table salt (iodized) 18.23 g/Tbsp (5.57 g/tsp); Morton table salt (plain) 19.5 g/Tbsp (5.67 g/tsp). **Weighing ingredients is strongly recommended over volume.**
- Salt/yeast amounts sometimes given to tenths of a gram — use an analytical scale (accurate to 1/10,000 g) for very small quantities; dilute into the water portion for even dispersal.
- Butter used in recipes: unsalted, 82–83% fat, added at room temperature.
- Malted flours contain diastatic malt powder (DMP), boosting amylase/sugar for yeast. DMP is added even to malted-flour doughs with long ferments, to ensure yeast never runs out of food.
- "Sugar" in ingredient lists always means granulated sugar.
- Baker's percentage symbol used throughout (flour = 100%).

### How To Scale a Recipe (p.1)
1. Note flour = 100% in the baker's-percentage column.
2. Recipe Conversion Factor (RCF) = desired yield ÷ recipe yield.
3. New flour weight = original flour weight ÷ RCF.
4. New ingredient weight = ingredient's baker's % × new flour weight.
5. For items with no baker's %, multiply the count/volume by the RCF.
(Worked example: 1.4 kg dough → 1 kg dough, RCF = 0.71; 630 g flour → 447 g; water at 76.19% → 340 g; salt at 2.94% → 13.1 g.)

### Commercial Yeast (p.1)
- **Instant yeast**: can go straight into flour, but blooming in room-temp water (19–21°C/66–70°F) improves dispersal. Skipping bloom → proofing time +20–30 min; in low-hydration doughs (≤55%) some granules may remain visibly undissolved.
- **Active dry yeast**: must bloom in 43°C/110°F water (warm liquid needed to slough dead cell layer). Dough will ferment faster since water starts warm — watch proofing time.
- **Fresh yeast**: dissolve in room-temp water portion by hand/whisk.

### Poolish (p.2)
Table — yeast amount vs. time before mixing into dough (storage 21–24°C/70–75°F):
| Start poolish before mixing | % instant yeast |
|---|---|
| 3 h | 0.4–0.5% |
| 8 h | 0.23–0.33% |
| 12–16 h | 0.1–0.2% |
| 17–18 h | 0.01–0.09% |

Ripens any time between 3–18 h; older poolish = better structure/more fermentation flavor. Ripe poolish shows CO2 bubbles, elevated height, bubbles rupture when moved, sweetish/fresh-milk aroma. Overripe poolish can still be used +0.5% commercial yeast, frozen, or converted to a levain.

### Levain (pp. 3–5)
Comparison of levain types:
| Type | Inoculation time | Advantages | Disadvantages |
|---|---|---|---|
| Liquid levain | 4–5 d | basic ingredients; biological expression of environment; economical | needs maintenance/temp control; excess levain produced |
| Commercial yeast/poolish-started levain | 3–4 d | shorter inoculation than liquid levain | needs maintenance; slower to develop levain flavor |
| Florapan | 18 h @ 28°C/82°F or 24 h @ 21°C/70°F | fresh daily; pronounced sour <1 day | less complex flavor |

**Starting a liquid levain** (yield 200 g): 100 g bread flour (11.5–12% protein) + 100 g water (100%/100% baker's %). Mix, cover airtight, hold at ~21°C/70°F. Wait 48 h for first CO2 activity. Discard 75% of culture, refeed equal parts flour/water (up to 120% water) at 24°C/75°F; repeat daily at consistent time. After day 4, discarded levain can be used or frozen (Second-Chance Levain).

**Best Bets for Levains** table (storage temp / ripeness windows):
- Liquid levain @ 13°C/55°F (wine fridge): 10–12h young/mellow sour, 14–16h mature, 18–20h ripe/pronounced sour, 25–27h no longer usable.
- Liquid levain @ 21–24°C/70–75°F: 8–10h young, 12–14h mature, 16–18h ripe, 23–25h spent.
- Frozen levain @ -10°C/14°F: not fed, very sour when thawed; needs 0.4–0.5% added instant yeast; freeze up to 2 months.
- Feed schedule: daily (once/day cool months, twice/day hot months or hot environments). Composition ratio 100% flour : 100% water : 25% liquid levain (of previous batch).
- Short-term storage (2–3 d): feed then refrigerate, or feed+keep at 10°C/50°F, or feed with reduced hydration to 60% + 2% salt at room temp.
- Beyond 3 d: feed, then freeze (≤2 mo) or dehydrate.

**Dehydrating a levain**: spread thin on nonstick mat, dehydrator at 30°C/86°F (lowest setting), dry ≥24 h, grind dried levain in coffee/spice grinder, store airtight in fridge up to 1 year. To revive: 100% water rehydrate 48 h at room temp, then feed 100% flour + 100% water; active again within 24 h. Best dehydrated at peak ripeness, 1.5–18 h after feeding.

**Second-Chance (inactive/frozen) levain**: provides flavor + hydrated flour only, NOT leavening — must add 0.4–0.5% instant yeast. Thaw 2–3 h in 29°C/85°F water bath, or thaw cubes 18+ h at room temp. Never let inactive levain exceed 60°C/140°F (starch gelatinizes, becomes unusable).

### Fat Percentages in Master Doughs (p.6)
| Dough | Min fat % | Fat % in master recipe | Max fat % |
|---|---|---|---|
| Thin-Crust | 0 | 0 | 2–5 |
| Neapolitan | 0 | 0 | 10 |
| Detroit-Style | 0 | 0 | 5 (must use oil, not lard) |
| Artisan | 0 | 3.2 | 10 |
| New York | 0 | 3.39 | 10 |
| New York Square | 0 | 3.39 | 10 |
| High-Hydration Al Taglio | 0 | 3.92 | 10 |
| Focaccia | 0 | 4.04 | 10 |
| Deep-Dish | 5 | 8.68 | 15 |
| Brazilian Thin-Crust | 5 | 9.76 | 30 (only with solid/crystalline fat, e.g. ghee — better flavor and crispier than butter) |

### Select Inclusions for Pizza Dough (p.7) — suggested baker's % (based on 70–75% hydration doughs)
| Ingredient type | Suggested % |
|---|---|
| Cooked fruits, chopped | 8–10 |
| Cured meats, chopped/diced | 7–8.5 |
| Dried fruits | 12–15 |
| Dry cheeses, grated | 8.5–10.5 |
| Firm-cooked vegetables, chopped | 8.5–12 |
| Fruit/vegetable purees | 15–20 |
| Grains (soaked/cooked/soft) | 10–20 |
| Herbs, chopped | 0.3–1 |
| Nuts, toasted/chopped | 10–16 |
| Porridges/grain purees | 15–20 |
| Pressure-caramelized grains | ≤50 |
| Seeds | 10–16 |
| Soft-cooked vegetables, pureed/chopped | 15–20 |

Add inclusions (except herbs) at end of mixing; herbs can go at start or end. Mix-and-match proportionally by category.

### Grain/Seed Inclusion Prep Methods (p.8)
Soaking (easy, may under-tenderize) / Sprouting (best texture, needs advance planning, mold risk) / Cooking al dente (textural contrast, easy to over/undercook) / Porridge (moist crumb, firms up when refrigerated) / Pureeing cooked grains/seeds (adds flavor/moisture, some get gummy).

### Puree Substitutions (p.9) — scaling % relative to water weight (not flour)
| Type | Scaling % |
|---|---|
| Acidic fruit/vegetable juices | 50–100 |
| Acidic fruit purees | 50–70 |
| Alliums | 50–70 |
| Canned/jarred ingredients | 15–50 |
| Herbs | 5–10 |
| Legumes | 25–40 |
| Nonacidic fruit purees | 50–80 |
| Nonstarchy vegetables | 50–70 |
| Nut butters/pastes | 20–35 |
| Pickled vegetables | 10–15 |
| Sauces (moles, salsa, curry, gochujang, romesco, tomato) | 20–35 |
| Starchy fruits/vegetables | 15–25 |

---

## CHAPTER 5: PIZZA OVENS — Best Oven by Style (pp. 10–11)

| Pizza style (bake temp range) | Best oven | Compromise | Not recommended |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Neapolitan** (455–480°C / 850–900°F) | Gas-fired pizza oven | Wood-fired, pizza deck, impinger oven | Bread deck, combi, convection, home oven |
| **Artisan (high-heat)** (250–315°C / 480–600°F) | Pizza deck, bread deck, impinger oven | Wood-fired, gas-fired | n/a |
| **Thin-Crust** (250–285°C / 480–550°F) | Pizza deck, bread deck, gas-fired, impinger | Combi, convection, wood-fired, home oven | n/a |
| **Brazilian Thin-Crust** (250–285°C / 480–550°F) | same as Thin-Crust | same | n/a |
| **New York** (290–315°C / 550–600°F) | Pizza deck, bread deck, gas-fired, impinger | Combi, convection, wood-fired, home oven | n/a |
| **Artisan (medium-heat)** (250–315°C / 480–600°F) | Pizza deck, bread deck, impinger | Combi, convection, home oven | n/a |
| **Focaccia** (205–260°C / 400–500°F) | Pizza deck, bread deck, impinger, home oven | Combi, convection | Wood-fired, gas-fired |
| **New York Square** (205–260°C / 400–500°F) | Pizza deck, bread deck, impinger | Combi, convection, home oven | Wood-fired, gas-fired |
| **Al Taglio** (250–315°C / 480–600°F) | Pizza deck, bread deck, impinger | Home oven | Combi, convection, wood-fired, gas-fired |
| **Detroit-Style** (250–275°C / 480–525°F) | Pizza deck, bread deck, impinger | Combi, convection, home oven | Wood-fired, gas-fired |
| **Deep-Dish** (250–290°C / 480–550°F) | Pizza deck, bread deck, impinger | Combi, convection, home oven | Wood-fired, gas-fired |

Key notes: Neapolitan needs the highest heat (up to 900°F) and is only well-served by gas-fired ovens where temperature is controllable; wood-fired ovens have inconsistent fires and require skill. Pan-baked styles (Detroit, deep-dish, al taglio) are hard to rotate/move in wood/gas pizza ovens — better in deck ovens. For combi/convection/home ovens, use a baking steel for a crispy bottom, and a heat gun on the rim for char if needed (Neapolitan).

### How To Preheat a Home Oven (p.11)
1. Calibrate oven — keep an oven thermometer inside at all times.
2. Place baking steel/stone in oven BEFORE turning on to preheat.
3. Position rack in top third of oven.
4. Turn oven on; turn OFF convection fan (need static heat).
5. Preheat duration depends on oven type.

---

## CHAPTER 6: MAKING PIZZA DOUGH — Method & Technique (pp. 12–24)

### Triangle Test (p.12)
Statistical tasting method: give each of ≥10 tasters 3 samples (2 identical + 1 different), randomize order across tasters (AAB, ABA, BAA...), have them ID the odd one out. Max 2 sets (6 samples) per session to avoid taster fatigue.

### Dough Capacity by Mixer (p.12)
| Mixer size | Dough amount |
|---|---|
| 3.8–4.7 L / 4–5 qt stand mixer | 1–1.5 kg / 2.2–3.3 lb |
| 5.7–7.6 L / 6–8 qt stand mixer | 1.5–2 kg / 3.3–4.4 lb (1.5 kg max for stiff doughs like Brazilian thin-crust) |
| 11.4 L / 12 qt commercial planetary | 3–6 kg / 6.6–13.2 lb |
| 18.9 L / 20 qt commercial planetary | 6–8 kg / 13.2–17.6 lb |
| Spiral/diving-arm/fork mixer (36 L/38 qt bowl) | 8–15 kg / 17.6–33 lb (8 kg min, 15 kg max) |

*(Continued in next section — mixing methods, bulk fermentation, shaping, proofing schedules pending further extraction.)*

# Regarding Cocktails

**Author:** Sasha Petraske, with Georgette Moger-Petraske
**Forewords:** Dale DeGroff, Robert Simonson
**Publisher:** Phaidon Press, ISBN (Phaidon), hardcover
**Source file:** `Regarding Cocktails (Sasha Petraske, Georgette Moger-Petraske etc.) (z-library.sk, 1lib.sk, z-lib.sk).pdf` (264 pages, scanned/photographed edition — no text layer; content extracted via page-image transcription)

Sasha Petraske (1973–2015) founded Milk & Honey on Eldridge Street in 2000, widely credited with launching the modern "speakeasy" craft-cocktail revival, and went on to open Little Branch, Dutch Kills, Middle Branch, Lantern's Keep, Milk & Honey London, and other bars, mentoring a generation of bartenders (Sam Ross, Richard Boccato, Michael Madrusan, Toby Maloney, Chad Solomon, Theo Lieberman, Joseph Schwartz, Eric Alperin, and more). He died before finishing this book; his widow Georgette Moger-Petraske completed it posthumously, assembling his essays, house rules, and recipes alongside first-person recollections from the "Milk & Honey family" of bartenders he trained. Each recipe in the book is presented under a named contributor's byline with a short personal anecdote about Sasha, followed by the exact spec.

## Structural Overview

- **Front matter:** Foreword by Dale DeGroff; Foreword by Robert Simonson; Introduction by Georgette Moger-Petraske; a full pictogram "Legend" key (each recipe's ingredients are also shown as a graphic glass diagram with symbol-coded liquid layers)
- **Part I — Setting Up:** The Home Cocktail Bar (glassware, equipment/garnish/syrup checklists); The Home Cocktail Party (an 8-step professional guide to throwing a party — safety, capacity planning, glassware vs. plastic, menu design, bar setup, ambiance/music, ice quantities); Consider the Peacock (an essay on garnish philosophy); On Frozen Water by Richard Boccato (Sasha's technical philosophy of ice, temperature, and dilution, with his own quoted words)
- **Part II — Recipes**, organized around Sasha's teaching that nearly every cocktail is a variation of five archetypes:
  - **The Old Fashioned** (built-in-the-glass, stirred with sugar/bitters/spirit)
  - **The Martini and the Manhattan** (stirred and served up)
  - **The Sour** (shaken, citrus-based, traditional and non-traditional)
  - **The Highball** (served long, including traditional fizzes)
  - **The Fix** (cobblers, swizzles, and peasant/crushed-ice cocktails)
  - **Punches, Flips, and Dessert and Temperance Cocktails** (large-format punches, cream-based drinks, and nonalcoholic recipes)
- **Part III — Guides:** Regarding Milk & Honey House Rules (Georgette); Regarding the Home Bar (Theo Lieberman); Regarding Style (Michael Arenella); Regarding Travel (Georgette); Regarding Charity (Georgette); Regarding Posture and Etiquette (Eric Alperin); Regarding How to Be a Good Bar Regular (Dave Wondrich); Regarding Milk & Honey Comp Protocol; Regarding Cocktails for Your Cat; Milk & Honey Closing Night Menu; Index

## Philosophy and Technique Synthesis

**The five-drink taxonomy.** Sasha's core teaching organizes essentially all cocktails into five families — Old Fashioned, Martini/Manhattan, Sour, Highball, Fix — a structure the book itself adopts as its table of contents. Learning to build within these five templates, rather than memorizing individual recipes, was his method for training new bartenders quickly.

**Precision and measurement.** Every recipe uses exact jigger measurements (down to ⅛ oz / ~4 ml), and Sasha was known for obsessive iteration — Matt Clark recounts spending "an hour and a half adjusting [The Sherpa] incrementally in different directions" only to land back on the first version tried. The lesson: exactness in measurement is what separates a "right" drink from a merely close one, because ingredient variation (a lime's acidity, a peach's ripeness) means even a "correct" recipe needs to be tasted and adjusted every single time.

**Ice and dilution (Richard Boccato's essay, quoting Sasha directly).** Key principles:
- Ice must be *dry* before use — most cooling energy transfers at the phase change (ice→water); wet ice adds water on top of ice, throwing off the intended dilution.
- Shake with large-format ice for a longer shake time (more chilling, better ice-crystal "head" on a strained drink) — the big cube must eventually break apart during the shake, or the drink stays underchilled ("thukka thukka thukka" = bad).
- A frozen spirit + 4 Kold-Draft cubes shaken briefly can replicate a fully-diluted Daiquiri.
- Target serving temperature: roughly ‑4°C (25°F), not the unrealistic ‑8°C often cited — every glass shape/size has its own "sweet spot" temperature that holds for about 20 minutes.
- Reject "dilution" as a vague term; think instead in final **ABV%** — total alcohol volume ÷ finished liquid volume. Example given: a shaken Daiquiri with 2 oz (60 ml) 80-proof rum, ~3.75 oz pre-shake volume, and 4.5 oz post-shake volume works out to 0.8 ÷ 4.5 = ~17% ABV, not the commonly cited "25–30% water from ice" rule of thumb.
- Drinks should be built/shaken/stirred to hit a specific **washline** (the gap left between liquid and rim) appropriate to the glass, never poured to the brim, and never with residual liquid left behind in the shaker/mixing glass ("always good to the last drop").

**Assembly order and jiggering (from Dale DeGroff's foreword, describing Sasha's early self-training):** build sour ingredient → sweet ingredient → dashes/splashes → base spirit → ice, always with a jigger (never free-poured), and always with all-metal Boston shaker sets (cited as Sasha's innovation that changed the entire bar-tool industry toward metal-on-metal shakers).

**Garnish philosophy ("Consider the Peacock").** Distinguishes *garnish-as-ingredient* (an Old Fashioned's citrus oils, a Julep's mint — essential, never omit) from *pure ornament* (candied ginger, a raspberry on a Fix) — for the latter, poor quality or wilted presentation is read as a warning sign that other corners are being cut, but a bartender should skip or substitute an ornamental garnish rather than serve a shoddy one; "it is better to garnish a Mojito with a lime wedge than with a sad sprig of mint."

**House Rules (from Milk & Honey, cast in bronze on the bathroom doors of Sasha's bars):**
1. No name-dropping, no star f*cking.
2. No hooting, hollering, shouting, or other loud behavior.
3. No fighting, no play fighting, no talking about fighting.
4. Gentlemen will remove their hats. Hooks are provided.
5. Gentlemen will not introduce themselves to ladies. Ladies, feel free to start a conversation or ask the bartender to introduce you. If a man you don't know speaks to you, please lift your chin slightly and ignore him.
6. Do not linger outside the front door.
7. Do not bring anyone unless you would leave that person alone in your home. You are responsible for the behavior of your guests.
8. Exit the bar briskly and silently. People are trying to sleep across the street. Please make all your travel plans and say all farewells before leaving the bar.

**On personal conduct.** Sasha's own maxims, quoted throughout the Guides section, include "Being early is not a waste of time," "Eat before you are starving," and "Remember things always take longer than you think they will." He held any piece of glassware at its lowest point, insisted bartenders wash their hands the instant they touched their face or hair during service, and co-founded the not-for-profit San Antonio Cocktail Conference (with Scott Becker), which channeled 100% of profits to children's charities (HeartGift, ChildSafe, Transplants for Children, The Children's Shelter) under the slogan "Pouring Our Hearts Out."

## Component Library (referenced throughout every chapter)

- **Simple Syrup** — 1:2 sugar:water by volume (½ cup/100 g sugar : ½ cup/120 ml water), never boiled (boiling evaporates water and skews the ratio)
- **Violette Syrup** — 2 oz gin : 1 oz violet syrup (e.g. Monin) : 1 oz Simple Syrup
- **Ginger Syrup** — equal parts fresh ginger juice and superfine sugar
- **Honey Syrup** — 1 cup honey : ⅓ cup hot water
- **Orgeat Syrup** — toasted slivered almonds, sugar, water, cooked into a syrup, then finished with vodka and orange flower water, strained through a jelly bag
- **Mineral Saline** — ½ cup kosher salt dissolved in 4½ cups warmed mineral water
- **A.P.P. Bitters** (house blend) — equal parts Angostura, Peychaud's, and Dale DeGroff's Pimento bitters
- **House Orange Bitters** — equal parts Fee Brothers West Indian Orange bitters and Regan's No. 6
- **White Peach Purée** — 1 white peach (skin on) blended with 1 oz fresh Meyer lemon juice and 1 oz Simple Syrup, strained

## Recipe Sample — Organized by Chapter

### The Old Fashioned

**American Trilogy** (whiskey glass) — 1 small brown sugar cube saturated with 2–4 dashes orange bitters, muddled with a bar-spoon splash of club soda into a paste; add 1 oz (30 ml) rye whiskey, 1 oz (30 ml) bonded applejack, and 1 large ice cube. Stir 10–15 times. Garnish with an orange twist.

**Home on the Range** (rocks glass) — Build over 1 large ice cube: 3 dashes Angostura bitters, ¼ oz (7.5 ml) sweet vermouth (preferably Cocchi Vermouth di Torino), ¼ oz (7.5 ml) Cointreau, 2 oz (60 ml) bourbon. Stir a few times. Garnish with a lemon twist and an orange twist.

**The Sherpa** (whiskey glass) — 2 dashes orange bitters, ¼ oz (7.5 ml) Curaçao, ¼ oz (7.5 ml) St. Elizabeth Allspice Dram, 2 oz (60 ml) bourbon (preferably Elijah Craig 12-year), built over 1 large ice cube, stirred a few times. Garnish with a lemon twist. (Designed as a slow-sipping drink, kept low on water content.)

**Si-Güey** (whiskey glass) — Build over 1 large ice cube: 3 dashes orange bitters, ¼ oz (7.5 ml) Curaçao, 2 oz (60 ml) tequila reposado. Stir, then finish with a ¼ oz (7.5 ml) float of Islay whisky.

**Tattletale** (room-temperature whiskey glass) — 3 dashes Angostura bitters, 1 bar spoon honey, 1¼ oz (37.5 ml) Highland Scotch, ¾ oz (22 ml) Islay whisky, built over 1 rock ice cube and stirred 5–6 times. Garnish with lemon and orange twists. (A smoky riff on an Old Fashioned, using honey instead of sugar — created by Sam Ross for Sasha, who hated smoky Scotch but loved this.)

**.38 Special** (coupe) — 2⅜ oz (67.5 ml) blended Scotch, ⅜ oz (11 ml) yellow Chartreuse, ⅜ oz (11 ml) Amaro CioCiaro, stirred with ice and strained. Garnish with a lemon twist.

### The Martini and the Manhattan

**Martini** — 1 oz (30 ml) dry vermouth, 2 oz (60 ml) gin (a wet 2:1 ratio — Sasha's house Martini was notably wetter than convention), stirred in a frozen mixing glass filled with ice, strained into a chilled coupe. Garnish with an olive, cocktail onion, or lemon twist. (Petraske-house stirred drinks are built to a 3-oz total liquid volume.)

**Red Hook** — 2 oz (60 ml) rye whiskey, ½ oz (15 ml) Maraschino liqueur (preferably Luxardo), ½ oz (15 ml) Punt e Mes vermouth, stirred with ice, strained into a chilled coupe. (Created by Vincenzo Errico at Milk & Honey in 2003 — the first of the "Brooklyn neighborhood" Manhattan variations.)

**Bensonhurst** — 1 oz (30 ml) dry vermouth (preferably Dolin), 1 tsp (5 ml) Cynar, 2 tsp (10 ml) Maraschino liqueur (preferably Luxardo), 2 oz (60 ml) rye whiskey (preferably Rittenhouse), stirred in a frozen mixing glass, strained into a frozen coupe.

**Cobble Hill** — 3 thin cucumber slices gently muddled in a mixing glass; add 2 oz (60 ml) rye whiskey, ½ oz (15 ml) Amaro Montenegro, ½ oz (15 ml) dry vermouth, fill with ice, stir 30 seconds, strain into a chilled coupe. Garnish with the remaining cucumber slice.

**Deep Blue Sea** — 2 oz (60 ml) gin, ¾ oz (22 ml) Cocchi Americano, ¼ oz (7.5 ml) Violette Syrup, 2 dashes orange bitters, stirred with ice, strained into a frozen coupe. Garnish with a lemon twist.

**Fallback Cocktail** — 2 dashes Peychaud's bitters, ½ oz (15 ml) Amaro Nonino, ½ oz (15 ml) Carpano Antica, 1 oz (30 ml) applejack, 1 oz (30 ml) rye whiskey, built over 1 large ice cube in a whiskey glass and stirred. Garnish with an orange twist.

**Gin & It** — 2 oz (60 ml) gin, 1 oz (30 ml) sweet vermouth, stirred with ice, strained into a chilled coupe, finished with a lemon twist (Sasha and Georgette drank this at a strict 2:1 ratio and served it as a wedding favor batched in Mason jars).

**Left Hand** — 1½ oz (45 ml) bourbon, ¾ oz (22 ml) sweet vermouth, ¾ oz (22 ml) Campari, 3 dashes Bittermen's chocolate bitters, stirred 30 seconds with ice, strained into a chilled coupe. Garnish with a brandied cherry. (A bourbon-based Negroni riff predating the Boulevardier's revival.)

**Savoir Faire** — ¼ oz (7.5 ml) Avèze, ¼ oz (7.5 ml) dry vermouth (preferably Dolin), 2 oz (60 ml) gin, stirred with cracked ice in a chilled mixing glass; strain into a coupe lightly sprayed with orange blossom water; finish with a lemon twist rubbed on the rim then discarded.

**Spring Forward** — 2 oz (60 ml) gin, 1 oz (30 ml) dry vermouth, 2 spring onions or ramps (trimmed) — muddle 1 onion briefly with the gin/vermouth in a frozen mixing glass (overmuddling turns it bitter), fill with ice, stir, strain into a chilled coupe. Garnish with the remaining onion.

### The Sour

**Daiquiri** — ⅞ oz to 1 oz (26–30 ml) fresh lime juice (to taste — Sasha often used ⅞ oz since a full ounce can run too tart depending on the fruit), ¾ oz (22 ml) Simple Syrup, 2 oz (60 ml) white rum, shaken hard with a 2-inch ice cube, strained into a chilled coupe. (Called by Abraham Hawkins "the test drink for anyone who wants to see what a bar or bartender is all about.")

**Bee's Knees** — 2 oz (60 ml) gin, 1 oz (30 ml) fresh lemon juice, ¾ oz (22 ml) Honey Syrup, shaken with ice, strained into a chilled coupe. (Prohibition-era drink; honey and lemon originally masked bathtub gin.)

**The Business** — 2 oz (60 ml) gin, 1 oz (30 ml) fresh lime juice, ¾ oz (22 ml) Honey Syrup, shaken with 1 large ice cube, strained into a chilled coupe. (A wordplay variant of the Bee's Knees — say the two names back to back.)

**Apple Jack** — ½ oz (15 ml) fresh lemon juice, ½ oz (15 ml) Simple Syrup, ½ oz (15 ml) apple cider, 1½ oz (45 ml) applejack, shaken vigorously with ice, strained into a chilled coupe.

**Caipirinha** — 6 lime wedges, 2 oz (60 ml) cachaça, ¾ oz (22 ml) Simple Syrup, 1 white sugar cube, all muddled in a shaker, filled with cracked ice and shaken, strained into a chilled double rocks glass over fresh cracked ice.

**Calvados 75** — 1 oz (30 ml) Calvados or applejack, ½ oz (15 ml) fresh lemon juice, ½ oz (15 ml) Simple Syrup, shaken with cracked ice, strained into a Collins glass half-filled with cracked ice, topped with Champagne or cava.

**Champs-Élysées** — 2 oz (60 ml) Cognac, ¾ oz (22 ml) fresh lemon juice, ½ oz (15 ml) green Chartreuse, ¼ oz (7.5 ml) Simple Syrup, shaken with ice, strained into a chilled coupe. Garnish with a lemon twist. (A Sidecar riff swapping orange Curaçao for green Chartreuse.)

**Cosmonaut** — 2 oz (60 ml) gin, ¾ oz (22 ml) fresh lemon juice, 1 heaping bar spoon raspberry preserves, shaken with ice, strained into a chilled coupe. (Named as a jab at the Cosmopolitan's popularity; a variation on the 1930s Marmalade Cocktail.)

**Turnpike** — ¾ oz (22 ml) applejack, ¾ oz (22 ml) fresh lemon juice, ¾ oz (22 ml) Simple Syrup, 1 oz (30 ml) rye whiskey (preferably Monongahela), shaken with ice, strained into a chilled coupe. Garnish with a lemon wedge.

**Water Lily** — ¾ oz (22 ml) orange liqueur (Cointreau), ¾ oz (22 ml) Violette Syrup, ¾ oz (22 ml) fresh lemon juice, ¾ oz (22 ml) gin, shaken with ice, strained into a coupe. Garnish with a lemon twist. (Created for Georgette; named for her middle name, Lillian.)

### The Highball

**Gin & Tonic** — 2 oz (60 ml) gin poured over 1 large ice cube in a double Old Fashioned glass, served with a full 6 oz (180 ml) bottle of tonic water on the side and a lime wedge, so the guest controls dilution.

**Grapefruit Collins** — 2 oz (60 ml) gin, ¾ oz (22 ml) fresh lemon juice, ¾ oz (22 ml) Simple Syrup shaken with ice, strained into a Collins glass over 1 large Collins ice cube, topped with 1 oz (30 ml) fresh grapefruit juice and ½ oz (15 ml) club soda. Garnish with a grapefruit twist.

**Bicycle Thief** — 1 oz (30 ml) gin, 1 oz (30 ml) Campari, 1½ oz (45 ml) fresh grapefruit juice, ½ oz (15 ml) fresh lemon juice, ½ oz (15 ml) Simple Syrup, built in a highball glass over a long Collins cube or 3 medium ice cubes, topped with club soda. Garnish with a thin orange slice. (A Tarzan Cocktail variant swaps in fresh pineapple juice for the grapefruit.)

**Bottlerocket** — 2 oz (60 ml) tequila blanco, ¾ oz (22 ml) fresh lime juice, ¾ oz (22 ml) Honey Syrup, one ¼-inch (5 mm) jalapeño slice, shaken with ice, strained into a Collins glass over ice and topped with club soda.

**French 75** — 1 oz (30 ml) Cognac (or gin, "if requested"), ½ oz (15 ml) fresh lemon juice, ½ oz (15 ml) Simple Syrup, shaken with a small piece of cracked ice, strained into the small half of the shaker and topped with Champagne directly in the shaker, then "rolled" into a chilled flute or coupe. Garnish with a lemon twist.

**Hayes Fizz** — ¾ oz (22 ml) fresh lemon juice, ¾ oz (22 ml) Simple Syrup, 2 oz (60 ml) gin, shaken with 1 large ice cube; a Collins glass is rinsed with absinthe/Pernod, filled with a Collins ice cube, and the shaken drink is strained in, topped with 2 oz (60 ml) club soda. Garnish with an orange slice and a cocktail cherry. (Named for regular Reed Hayes, who added his own absinthe "offerings" to his Tom Collins.)

**Tritter Collins** — 2 oz (60 ml) gin, ¾ oz (22 ml) fresh lemon juice, ¾ oz (22 ml) Simple Syrup, ¼ oz (7.5 ml) absinthe, shaken with ice, strained into a Collins glass over a long ice cube, topped with 1 oz (30 ml) grapefruit juice and ½ oz (15 ml) club soda. Garnish with a grapefruit twist. (A Grapefruit Collins plus a dash of absinthe.)

**Tritter Rickey** — 2 oz (60 ml) gin, 1 oz (30 ml) fresh lime juice, ¾ oz (22 ml) Simple Syrup, shaken with ice; a Collins glass is coated with an absinthe rinse and filled with ice, the drink strained in, topped with club soda. Garnish with a fresh mint sprig.

### The Fix (Cobblers, Swizzles, Peasant Cocktails)

**Mojito** — 8–10 fresh mint leaves, 1 oz (30 ml) fresh lime juice, ¾ oz (22 ml) Simple Syrup, 1 brown sugar cube, gently muddled in a shaker; add 2 oz (60 ml) white rum, pour into a chilled rocks glass, top with crushed ice. Garnish with a mint bouquet. (Adapted from a 1931 Sloppy Joe's Bar, Havana recipe; the book traces its lineage back to "El Draque," a 16th-century precursor named for Sir Francis Drake.)

**Eskimo's Kiss** (double Mojito) — 10–12 fresh mint leaves, 2 Demerara sugar cubes, 2 oz (60 ml) fresh lime juice, 1½ oz (45 ml) Simple Syrup muddled together, then 4 oz (120 ml) white rum added and swirled in; poured into an imperial pint glass filled with crushed ice, garnished with a mint bouquet, served with two straws. (Sasha's own personal drink of choice, always doubled.)

**Queen's Park Swizzle (Dark)** — 8–10 fresh mint leaves, ¾ oz (22 ml) Demerara Simple Syrup, 1 oz (30 ml) fresh lime juice, gently muddled; add 2 oz (60 ml) overproof (80-proof) Demerara rum, transfer to a chilled Collins glass with mint settled at the bottom, fill ¾ with crushed ice and swizzle, add 4–6 dashes Angostura bitters, swizzle again for a tricolor effect, top with more crushed ice. Garnish with a mint bouquet.

**Queen's Park Swizzle (Light)** — Same build, but 1 brown sugar cube instead of Demerara syrup base measure adjustments, 2 oz (30 ml as printed — likely 2 oz) white rum (preferably Flor de Caña 4-Year), and 4–5 dashes Peychaud's bitters instead of Angostura for a red (rather than dark) float.

**Maloney Park Swizzle** — Same swizzle technique with 10–12 mint leaves, ¾ oz (22 ml) lime juice, ¾ oz (22 ml) Simple Syrup, 2 oz (60 ml) rum (preferably Matusalem Classico), and 5–6 dashes Peychaud's bitters swizzled to a "tricolor" float. (Named after Toby Maloney by Sasha.)

**Asbury Park Swizzle** — 3 mint sprigs, ¾ oz (22 ml) fresh lemon juice, ¾ oz (22 ml) Honey Syrup, ¼ oz (7.5 ml) apple liqueur (Berentzen's), 2 oz (60 ml) white applejack (Laird's Jersey Lightning), 2 drops Mineral Saline, 4–5 dashes A.P.P. Bitters, 1 dash House Orange Bitters — 2 mint sprigs muddled with lemon/honey/apple liqueur, applejack and saline swirled in, poured into a Collins glass with crushed ice, bitters swizzled into a red top layer, more ice added in a cone. (A Queen's-Park-Swizzle riff built around New Jersey's Laird's applejack, honoring Bruce Springsteen's *Greetings from Asbury Park*.)

**Bin & Gitters** — ¾ oz (22 ml) Simple Syrup, 1 oz (30 ml) fresh lime juice, 2 oz (60 ml) gin, shaken and strained into a chilled double rocks glass over crushed ice (¾ up the glass), with a dash of Angostura bitters swizzled in to create a visible color layer; topped with more crushed ice in a cone, served with a straw, no garnish ("no garnish for a bartender"). Effectively "a gimlet on crushed ice with a bitters float," per Sasha's own description.

**Strawberry Fix** — 1–2 hulled, quartered strawberries muddled gently in a chilled double rocks glass to form a "jam" layer; glass filled with crushed ice. Separately shake ¾ oz (22 ml) fresh lemon juice, ¾ oz (22 ml) Simple Syrup, 2 oz (60 ml) bourbon, and pour over the crushed ice. Garnish with a whole strawberry, serve with a straw.

### Punches, Flips, and Dessert/Temperance Cocktails

**Café con Leche Flip** — 1 oz (30 ml) Cruzan blackstrap rum, 1 oz (30 ml) coffee liqueur (Café Lolita), ¾ oz (22 ml) Simple Syrup, ¾ oz (22 ml) heavy cream, 1 egg yolk (medium egg) — shaken first without ice to emulsify, then shaken vigorously with ice, strained into a chilled sour glass. Garnish with freshly grated nutmeg.

**Dominicana** — 1½ oz (45 ml) coffee liqueur (Café Lolita), 1½ oz (45 ml) aged Dominican rum, stirred with ice, strained into a chilled coupe, floated with a thin layer of fresh whipped cream. (One of the very first drinks Sasha created at Milk & Honey.)

**Croque M. Petraske / Croque Mme Moger** — the house cocktail-hour aperitif ritual: "Monsieur" = 1½ oz (45 ml) Campari + 4 oz (120 ml) club soda over 2 medium ice cubes in a frozen highball glass; "Madame" = the same, plus 1 oz (30 ml) gin (preferably Plymouth), garnished with an orange twist if available.

**Faker Face** — ¾ oz (22 ml) fresh lime juice, ¾ oz (22 ml) grenadine syrup, 1 oz (30 ml) fresh orange juice, shaken with ice, strained into a coupe and topped with club soda. Garnish with a large blackberry balanced on two toothpicks across the rim.

**House Ginger Beer** (temperance/nonalcoholic) — 1 oz (30 ml) fresh lemon juice, 1 oz (30 ml) fresh pineapple juice, 1 oz (30 ml) Ginger Syrup, 1 bar spoon maple syrup, a dash of Angostura bitters, shaken vigorously, combined with 3 oz (90 ml) club soda and poured into a Collins glass filled with ice cubes. (Sasha's formula for temperance drinks generally: double the non-alcoholic ingredients of an existing cocktail template — Gimlet, Collins, Buck — to build a satisfying alcohol-free base.)

**Wedding Punch** (large format, makes ~2½ gallons/10.75 L) — 1 (750 ml) bottle elderflower liqueur (St-Germain), 4 (750 ml) bottles dry vermouth (Martini Bianco), 8–10 (750 ml) bottles prosecco, 8 oz (230 g) assorted berries, 1 orange sliced into wheels, 2 lemons sliced into wheels, 2 limes sliced into wheels — combined in a punch bowl large enough for an 8-inch (20 cm) ice block, garnished with the fruit. (Served at the Petraskes' own wedding.)

# Spritz: Italy's Most Iconic Aperitivo Cocktail, with Recipes

**Authors:** Talia Baiocchi & Leslie Pariseau
**Photography:** Dylan + Jeni | **Illustrations:** Matthew Allen
**Publisher:** Bantam Press (UK edition referenced)
**Source file:** `Spritz Italy's most iconic aperitivo cocktail, with recipes (Baiocchi, Talia, author) (z-library.sk, 1lib.sk, z-lib.sk).pdf` (text-layer PDF; extracted via `pdftotext -layout`)

Talia Baiocchi (founder/editor of *PUNCH*) and Leslie Pariseau co-wrote this cultural and recipe history of the spritz, built around a reporting trip across northern Italy (Venice, Milan, Turin) interviewing bitters producers, prosecco makers, bar owners, and drinks historians. The book blends deep-dive cultural history (Greek/Roman wine-dilution rituals, Habsburg-era Austrian soldiers, the rise of Aperol and Select, the Futurist cocktail movement, the 1990s prosecco-and-ice revolution) with a curated recipe collection split into Classic, Modern, and Cousin categories, plus an aperitivo food chapter.

## Structural Overview

- **Introduction** — the etymology of *sprezzatura*, the authors' personal relationship to the drink, and the book's mission to explain the spritz as more than a recipe
- **A Spritz Is Born** — full history section:
  - Greek/Roman wine-and-water "proto-spritzing" (Falernian, Setine, Mulsum, Conditum, Rosatum)
  - "Water Into Wine": the Habsburg/Austrian-soldier origin legend, the German root *spritzen* ("to spray")
  - "The Rise of the Italian Bitter": Turin's 19th-century café culture, Gaspare Campari and Alessandro Martini's beginnings, "Aperitivi 101" sidebar (bitter liqueurs vs. aromatized wines: vermouth, chinato/quinquina, Americano)
  - Sidebar: "The Cocktails of the Italian Futurists" (1909–1944 Futurist mixology/*polibibita*, categorizing drinks as "war in bed" vs. "peace in bed")
  - "Bitter Into Wine": Aperol (1919) and Select (1920) enter the market; regional bitter loyalties (Select in Venice, Aperol in Padua, Cappelletti in Brescia)
  - The 1990s shift to prosecco-and-ice on the beaches around Venice (Bar Capannina, Lido di Jesolo) and Aperol's 1990s–2007 marketing campaign that turned a regional ritual into "Aperol Spritz," a global phenomenon
  - Sidebar: "1980s Flashback: The White Wine Spritzer" (the unrelated American diet-culture drink)
- **The Spritz Life** — the aperitivo ritual as social institution; a first-person "Spritz Trail" through Venice (bacari, *giro di ombre* — rounds of drinks), Padua, Treviso, Brescia (local dialect name *pirlo*), Alto Adige (Hugo Spritz/*acqua santa*), Milan (Bar Basso, birthplace of the Negroni Sbagliato), and Turin
- **Spritz Cocktails** (the recipe chapter) — opens with "How to Spritz," three defining rules, then:
  - Reference sections: **Prosecco 101** (Charmat method vs. *col fondo*; producer recommendations — Nino Franco, Sorelle Bronca, Adami); **Building a Spritz Bar** (bubbles, bitter aperitivo liqueurs/wines, vermouth/sherry/table wine, fruit liqueurs/syrups/shrubs/infusions); a syrup formulary (Ginger, Grape, Honey, Lemon, Raspberry, Simple, Vanilla Syrup, and a Cranberry Shrub)
  - **Classic** recipes (the traditional regional spritz canon)
  - **Modern** recipes (bartender-created riffs from craft cocktail bars worldwide)
  - **Cousins** (spritz-adjacent low-alcohol, bubbly, bitter drinks that diverge from the core formula — Americano-family drinks, fizzes, coolers)
- **The Aperitivo Table** — a food chapter on regional aperitivo snacks: Venetian *cicchetti*, Turin's buffet culture, Milan's *apericena*; recipes for Sarde in Saor, Tramezzini (including Caffè Florian's version and an Italian Tuna Salad filling), an "Aperitivo Essenziale" snack spread (Oven-Roasted Olives, Saffron Almonds, Hand-Cut Potato Chips), Grissini with Prosciutto and Pickled Radicchio, and Carciofi alla Veneziana (Venetian-style artichokes)

## Philosophy and Technique Synthesis

**The three rules of "How to Spritz."** The authors distill the entire category to three non-negotiables:
1. **Always effervescent** — via soda water, prosecco, another sparkling wine, or a flavoured soda; a spritz without bubble isn't a spritz.
2. **Low in alcohol** — no more than about 1 oz of strong spirits (preferably less); it's a drink for the liminal hour between work and play, not for getting drunk.
3. **A pre-dinner drink** — meant to be bitter, opening the stomach for a meal, consumed in the specific window between work and play (the *aperitivo* hour).

**The archetypal ratio.** The "spritz archetype" that emerged by the late 20th century is roughly **3 parts prosecco : 2 parts bitter liqueur : 1 part soda**, built over ice, always assembled in the same order in Venice — ice first, then prosecco, then bitters, then soda — a sequence the authors describe as "not a joking matter" regionally.

**Regional identity is embedded in the bitter.** Which bitter liqueur goes into the spritz is a matter of local allegiance almost like soccer-club loyalty: Select in Venice, Aperol in Padua, Cappelletti Aperitivo (aka Specialino) in Brescia, and so on. The book treats this variability as a feature, not a bug — a way of reading a region's identity through its glass.

**Category logic (Classic / Modern / Cousins).**
- *Classic* = the traditional canon along the actual "spritz trail" (Trieste to Turin): wine or prosecco + soda + citrus + a bitter (Aperol, Campari, or sometimes an amaro like Cynar), always finished with a signature regional garnish.
- *Modern* = bartender-created riffs (from bars like Attaboy, Death & Co, Nitecap, The NoMad, Maison Premiere) that keep the classic template's proportions and spirit but substitute in craft ingredients — fresh juices, housemade syrups/shrubs, infused vermouths, unconventional bitters.
- *Cousins* = drinks that share the spritz's bitter/bubbly/low-alcohol DNA but diverge structurally — using egg whites, beer, muddled fruit, or a Collins/sour build instead of the straight three-ingredient assembly (the Americano and its riffs, Sgroppino, various fizzes).

**Substitution philosophy.** The authors explicitly encourage flexibility: swap Cocchi Americano for bianco vermouth, lime juice for lemon juice, tonic water for quinquina wine, etc. — "the true spirit of the spritz requires a bit of homegrown curiosity."

**Prosecco quality matters.** Since prosecco typically makes up the bulk of a spritz's volume, the book insists that "the better the prosecco, the better the drink," recommending Charmat-method producers (Nino Franco's Rustico, Sorelle Bronca's Extra Dry/Brut, Adami's Garbèl and Bosco di Gica) and flagging the more traditional, lees-retaining *col fondo* style (Casa Coste Piane, Ca dei Zago, Bele Casel, Zanotto) as a more serious, avant-garde expression of the region.

## Component Library (Syrups & Shrubs, referenced across the recipe chapter)

- **Simple Syrup** — 1 cup sugar : 1 cup water, heated on very low heat until dissolved (never boiled), rested 30 minutes
- **Honey Syrup** — 1 cup honey : ¼ cup water, warmed until dissolved
- **Ginger Syrup** — ½ cup fresh ginger juice : ½ cup sugar, warmed until dissolved
- **Lemon Syrup** — 1 cup fresh lemon juice : 1 cup sugar
- **Raspberry Syrup** — 1 cup raspberries, 2 cups sugar, 1 cup water, crushed and heated gently (not boiled)
- **Grape Syrup** — 2 cups purple slipskin grapes, 1 cup sugar, 1 cup water, crushed and simmered
- **Vanilla Syrup** — 1 cup sugar, 1 cup water, 1 split vanilla pod, simmered together
- **Cranberry Shrub** — 1 cup apple cider vinegar, 1 cup water, 1 cup sugar, ¼ cup frozen cranberries, simmered 10 minutes, crushing the berries

## Recipe Sample — Organized by Chapter

### Classic

**Venetian Spritz** (rocks or wine glass; garnish: olive + orange half-wheel) — 2 oz bitter liqueur (Aperol or, more traditionally, Select), 3–4 oz prosecco, 2 oz soda water, built over ice. *Note: for a more bracingly bitter version, split the bitter liqueur 1:1 Aperol/Campari.*

**Negroni Sbagliato** (rocks glass; garnish: orange half-wheel) — 1 oz Campari, 1 oz sweet vermouth, 3 oz prosecco, built over ice. (Claimed birthplace: Bar Basso, Milan, invented "by accident" in the early 1970s by Mirko Stocchetto, substituting prosecco for gin.)

**White Spritz / Spritz Liscio** (wine glass; garnish: seasonal citrus, herbs, or fruit) — 4 oz dry white wine, 2 oz soda water, an optional splash (~¼–½ oz) of homemade Lemon Syrup or fruit liqueur, built over ice.

**Bicicletta** (wine glass; garnish: lemon half-wheel) — 1–2 oz Campari, 3 oz white wine, soda water to top, built over ice. (Lombardia, 1930s; named for the bicycle its drinkers wobble home on.)

### Modern

**Rosé All Day** (Gabriel Orta & Elad Zvi, Broken Shaker, Miami) — 2 oz rosé, 1 oz Cocchi Americano, 1 oz Papaya Shrub, ½ oz fresh lemon juice, shaken and strained into a wine glass over ice, topped with 1 oz prosecco. *Papaya Shrub: 5–8 chunks papaya, 1 cup rice wine vinegar, ¼ cup sugar, simmered 20 minutes.*

**Tarocco Spritz** (rocks glass; garnish: blood orange wheel) — 1 oz gin, ½ oz Cappelletti, ½ oz fresh blood orange juice, ½ oz fresh lemon juice, ½ oz Vanilla Syrup, shaken with ice, strained over fresh ice, topped with 2 oz prosecco.

**The Rib Tickler** (Alex Day, Nitecap, NYC) — 2 oz Dolin dry vermouth, ½ oz Suze, ½ oz St-Germain, ½ oz fresh lemon juice, ½ oz Simple Syrup, built in a wine glass over ice, topped with soda water. (A bubbly riff on the White Negroni.)

**Viking Culture** (Natasha David, created for Sunshine Co., Brooklyn) — 2 oz Caraway-Infused Cocchi Americano, ½ oz fresh lemon juice, ½ oz Simple Syrup, 1 tablespoon peach conserve (preferably Bonne Maman), shaken with ice, double-strained into a coupe, topped with sparkling wine. *Caraway-Infused Cocchi Americano: 2 tablespoons caraway seeds infused in a 750 ml bottle of Cocchi Americano for 2 hours, strained.*

**Alpine Spritz** (double rocks or wine glass; garnish: rosemary sprig) — ¾ oz Braulio, 1 oz Riesling, ½ oz Grape Syrup, 3 oz Crémant d'Alsace, built over ice.

**Nero Chinato Spritz** (Daniel Zacharczuk, The Varnish, LA) — 4 blackberries muddled in a Collins glass; add ice, ¾ oz fresh lemon juice, 2½ oz Cocchi Americano; top with equal parts Champagne/sparkling wine and soda water; garnish with 2–3 skewered blackberries.

**Grab Orange** (Matthew Biancaniello, LA) — 3 oz sparkling dry rosé, ½ oz Cappelletti, 1 oz Reduced Blood Orange Juice, built over ice in a flute or wine glass. *Reduced Blood Orange Juice: 1.1 litres blood orange juice simmered down to 1 cup.*

**Punch House Spritz** — 2 oz Cocchi Americano, 4 oz Lini Lambrusco Rosato, 1 oz fresh grapefruit juice, 1 oz soda water, built over ice. *Large-batch: 3 cups lambrusco rosé, 1½ cups Cocchi Americano, ⅔ cup grapefruit juice in a pitcher, divided among 4–6 glasses, topped with 1–2 oz soda water each.*

**Appennine Spritz** (Adam Bernbach, 2 Birds 1 Stone, Washington DC) — 1 oz Cocchi Barolo Chinato, 1 oz Cappelletti, 2 oz cava (if substituting prosecco, add ½ oz lemon juice), built over ice.

**Hugo Spritz** — ½ oz St-Germain and a mint sprig muddled together in a wine glass, rested 3 minutes; add ice, 4 oz prosecco, 1 oz soda water; stir gently. Garnish with mint sprig and lemon wheel. (South Tyrol; the traditional local base is *sciroppo di sambuco*/elderflower syrup, called *acqua santa*.)

**Amarena Spritz** — 2–3 brandied Amarena cherries (or 3 fresh stoned cherries) muddled with 1 teaspoon high-quality balsamic vinegar in a Collins or rocks glass; add 1 oz Punt e Mes, 1 oz Carpano Bianco, ice; top with 2 oz prosecco and 2 oz soda water.

**Byrrh It's Cold Outside** (Natasha David, Nitecap) — ¾ oz Byrrh Grand Quinquina, ¾ oz Lustau Amontillado sherry, ½ oz Ancho-Infused Moscatel sherry, ½ oz Cranberry Shrub, ½ oz fresh lemon juice, shaken briefly with ice, strained over crushed ice into a Collins glass, topped with 1½ oz sparkling wine.

**Mai Tai Spritz** (Martin Cate, Smuggler's Cove, San Francisco) — ½ oz fresh lime juice, ½ oz orgeat, ½ oz Pierre Ferrand Dry Curaçao, 1 oz Denizen Merchant's Reserve rum (or Appleton Estate Reserve) combined and chilled 1–2 hours; poured into a Collins glass filled with ice and topped with 4 oz chilled Brut Champagne.

**Spritzz** (Jim Meehan, PDT, NYC — created for Caffè della Posta, Bolgheri, Italy, 2011) — 2 oz Pellegrino Aranciata, ½ oz Zwack amaro, 2 oz prosecco, built over ice in a rocks glass.

**Viva Alberti** (Will Elliott, Maison Premiere, Brooklyn) — 1 oz Strega, ½ oz Cognac, 1 teaspoon Cappelletti, ½ oz fresh lemon juice, 3 dashes Angostura orange bitters, stirred with cracked ice in a wine glass, topped with high-quality sparkling wine.

**Wildair Spritz** (Jorge Riera, Wildair, NYC) — 2 oz Mauro Vergano Americano over 3 ice cubes in a wine glass, topped with Les Capriades Pet'Sec (or a col-fondo-style prosecco as substitute).

**Everything's Coming Up Rose** (Natasha David, Nitecap) — 2 oz dry rosé, 1 oz Lillet Rosé, 1 oz hibiscus tea, ½ oz fresh lemon juice, ½ oz Simple Syrup, ¼ oz Aperol, poured over ice in a wine glass, topped with more ice and 1 oz sparkling wine. Garnish with lemon wheel, grapefruit slice, and edible flowers.

**Safe Passage** (Keneniah Bystrom, Essex, Seattle) — 1 oz Amaro Nardini, ½ oz Aperol, ½ oz fresh lemon juice, ⅛ oz Castelvetrano olive brine, shaken with ice, strained into a chilled coupe, topped with 2½ oz prosecco, garnished with 2 Castelvetrano olives.

**Aperol Betty** (Terroni, Los Angeles) — 2 oz Aperol, 1 oz fresh orange juice, ½ oz fresh grapefruit juice, shaken with ice, strained over ice into a rocks or Collins glass, topped with 3 oz prosecco.

### Cousins

**Americano** (Collins glass; garnish: orange half-wheel) — 1½ oz Campari, 1½ oz sweet vermouth, built over ice, topped with soda water. (First documented in Ferruccio Mazzon's *Guida al Barman*, 1920 — the precursor to the Negroni.)

**Americano Perfecto** (Damon Boelte, Grand Army Bar, Brooklyn) — 1½ oz Campari, ¾ oz Dolin Rouge, ¾ oz Carpano Antica, built over ice in a Collins glass, slowly topped with 4 oz pilsner.

**Coffee Spritz** — 2 oz soda water poured into a Collins glass; separately combine ¾ oz Averna, ½ oz aged rum (preferably Flor de Caña 12), ½ oz Vanilla Syrup, 1¼ oz cold-brewed coffee, and 1 egg white, dry-shake, then shake with ice; strain into the glass, let sit 10 seconds, top with prosecco until foam rises above the rim.

**Punch Romaine** (adapted from the *Stork Club Bar Book*; snifter or rocks glass; garnish: orange peel) — 1 egg white, 1 oz white rum, ½ oz Simple Syrup, ½ oz fresh lemon juice, 1 oz fresh orange juice, dry-shaken then shaken with ice, strained over crushed ice, topped with Champagne or sparkling wine. (Served as the sixth course of the *Titanic*'s final first-class dinner.)

**Sensa Spritz** (Collins glass; garnish: cucumber ribbon or spear) — a pinch of sea salt and ½ oz Simple Syrup muddled with 2 cucumber slices in a shaker; add ¾ oz Salers, ¾ oz Dolin dry vermouth, ½ oz fresh lime juice, shake with ice, strain into a Collins glass over ice, top with soda water.

**Shore Leave Spritz** (Matthew Belanger, Donna, Brooklyn) — 1½ oz Cocchi Americano, ½ oz Perry's Tot navy-strength gin, ¾ oz fresh lime juice, ¾ oz fresh grapefruit juice, ½ oz Honey Syrup, ½ teaspoon Vanilla Syrup, ½ teaspoon allspice dram, shaken briefly with ice, strained over crushed ice into a hurricane glass, topped with 1 oz soda water.

**Rome with a View** (Michael McIlroy, Attaboy, NYC) — 1 oz Campari, 1 oz dry vermouth, 1 oz fresh lime juice, ¾ oz Simple Syrup, shaken with ice, strained over ice into a Collins glass, topped with soda water.

**Sippy Cup** (Leo Robitschek, The NoMad, NYC) — ¾ oz Ginger Syrup, 1 oz fresh lime juice, 1 oz Cocchi Vermouth di Torino, 1½ oz Averna, shaken with ice, strained into a highball over ice; add 2 dashes Angostura bitters and top with 1 oz soda water.

**White Port & Tonic** (highball glass; garnish: lemon half-wheel and mint sprig) — 2 oz white port, 4 oz tonic water (preferably Fever Tree), ½ oz fresh lemon juice, built over ice. *Variation: muddle mint leaves with orange bitters at the bottom of the glass before topping with port and tonic.*

**Tinto de Verano** (snifter glass) — 3 oz full-bodied, dry red wine and 1 oz Lemon Syrup shaken gently with ice, poured over ice into a tumbler or wine glass, topped with soda water.

**Sgroppino** (small rocks glass; garnish: mint sprig) — 1½ oz citrus vodka (preferably St. George), ½ cup lemon sorbet, 1 oz prosecco, whipped together with a whisk in a shaker until smooth and uniform, poured into a small rocks glass.

**Mont Blanc Fizz** (Dan Sabo, Ace Hotel, LA) — 1 egg white, 1¼ oz Amaro Meletti, ½ oz Grand Marnier, ¾ oz fresh lemon juice, ¼ oz orgeat, 2 dashes Angostura bitters, dry-shaken then shaken with crushed and cubed ice until very cold; strained into a highball glass, rested 10 seconds, topped with soda water.

**Cold in the Shadows** (Pamela Wiznitzer, Seamstress, NYC) — 1 oz Campari, ½ oz St. George raspberry liqueur, ½ oz Honey Syrup, 1 oz fresh lime juice, shaken quickly with ice; add 1½ oz Anderson Valley IPA; strain over crushed ice into a highball glass.

**Diamond Spritz Fizz** (Isaac Shumway, Tosca, San Francisco) — 1½ oz Aperol, ½ oz fresh lemon juice, ½ oz Dolin dry vermouth, ½ oz Honey Syrup, ¼ oz Gran Classico bitter amaro, 1 teaspoon fresh orange juice, 1 egg white, dry-shaken then shaken very hard (20 seconds) with ice; strained over a small amount of ice into a Collins glass, slowly topped with 2½ oz sparkling wine.

**Sangrita** (adapted from the *Stork Club Bar Book*; highball glass) — 2 oz red wine, 1 oz fresh pineapple juice, ¾ oz Simple Syrup, ¾ oz fresh lime juice, built over ice, topped with soda water.

**Hungry Hungry Hipster** (Toby Maloney, The Violet Hour, Chicago) — 2 oz Zwack amaro, 1 oz fresh orange juice, ¾ oz fresh lemon juice, shaken with ice, strained over ice into a Collins glass, topped with Miller High Life.

**Pink Lemonade à la Playboy** — 2 oz rosé, 1 oz fresh lemon juice, 1 oz fresh orange juice, ½ oz rosé/pink grapefruit liqueur, built over ice in a Collins glass or large tumbler, topped with soda water.

**Tunnel Vision** (Alex Day, Death & Co, NYC) — 2 oz Basil-Infused Dolin Blanc Vermouth, ½ oz pear eau de vie, ½ oz fresh lime juice, ½ oz Simple Syrup, 2 dashes absinthe, shaken with ice, strained into a Collins glass over ice, topped with soda water. *Basil-Infused Dolin Blanc: 8–10 basil leaves infused in a 750 ml bottle for 2 hours, strained.*

**Second Serve** (Dan Greenbaum, Attaboy, NYC) — 1 oz Amaro Montenegro, 1 oz fino sherry, 1 oz fresh lime juice, ¾ oz Simple Syrup, shaken and strained into a Collins glass over ice, topped with soda water.

**Bitter Intentions** (Bobby Heugel, Anvil / The Pastry War, Houston) — 1½ oz soda water poured into a Collins glass without ice; separately shake 2 oz Campari, ¾ oz fresh lemon juice, ¾ oz Simple Syrup with ice and strain into the glass over ice; add cracked ice and top with 1 oz vermouth (preferably Carpano Antica).

## Food Sample (The Aperitivo Table)

**Sarde in Saor** (adapted from Polpo, London; serves 6–8) — floured, pan-fried small sardines layered in a container with sweated onions marinated in white wine vinegar, toasted pine nuts, and raisins; refrigerated 2 days (keeps up to 5) and served at room temperature with toasted bread.

**Caffè Florian's Tramezzini** (makes 8) — crustless white sandwich bread spread with mayonnaise, layered with sliced hard-boiled egg and thin-sliced *parma cotto* (cooked ham), cut into triangles and skewered in pairs.

**Aperitivo Essenziale** — a house snack spread of Oven-Roasted Olives (mixed olives roasted at 240°C/465°F with olive oil, finished with orange zest), Saffron Almonds (Marcona almonds toasted and tossed in saffron-infused oil), and Hand-Cut Potato Chips (mandolined russet potatoes fried at 180°C/350°F, salted).

**Grissini with Prosciutto and Pickled Radicchio** (adapted from Polpo, London; makes 20) — radicchio leaves quick-pickled in boiled white wine/vinegar/juniper berry brine, wrapped with prosciutto around Italian breadsticks.

<!-- ===== INÍCIO: the-art-and-science-of-foodpairing.md ===== -->

# The Art & Science of Foodpairing: 10,000 Flavour Matches That Will Transform the Way You Eat

**Authors:** Peter Coucquyt, Bernard Lahousse, Johan Langenbick
**Publisher:** Mitchell Beazley
**Source file:** `726429500-The-Art-Science-of-Foodpairing-...-Z-Library.pdf` (388 pages)

The authors are the founders of **Foodpairing** (foodpairing.com), the Belgium-based company/database launched in 2009 that pioneered "aroma-matching" as a professional culinary R&D tool, used by Michelin-starred chefs, bartenders, and food brands worldwide.

## 1. Structural Overview

Per the book's own Contents page:

1. **Introduction (pp. 7–36)**
   - *The Story of Foodpairing* (pp. 8–12) — origin narrative, told partly in first person by Bernard Lahousse, plus sidebar essays by **Heston Blumenthal** (The Fat Duck) and the François Benzi/Heston Blumenthal "caviar and white chocolate" discovery story.
   - *Key Aromas* (pp. 13–17) — volatile organic compounds (VOCs), gas chromatography–mass spectrometry (GC–MS), key odorants/odour thresholds, synthetic (gestalt) aroma perception, and interactions between aroma molecules (synergy, inhibition, suppression, overshadowing).
   - *Olfaction versus Gustation* (pp. 18–21) — orthonasal vs. retronasal smell, taste-bud biology, trigeminal sensation, supertasters.
   - *The Importance of Aroma* (pp. 22–24) — evolutionary/biological role of smell, flavour-flavour learning (Pavlov, evaluative conditioning).
   - *Aroma Molecules* (pp. 25–27) — precursors (carbohydrates, amino acids, fatty acids), Maillard reaction, caramelization, fermentation, and a taxonomy of aroma "building blocks": Aldehydes, Alcohols, Ketones, Esters, Lactones, Acids, Terpenes, Furans/furanoids, Furanones, Phenols.
   - *Building Your Aroma Library* (pp. 28–29) — how to train your palate/nose.
   - *How Foodpairing Works* (pp. 30–36) — the proprietary **14 Aroma Types / 70 Aroma Descriptors** system, how to read an **Aroma Wheel** and a **Pairing Grid**, and "From Pairing Ingredients to Delicious Dishes" (balancing texture and taste on top of aroma).
2. **Ingredients & Pairings (pp. 39–371)** — "From Kiwi to Oyster": ~90 ingredient chapters, each with an aroma wheel, ingredient essay, at least one professional chef's recipe/pairing sidebar, and multiple **pairing grids** (each listing ~10 ingredient pairings with dot-matrix charts across the 14 aroma types).
3. **Index of Ingredients (p. 372)**, Glossary of UK & US Terms, Endnotes, Acknowledgements, About Foodpairing (p. 387–388).

### Full ingredient list (in book order, with starting page)
Kiwi (40), Apple (44), Celeriac (50), Vanilla (54), Chocolate (58), Cauliflower (64), Strawberry (68), Basil (72), Watermelon (76), Tequila (80), Lemon & Lime (84), Chilli Pepper (90), Coriander (96), Fish (100), Red Bell Pepper (106), Garlic (110), Sweet Potato (116), Cognac (120), Shiitake Mushroom (124), Cinnamon (128), Coconut (132), Makrut Lime (136), Pilsner (138), Ginger (140), Lemongrass (144), Crustaceans (148), Sauvignon Blanc (154), Tomato (158), Blue Cheese (162), Butternut Squash (166), Olive Oil (168), Sourdough Rye Bread (174), Lambic Beer (178), Meat (180), Truffle (190), French Fries (194), Goats' Cheese (198), Blueberry (202), Apricot (206), Jasmine Blossom (208), Gin (212), Black Olive (216), Bergamot (218), Beetroot (220), Pomegranate (226), Cumin (228), Carrot (230), Orange (234), Rum (238), Pineapple (240), Doenjang (244), Cassava (248), Plantain (252), Cardamom (254), Peach (258), Yogurt (262), Seaweed (264), Cucumber (268), Black Peppercorns (270), Ibérico Ham (276), Parmigiano-Reggiano (280), Cabernet Sauvignon (284), Chorizo (286), Bourbon Whiskey (290), Durian (292), Coffee (294), Soy Sauce (298), Kimchi (302), Sesame Seeds (306), Mango (310), Balsamic Vinegar (314), Green Beans (318), Durum Pasta (322), Artichoke (326), Hazelnut (330), Brie (334), Raspberry (338), Banana (340), Almond (344), Pear (348), Avocado (352), Grapefruit (356), Tea (360), Elderflower Blossom (366), Oyster (368).

## 2. Core Methodological Framework

- **Founding hypothesis** (same lineage as Blumenthal/Benzi): *ingredients that share key aroma molecules at the right concentration pair well.* The company's own origin story is the **kiwître** — chef Sang-Hoon Degeimbre's dish pairing raw oyster with kiwi, discovered after Bernard Lahousse and bioengineer Jeroen Lammertyn ran GC–MS analysis and found kiwi shares green/grassy/fatty marine-like aldehydes with oysters.
- **Two independent discoveries are credited as the field's origin points**: François Benzi (Firmenich) noticing jasmine and liver both contain indole (1992), and Heston Blumenthal's caviar + white chocolate pairing (also explained post-hoc via shared aroma compounds by Benzi).
- **VOCs and GC–MS**: >10,000 volatile organic compounds identified in food to date; a GC–MS separates a food sample's compounds by molecular weight/retention time and quantifies each by peak area. Aroma compounds are often present at trace levels (10–15 mg/kg).
- **Key odorants**: only a fraction of the VOCs detectable by GC–MS actually exceed the human "odour recognition threshold" and thus contribute to perceived flavour (e.g., coffee has 1,000+ detectable VOCs but only ~30–40 are perceptible key odorants).
- **Aroma is synthetic, not additive**: mixtures of more than ~4 odorants stop being perceived as their individual components and instead create a new, emergent "gestalt" smell (e.g., no single molecule smells like "strawberry" — it's a synthesis of fruity esters + coconut-like lactones + caramellic furanones + green/cheesy notes). Foodpairing uses machine-learning models to predict this synthetic perception.
- **Molecule interactions**: synergy/additivity, inhibition, suppression, and overshadowing/hypoadditivity — four documented ways aroma molecules combine non-linearly in mixtures (illustrated with dose-response graphs).
- **Aroma building blocks** (chemical functional groups covered): Aldehydes (hexanal, nonanal, undecanal, vanillin, cinnamaldehyde, benzaldehyde), Alcohols (geosmin, 1-octen-3-ol), Ketones (beta-damascenone, beta-ionone, filbertone), Esters (ethyl butanoate, ethyl hexanoate, isopentyl acetate), Lactones (gamma/delta lactones, whisky lactone, jasmine lactone), Acids (acetic acid), Terpenes/terpenoids/sesquiterpenes (limonene, pinene, menthol, linalool, geranial/neral), Furans/furanoids (sotolon), Furanones (furaneol), Phenols (eugenol).
- **The 14 Aroma Types & 70 Descriptors system** (Foodpairing's proprietary taxonomy, used to build every wheel/grid in the book): Fruity (apple, banana, berry, coconut, fruity, grape, peach, pineapple, tropical) · Citrus (citrus, grapefruit, lemon, orange) · Floral (floral, geranium, honey, rose, violet) · Green (cucumber, fatty, grass, green, oat flakes, waxy) · Herbal (herbal, mint, thyme) · Vegetable (bell pepper, cabbage, celery, garlic, mushroom, onion, potato) · Caramel (caramel, maple) · Roasted (coffee, earthy, fried, malty, popcorn, roasted) · Nutty (hazelnut, nutty, tonka) · Woody (balsamic, phenolic, pine, smoky, woody) · Spicy (anise, camphor, cinnamon, clove, cumin, pungent, spicy, vanilla) · Cheesy (acidic, buttery, cheesy, creamy) · Animal (animal, fishy, meaty) · Chemical (burnt, dusty, petroleum, soapy, solvent).
- **Aroma Wheels**: a two-ring radial chart per ingredient — inner ring = the 14 aroma types present/absent (greyed out if absent); outer ring = concentration bands for each of the 70 descriptors, sized by distance from center and by band thickness/length.
- **Pairing Grids**: a table with the main ingredient in bold at top and up to 10 suggested pairing ingredients below, each row rendered as 14 coloured dots (one per aroma type) — dot size indicates a *shared, specific aroma molecule* between the two ingredients, not just "same category."
- **Classic vs. Potential vs. Chef's pairings**: like Book 1's Best/Surprising split, this book labels each featured pairing as a "Classic pairing" (culturally established), "Potential pairing" (data-suggested, novel), or attributes it directly to a named professional chef (e.g., Dan Barber of Blue Hill at Stone Barns for celeriac + Cheddar + miso).
- **From aroma-pairing to a finished dish**: a dedicated section (p. 35–36) on layering **texture contrast** (soft vs. crunchy/crispy — 60 texture types identified), **taste contrast** (sweet/salty/sour/bitter/umami, with a diagram of which tastes enhance, reduce, or balance one another), and **"aromatic complexity"** management — via "overtoning" (closely related aromas layered for subtlety) vs. deliberately contrasting aroma profiles, referencing psychologist Daniel Berlyne's "unity-in-variety" aesthetic principle, and using the Mexican dish *mole de pollo* as the paradigm case of successfully balanced complexity.

## 3. Sample Ingredient Pairing Data (organized)

### Kiwi (p. 40–43)
- **Signature dish:** *Kiwître* (Sang-Hoon Degeimbre, L'Air du Temps, Belgium) — raw oyster over diced kiwi, croutons, lime-coconut cream.
- **Classic pairing:** kiwi + melon (shared tropical ester ethyl 2-methylpropanoate).
- **Potential pairings:** kiwi + chayote squash (boiled); kiwi berry + quail; kiwi + apple (shared aldehyde hexanal, also found in olive oil).
- Kiwi berry pairing-grid sample (top rows): pan-fried quail, hazelnut, purple sage, baked king scallop, aji mirasol chilli, dry-aged beef, boiled pumpkin, black garlic purée, usukuchi (light soy sauce), basil.
- Grid ingredients for "melon": grilled green asparagus, granadilla, roast pork liver, turnip-rooted chervil, boiled brown crab meat, aji panca chilli, Gruyère, guava, Korean fish sauce, kiwi.

### Apple (p. 44–49)
- Traced to a single wild ancestor, *Malus sieversii* (Kazakhstan/Xinjiang), cross-bred with the tart European crab apple *Malus sylvestris*.
- Character-impact compounds shared across all apple varieties: hexyl acetate (generic apple), hexanal/trans-2-hexenal/butanal (green apple, fruity complexity).
- **Cultivar-specific aroma wheels compared:** Cox's Orange Pippin (dominant: beta-damascenone → rose/floral), Elstar (tart, citrusy, clove-spiced), Jonagold (coconut- and banana-scented, more tropical), Boskoop (fruity/tropical, geranium, anise, oat flakes).
- **Classic pairings:** apple + cheese (Jonagold + Brie, Braeburn + Parmesan); apple sauce + potato (Dutch *hete bliksem*/"heaven and earth" mash); apple + caramel; Braeburn apple + rolled oats (Bircher muesli); apple + celeriac rémoulade.
- **Potential pairings:** apple + hay; Elstar apple + cooked quinoa; apple sauce + prickly pear cactus.
- Grid sample for "cooked quinoa": boiled parsnip, European bay leaf, pickled sakura leaf, corn smut, tomato ketchup, miyabi melon, red bell pepper, mature Cheddar, sencha tea, white truffle.

### Celeriac (p. 50–53)
- Shares ~70% of its volatile compounds with celery stalks; raw celeriac is citrusy/woody/pine-like, cooked celeriac becomes sweeter and creamier with mint/honey notes.
- **Chef's pairing:** Dan Barber (Blue Hill at Stone Barns, NY) — whole-plant celeriac dish using root, leaves, and secondary roots; braised celeriac with Cheddar-miso foam, celeriac-leaf sauce, celeriac-skin tea.
- **Classic dish:** apple and celeriac rémoulade (French).
- **Potential pairings:** raw celeriac + brown shrimp; celeriac + lavender; celeriac + vanilla (shared vanillin/floral-rose notes; also barley malt).
- Grid sample for "boiled celeriac": orange, aji amarillo chilli, avocado, pan-fried guinea fowl, goats' cheese, roasted peanut, baked dab, Alphonso mango, cow's milk yogurt, roasted sweet potato.

### Vanilla (p. 54–57)
- *Vanilla planifolia* (Madagascar Bourbon vanilla, 75% of world production) vs. *Vanilla tahitensis* (Tahitian vanilla, more anise/floral, less classic "vanilla" than Bourbon).
- Vanillin makes up ~82% of Bourbon vanilla's aroma profile; also phenolic/balsamic, cheesy, fruity-coconut and peach lactones.
- **Classic pairings:** vanilla + cream (vanillin is fat-soluble, intensifies in high-fat matrices); vanilla + chocolate (Aztec *xocoatl*).
- **Potential pairings:** white asparagus + vanilla (beurre blanc); vanilla + shiro (white soy sauce).
- Grid sample for "Bourbon vanilla": oven-baked pork loin rib steak, roasted red bell pepper, roast lamb, cooked brown rice, turnip-rooted chervil, boiled brown shrimp, boiled potato, tomato, boiled brown crab meat, Camembert.

### Chocolate (p. 58–60)
- History: Olmec/Aztec *xocoatl*; three cacao varieties (Criollo, Forastero, Trinitario); fermentation → drying/roasting → conching stages, each generating new aroma compounds (Maillard products in roasting; caramel/spicy/cheesy compounds in conching).
- **Dark chocolate aroma wheel**: dominant types are Roasted, Nutty, Caramel, Cheesy/Acidic, Vanilla, Woody, plus a distinct Vegetal/green thread (bell pepper, cabbage, garlic, mushroom, onion, potato) enabling savoury-vegetable pairings.
- **Classic pairing:** milk chocolate + strawberry (shared molecule: strawberry furanone/4-hydroxy-2,5-dimethyl-3(2H)-furanone).
- **Potential pairing:** dark chocolate + broccoli (and other vegetables — beetroot, butternut squash, courgette, cucumber, parsnips, peas, potatoes, tomatoes).
- Grid sample for "dark chocolate": cassia bark (Chinese cinnamon), white button mushroom, rhubarb, lamb's lettuce (mâche), pickled sakura leaf, boiled broccoli, acacia honey, Tahitian vanilla, shiso leaf, peanut butter.
- Milk and white chocolate get their own comparative mini aroma-wheels and pairing grids (milk chocolate: sobrassada, Bourbon vanilla, fish miso, sesame halva, boiled squid, Mara des Bois strawberry, Hennessy VS cognac, boiled lobster, toasted sesame seeds, grilled green asparagus; white chocolate: lemon balm, elderberry, apple juice, pisco, açaí berry, smoked bacon, horseradish purée, peppermint, ground black pepper, red grapes).

## Note on relation to Book 3

`foodpairing-arte-e-ciencia-pt.md` documents the Portuguese-language edition of this exact book — same three authors (Peter Coucquyt, Bernard Lahousse, Johan Langenbick), same "10,000 flavour matches," same 14-aroma-type / 70-descriptor system, aroma wheels, and pairing grids, translated/localized for the Brazilian/Portuguese market. It is not an independent title but the official translated edition of *The Art & Science of Foodpairing*.
